## W: wild turnip–WZ

### wild turnip
  
noun. yellow weed, wild mustard  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD TURNIP (<i>Sinapsis arvensis</i>) [1967, no species given] is also commonly called charlock and yellow weed, and even wild mustard. It is exceedingly common on rich agricultural lands, at any rate in Canterbury and Otago, where masses of it often rival gorse in brilliancy. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,90,1967)*

### wild turnip
  
noun.  
See: also keha, native turnip  
> **BIDWILL** The natives had a large copper mowrie to cook the pork they had brought with them, and for the first time I saw them eat the common milk-[52]thistle. They cook it as constantly as the leaves of the wild turnip and cabbage, and seem to relish it just as much. It is so precisely like the English milk-thistle that I am almost inclined to think it must have come with the potatoes. *(1841,Rambles in NZ,51)*  
> Wild turnip is said to lie dormant in some soils for years and to come up when the land is ploughed. *(Feb 10, 1923,Hawera & Normanby Star,10)*  
> Another farmer whose crop he was to thresh next had asked him to clean out the machine thoroughly, as his crop was free from wild turnip and other weeds. *(Feb 1, 1935,Ellesmere Guardian,4)*  
> The stones having been made red-hot, the provisions, which consisted of fish ..., after being cleansed and bound up in the leaves of the kaha [a's-acute], or wild turnip, which almost covers every spare surface of vegetable soil in the country, ... were all placed in a basket on the hot stones, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/134 Not a spot on the earth lay waste; many parts teeming with the keha [e,a-acute] or wild turnip whose yellow flower on stalks of six feet in height, covered the distance, as far as the eye could discern, and emitted a pleasing fragrance. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/70)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** There are also several grasses, as well as <i>wild carrots, turnips,</i> and <i>cabbages,</i> whose origin, from their similarity to ours, may be attributed to the visits of some European shipping: and a <i>sowthistle</i> grows very luxuriantly wherever the forest has been recently felled, affording nourishing fodder for pigs, horses, and cattle. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,150)*  
> Arrived ..., hailed Redpath who had no boat to send us - consequently we bushed it and supped on wild turnips. *(Sep 28, 1850,Torlesse Papers,169,1958)*  
> **SHORTLAND** These fish [eels], `korau' or wild turnip tops, and fern root, were just now the natives' only food; ... *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,170)*  
> **EARP** The wild turnip and wild cabbage are in many parts [of Wairarapa] equally abundant, and equally beneficial to the stock-farmer. *(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),126)* [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 126]  
> **HUNT** ... we found ourselves in Evans' Bay; and here, to our great surprise, we discovered wild oats and turnips growing luxuriantly. By whom the seed was first sown God only knows; but as the Maori designates the turnip Captain Cook's cabbage, I presume that great circumnavigator had something to do with it. *(1866,Chatham Islands,9)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>horuhoru</i> n<i>. wild turnip. </i> *(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,31)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>horuhoru, wild turnip. </i> *(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,35)*  
> **HOCKEN** At the kaik they remained two days, supplementing their old fare of salt beef and biscuit with the delicious addition of fernroot and wild turnip tops. *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37)*  
> **LEVY** Wild turnip - <i>Brassica campestris </i> *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,Appendix II 357,1970)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD TURNIP ... (a) <i>Brassica campestris </i>(WILD TURNIP) with one faint line on the sides of the pods; ... *(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,98)* [NOTES: 1967 [nf. 1926] Hilgendorf and Calder Weeds 98]  
> <i>turnip, wild</i> [s] <i>Brassica campestris</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILD TURNIP (<i>Brassica campestris</i>) ... is common in many parts of New Zealand and a serious weed of arable crops in Canterbury and Southland. *(1980,Weeds of Crops,114)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Brassica rapa </i>L. <i>Common name: Wild turnip </i> Maori name: Pohata ... Forms of <i>Brasica</i> escape from gardens and persist for short periods in fields in both islands. *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,48)*  
> **WILSON** <i>Brassica rapa</i> subsp. <i>sylvestris </i>WILD TURNIP ... Erect herb up to a metre or more tall ... Native to Europe. *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,140)*  
> .. the few who were fortunate enough to get work could only earn about eight shillings per week, payment of which had, in some instances, to be taken in potatoes, or such stores as their employers could spare; while some depended solely for existence upon what fish they could catch, and a sort of wild turnip which luckily was to be found in abundance at that time. *(Feb 24, 1872,Otago Witness,19)*  
> The Agricultural Department wrote declaring broom, giant burdock, dock, gorse, wild turnip, and ragwort or ragweed to be noxious weeds within the borough, and notifying that the local inspector under the Noxious Weeds Act had been instructed in the matter. *(Dec 16, 1904,Bruce Herald,7)*  
> Among farm pests wild turnip, with its golden bloom, is an all too familiar feature, but as it only appears in land under cultivation it is not such a serious menace as the Californian thistle, which has now gained a strong and rapidly increasing hold in Otago and Southland. *(Jan 4, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*

### wildcat
  
noun.

### wilderness area
  
noun.  
> The Department of Conservation has been instructed to declare two wilderness areas this year... Wilderness areas are basically wild places which can only be accessed on foot and where there are no facilities for visitors. Huts, tracks, bridges and aerial access are not permitted. *(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/4)*

### wilding
  
adjective. growing wild  
> Wilding pines blown across such landscapes are a common problem already in the high country. *(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/26)*  
> Twenty years ago, few of us would have heard of the word 'wilding'. Today the unchecked spread of wilding exotic conifers can pose a real risk to the landscape, biodiversity and pastoral values... [17] A saying often used in wilding control is 'a stitch in time saves nine'. *(Mar 2003,Open Space,No. 60/17)*

### wildings
  
noun. a viral disease of commercial potato crops.  
> J of Ag January 21. 6 In New Zealand several types of virus are present..Mild-mosaic, rugose-mosaic, aucuba-mosaic, leaf-drop, stipple-streak, spindle-tuber, curly-dwarf, leaf-roll, streak and wildings are all present in New-Zealand-grown commercial lines. *(1929)*

### wildlife officer
  
noun.  
[NOTES: oficer in cit. sic? capping review??]  
> **FRANCIS** Without the past years of stock farming, horse riding ... I could not have coped with the varied ranging duties, which were the first step towards my becoming New Zealand's first Wildlife Oficer. capping review *(1983,Wildlife Ranger,6)*

### wilk
  
noun. whelk?  
> **POLACK** Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called para[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn)*

### willie
  
noun. wandering willie (<i>Tradescantia </i>sp)  
> The main concerns are wandering willie (tradescantia), and the winter oxalis, in full growth and flower (white) now. ... Modern chemicals ... are not easy to apply successfully when these weeds appear among wanted plants, and quickly smother them, in the case of `willie.' *(Aug 17, 1984,Evening Post,13)*

### Willie away
  
noun. a forward play in which the front-of-lineout forwards follow the throw-in, receive the tapped ball, and drive it out round the back into the opponents' midfield  
> **McLEAN** Willie away, they called it ... As the ball left the threequarter's arm, all aspects of the straightforward, the normal, the usual, departed. Galvanic energy possessed various players. The Number 4 began running along the back of the lineout. The halfback of the day kept pace with him. Other forwards, 2, 3 and 5, tailed after him. Number 6 or number 7 tensed and poised and made ready to spring into the air to touch, tap or grab and pass the ball down into the arms of the first runner. And within seconds, the whole pack was shambling in his wake into the the [sic] open field, ... This was `Willie Away', a favourite ploy of the 1963-64 All Black Rugby Union team from New Zealand while they were on tour of the British Isles, France and British Columbia between October 1963 and the end of February 1964. The name came from the captain, Wilson James Whinerary, who was the principal instrument in it ... . *(1964,Willie Away,7)*  
> **SLATTER** Their [lineout] code is any number to fifty goes to two, ...any name is Willie Away. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,125)*  
> ... the use of the Willie Away man to take the lineout delivery and break to the open. Since New Zealand introduced the Willie Away through the expertise at it of Wilson (Willie) Whineray other countries have adopted it as an effective primary phase attack. *(Mar 8, 1968,Dominion,16)*  
> **HOWITT** I didn't think I'd had an especially good game although I did do the Willie Away move a few times. <i> Ibid.</i> 291 In 1961 the `Willie Away' move was born. It was inspired by the French touring team who, although they lost all three tests, had Whineray's men sweating at Eden Park ... `The French kept peeling off the rear of the lineout and driving up the middle of the field. ... we thought if we could create a blindside by driving up midfield from a lineout ... we could extend the move where it kept dying out with France.' *(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,134)*  
> Some [N.Z.] words and senses get left out [of the Collins <i>Concise Dictionary</i>]. One might instance <i>pikelet, car conversion</i> and <i>Taranaki gate ; boatie</i> is in but not <i>postie</i> or <i>yachtie </i> (used recently in a 1YA newscast), <i>garryowen</i> but not <i>willieaway. </i> *(Aug 20, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101)*  
> **BRIDGES & DOWNS** New Zealanders have given hundreds of innovations to the sport of rugby.. but the one with its own name is a move called the 'Willie Away.' *(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,60)*  
> For a prop, he [sc. Wilson Whineray] was unusually gifted in the loose and perfected a peeling-off movement from the lineout that has since been named after him - the "Willie Away". *(Oct 2, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30)*  
> **ZAVOS** "The Willie-away" was a French invention... Fred Allen grabbed it for Auckland and New Zealand, using Whineray ("Willie" to the players) as his ball runner. *(1979,After the Final Whistle,183)*  
> McDowell showed up, leading the willy-aways from the line-out. *(Aug 6, 1992,Dominion,35)*  
> wind whip & willy away / catch a bus to island bay *(Sep 1, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63)*  
> **KNIGHT** Despite its refinement by New Zealand,the "Willie Away" was not an All Blackinnovation. It was something borrowedfrom the French side, whose lock JeanPierre Saux was used in a similar move. *(1991,They Led the All Blacks,52)*

### willies
  
noun. in NZ as elsewhere constr. THE WILLIES, esp. in the GET/GIVE THE WILLIES, the shakes (from terror, drink, fever, etc.) but esp. from fright or fearful anticipation  
[NOTES: In OED from 1896 and long known in NZ OEDS US 1900; anglicised ca1925; WF n. pl. since ca1895 so P8 [OMIT??]]  
> **DEVANNY** `A month in the bush would put that right.' Yes, and give me the willies and rheumatism.' *(1930,Bushman Burke,21)*  
> <i>willies, willy n. (slang)</i> a fit, brainstorm, especially in phrases, throw a willy, have the willies. *(1966,Contemp. Dict. Supplement (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>willies, willy ... n. (Sl.)</i> a fit, brainstorm, especially in <i>throw a willy, have the willies. </i> *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> **MANTELL** Gave me the willies just looking at it. So I thought maybe Angela was cracking hardy - y'know. *(1980,Murder or Three, A,34)*  
> Helen Clark's unkind diagnosis, "He's got the willies", is probably close to the mark. *(Nov 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> These include .. Penny Simmonds, who put the willies up the opposition a couple of years ago by introducing a zero-fees scheme to attract students to the far south. *(Aug 2002,Unlimited,38)*  
> "I do get the willies before a race but once it start the adrenaline takes over. ..." *(Feb 17, 2006,NZ Herald,Sport. p D15)*  
> Her speech put the willies up middle New Zealand, judging by the deluge of submissions to the Justice and Electoral select committee. *(Nov 7, 2000,Evening Post,4)*

### willing
  
adjective. rough, violent  
> Ted Sheehan reporting on crowd reaction at Fiji Airport The crowd surged back against the police. Things were getting very willing. *(May 19, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: Morning Report]

### williwaw
  
noun.  
[NOTES: My thoughts were that the word sounds Scottish (willy-wombles etc)  (or Scottish via Newfoundland whalers) but it doesn't turn up in any Scottish or Newfoundland Dictionary I have seen. Then whaling possibilities suggest New England but have found nothing so far. Another thought was Caribbean dictionaries (because of whaling there) but nothing turned up. One of your cits suggests Patagonian but no Patagonian dictionaries around. Meteorological dictionaries ignore it although they touch on willy-willy. Otherwise, it is just one of their unspecified "local winds". However, the attached dictionary entry seems to have a lot of sense, particularly the statement that it was common 18th Century oral nautical usage, to which can be added "preserved in some of these localities where these winds occur. dhurley		11/25/1999]  
> **ASHBY** Williwaw. A swirling gust of wind which comes off the land, especially from high cliffs. *(1975,Phantom Fleet,162)*  
> **ELVEY** Williwa Point. A limestone bluff justbelow Tirohanga, so-called because highwinds seem to reach their greatestvelocity at this point. *(1949,Kaikoura Coast,43)*  
> **REED** Williwa Point: Marlborough, PortUnderwood. It appears that the name wasconferred because of the violent windsthat blow across the bluff. *(1975,Place Names of NZ,480)*  
> Williwa Point. Headland on the southernend of Oyster Bay, on the westernmosthead of Point Underwood, on the easterncoast of Marlborough. Inside the point isthe site of a long vanished Maori villagecalled Tuingaro. *(1979,Wise's NZ Guide,7th ed. 514)*  
> **BALDWIN** Gales and willy-waws were always aproblem to mariners sailing D'UrvilleIsland, French Pass / Admiralty Baywaters. And they still are a problem. Lt.H.E. Rennick of the British Admiralty,reported in 1912 that the whole ofAdmiralty coast was subject to severewilly-waws except for Ngamuka Bay wherehe reported there was good shelter frommost winds... (Note: willy-waw - funnelof fine seaspray sucked up by a gale orsquall and blown willy-nilly). *(1981,New Zealand's French Pass & D'Urville Island,10)* [NOTES: Rennick was on Scott's Terra Nova andwas asked by NZ Marine Department tocarry out some survey work in NZ waters.AJHR H-15 1913 says he did a survey ofAdmiralty Bay/French Pass and his reportwent to Admiralty.]  
> **SLOCUM** [While I was wondering why no trees grewon the slope abreast of the anchorage.. awilliwaw came down with such terrificforce as to carry the Spray, with twoanchors down, like a feather out of thecove and away into the deep water [sc.in Cockburn Channel, Patagonia].] *(1900,Sailing Alone,104)*  
> **SLOCUM** By this time the wind had gone down andcat's-paws took the place of williwaws. *(1900,Sailing Alone,106)*  
> **MULGREW** During the night those frequent and heavysqualls called williwaws swept down fromthe mountains above, wailing likebanshees, making sleeping difficult forus all. *(1974,Gentleman's Magellan,83)*

### williwaw
  
noun. a sudden violent squall or whirlwind. [originally a whalers' or sailors' use] (esp. Marlborough Sounds) a whirling squall sweeping down from steep coastal hillsides across the water (the force varies from a small whirlwind lifting spray to a gut strong enough to overturn a boat) (cf WILLY 1840) -->Collins Concise U S 1. a sudden stray gust of cold wind blowing offshore from a mountainous Coast, as in the Strait of Magellan  
[NOTES: also williewaw and other spellings]  
> **EARLE** We ran down the coast; and ... the weather was beautiful, the sea smooth as a lake - indeed, a dead calm; when suddenly a squall came from the mountains, which very nearly laid us on our beam ends. All was confusion in an instant, the sea heaving up masses of foam in all directions, though not a cloud was visible in any part of the horizon. These sudden squalls are called<i> `Willies',</i> at least such is the name given to them by the sailors who frequent the island. [1966 fn] The word, with a similar meaning, is included in a modern vocabulary of Tristan da Cunha. Crawford, pp. 158, 268. *(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ,204,1966)*  
> **McNAB** heavy wind at SE and Whirlwinds [at Cloudy Bay] Cleared decks Setting up shooks [440] 8 May Latter [SE] heavy wind and Gusts real Whirlwinds taking the water up in eddies] and *(1913,Old Whaling Days,439)* [NOTES: [1836 May 4 Capt. Joy <i>Mary Mitchell</i> log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 439]  
> **ARNOLD** The skipper ... was a French Canadian. There were three men besides, one an old whaler, another a native of Nova Scotia, the third ... [from] the Azores ... [We] entered Queen Charlotte's Sound. By this time the wind had increased to a gale from the North West, and blowing over the low ridge which divides the Sound from Port Gore, it came to us in `williwaws', that is, violent puffs, succeeded by temporary calms. You can see the Williwaw coming from a long distance; the sea is blown up in a cloud of spray; curling and whirling it sweeps down upon you, and immediately before it comes, `Let go,' shouts the skipper, and the fore and main sheets are let go, blocks rattle, masts strain, canvas flaps violently, and the weight of wind forces the vessel nearly on her beam ends. Then in half a minute it is nearly a dead calm. *(Nov 22, 1848,Letters,157)* [NOTES: (Marlborough Sounds)]  
> The next thing we saw was the boat upside down. ... Fisher said it must have been a `Willey Waugh', whatever that might be. *(1897,Bracken's Second Annual,49)* [NOTES: [?locality]]  
> A special inconvenience [in Queen Charlotte Sound] introduced me to a new word - viz., the `willy waughs', [[fn] Thus was I instructed to spell.] brief gusts of wind that blew across the sound, carrying spindrift with them. Sometimes, crossing the mouth of a bay, we caught these gusts full and were well-nigh drenched. ... In Wellington I was assured that `willy waugh' was a Scotch word, also that it was a corruption from the Maori `wirriwa', which I later found did not exist. On my return to my dictionaries in Melbourne, I found the word in the Standard as Patagonian. `Willi-wa, a violent wind from mountains in the fiords of Patagonia'. An this be true (and I can find no more about it), by what process, through what book, did the word pass from Patagonia to Queen Charlotte Sound? The `willi-waught' of `Auld Lang Syne' means a draught of liquor, a long drink, not a sprinkling of wet externally. *(1901,TrNZI,33/504)* [NOTES: (Professor E.E. Morris) [corrected 28/1/90]]  
> **WALKER** ... `Willie-wa reach' and `Doldrum Point' [in the Marlborough Sounds]. *(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,108)*  
> **EADDY** With a north-easter and easterly gale the `willie waws' which come down over the high peaks are very sudden and severe. *(1939,Hull Down,58)* [NOTES: [?Auckland]]  
> **JOHNSON** During one southerly gale when the bay was white with willie-waws our tin whare was demolished. [Marlborough Sound] *(1970,Life's Vagaries,46)*  
> Boats sailing to east of Kapiti Island should beware of willie-waws coming down the valley. *(Jun 15, 1984,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: 2YA Wellington Local News 8.15am]  
> **A.B. CRAWFORD** A shower is a `light squall' and `willies' are eddies of spray above the surface of the sea caused by small whirlwinds. [268] <i>Willie</i>, whirlwind of spray over the sea. *(1941,I Went to Tristan,158)*  
> The willie-waw raises enough of a dust-screen. - *(1942,Time (NZ edition))* [NOTES: ALSO: 1943 Alaska radio What on earth is a willy waw? [wili:w ]]

### willow
  
noun. willow weed  
> **HILGENDORF** WILLOW WEED (<i>Epilobium</i> sp[p].). - This is also called herb willow, swamp willow, and by confusion redshank and fireweed. It is a native that occasionally grows as a weed in or near ditches. The name is sometimes given to redshank [p.59], and it is true that both polygonum and epilobium grow in damp places, have willow-like leaves and are about 2 feet high. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,1967)*

### willow sawfly
  
noun.  
> Scientists are taking a new tack in a bid to win funding for research on the threat to flood-control posed by the willow sawfly. *(Dec 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A17)*  
> Scientists have conceded that eradication of the willow sawfly pest is "unlikely"... The sawfly, which feeds on the leaves of willows, eventually killing the trees by stunting their growth, was first discovered in Auckland in 1997, apparently as an unwanted visitor from the United States or South Africa. *(Apr 4, 2001,Dominion,9)*  
> A small Japanese willow is being investigated as a possible defence against the willow sawfly, which is devastating willows in New Zealand. *(Nov 22, 2003,Dominion Post,A7)*

### willow weed
  
noun.  
> **HILGENDORF** WILLOW WEED (<i>Epilobium</i> sp[p].). - This is also called herb willow, swamp willow, and by confusion redshank and fireweed. It is a native that occasionally grows as a weed in or near ditches. The name is sometimes given to redshank [p.59], and it is true that both polygonum and epilobium grow in damp places, have willow-like leaves and are about 2 feet high. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,1967)*  
> **HI;LGENDORF** REDSHANK (<i>Polygonum persicaria</i>) is also called persicaria, spotted persicaria, knot weed, smart weed, willow weed, and lady's thumb. Perhaps also it is this weed that on the west coast of the South Island is called water pepper or Walker's weed. It is an annual weed of gardens, crops, and wet pastures. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967)*  
> <i>willow weed [=] pale willow weed: willow herb [s] ... willow weed [s] Polygonum persicaria</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,87)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILLOW WEED OR REDSHANK (<i>Polygonum persicaria</i>) ... This cosmopolitan weed is common throughout New Zealand, mainly in cultivated ground. *(1980,Weeds of Crops,118)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WILLOW WEED (<i>Epilobium</i> sp[p].). - This is also called herb willow, swamp willow, and by confusion redshank and fireweed. It is a native that occasionally grows as a weed in or near ditches. The name is sometimes given to redshank [p.59], and it is true that both polygonum and epilobium grow in damp places, have willow-like leaves and are about 2 feet high. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,1967)*

### willowherb
  
noun.

### willy
  
noun.  
> ... a strong north-west gale in squalls and willies. *(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/203)* [NOTES: (E.J. Wakefield)]

### willy willy
  
noun.  
[NOTES: Australian: not relevant to the origin of williwaw, according to DNZE 913]  
> **MORRIS** <i>Willy Willy</i>, native name for a storm on North-west of Australia. *(1898,Austral English,512)*  
>  *(Jan 20, 1984,Age, The,13/4)* [NOTES: [Letter by `Bengalee']:]  
> <i>willy-willy n. </i>a heavy gale on the north-west coast; a sandstorm, a land typhoon. [Abor.] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> <i>willy-willy n.</i> in N.W. Australia a cyclonic storm. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>willy willy ... n</i>. in N.W. Australia a cyclonic storm. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> <i>willy-willy n. NZ & Austral.</i> a cyclone or whirlwind. [Abor.] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,883)* [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1350]]

### wilsonite
  
noun.  
> The various rhyolites near Waihi are described ... as ... `a remarkable brecciated rhyolite' (wilsonite), and `an earthy compact rhyolite'. *(1911,TrNZI,43/260)*

### winch up
  
verb.  
> **MIDDLETON** He was `winching-up' a matai cask - the kind in which the pelts of new-born calves are pickled - and the hard red timber squeaked ... as the petals of the flower were drawn shut and the upper hoops were slipped on. He finished the job, took the cask out of the machine, and put on his coat. *(1959,Stone, The,90)* [NOTES: (Married Man)]

### winchman
  
noun.  
> Before preference to unionists was passed the pay [for coal workers in the Wellington Branch of the Waterside Workers' Federation] was per hour for shovellers 1s 6d and for top hands (i.e. winchmen, bullrope-men, trimmers, and tippers), is 3d. *(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/12/3)*

### wind
  
verb. (of a pig dog) to get the scent of, pick up a scent  
> **MITCALFE** `Nah, got on to a scent, winded one, right off?' `Down your place? don't believe it. 'nless you mean that bloody swanny you wearin'' <i>Ibid.</i> 12 `Look at ol' Gutty, winding.' `Yeah, winded that old swanny, more like.' `No way, she's on to something.' *(1983,Pighunter,11)*

### wind
  
verb.  
> [caption] Above: Rouseabouting can be an international job. Here Margaret Way (in red) and friend Robin are pictured atop a wool pile in an English woolshed. `No woolpresses here,' comments Margaret. `Robin and I wound each one of these fleeces by hand.' *(1988,More Magazine,29)*

### Wind Wand
  
noun. a wand-like sculpture in New Plymouth designed to move in the wind  
> If Mount Taranaki watches proprietorially over the land then Len Lye's Wind Wand keeps its eye on the city. *(Jul 2002,North & South,38)*

### wind/wind scent
  
verb. [pig dogs] to discern wild pigs by scent.  
> SWAN Grunter Hunters 26 He has learnt to bonnet find, ground trail and wind scent and he will often stick with his quarry for many hours. *(1992)*  
> ibid. 131 The dogs appeared keen and after winding briefly they took to the weatherbeaten scrub in a final effort to find the pig. *(1992)*  
> ibid. 158 She [the dog] winded off the bonnet as if there were pigs about and yet she did not want to jump off the front when Colin slowed the Land Rover. *(1992)*  
> BEACH Over 30 Years Pig Hunting 103 Sam moved to the edge of the track and looked and winded in the same direction as Bill was pointing the rifle. He stood there winding for a couple of minutes then down over the side he went, the other dogs following. *(1993)*

### wind-berry
  
noun.  
> **BEATTIE** On the tussocky sides of gullies the pleasant-tasting snowberries grew in profusion, while a berry known as the wind-berry grew on a little plant similar to the snowberries and both kinds were eaten and enjoyed. *(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1956)*

### wind-blow
  
noun.  
> **HOLMES** Also in smaller areas, artifacts such as clay pipe pieces can still be found in `wind blows'. *(1984,Chatham Islands,90)*

### wind-blow
  
noun. [ used elsewhere, but first recorded in NZ] a bare land area, of which soil and vegetation have been removed by wind erosion.  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 180 The sheep .. are returfing the naked windblows. *(1921)*  
> ibid. 190 On these bare naked wind-blows sheep lay, their numbers increasing as the run progressed. *(1921)*  
> MCCASKILL Molesworth 184 While there, the grader was used to clean up a wind-blow on the Tarndale flats sufficiently to permit it being called an airstrip. *(1969)*

### windbreak
  
noun. a row of trees or a fence or wall etc. serving to break the force of the wind. [AOD]; a line of trees, etc. giving shelter from the wind  
> **MARSHALL** I saw the stock dams I had swum in, shot ducks on: I passed the windbreaks. *(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,17)* [NOTES: (Takahe Man)]  
> **BAKER** She laughed, loud enough to startle the birds in a nearby poplar windbreak. *(2000,Kokopu Dreams,216)*

### winder
  
noun. wool winder  
> **RICKERTS** [Keri Hulme speaks] I became a winder in various woollen mills - a winder is a person who keeps tying knots so the yarn keeps going on to the spools so the weavers can use it. *(1986,Talking About Ourselves,22)*

### winder-up
  
noun. an end, finish  
> **HUNT** This affair was the winder-up of the puncheon of rum. *(1986,Twenty-Five Years in NZ,16)*

### windfall
  
noun. a tree blown over by the wind  
[NOTES: ca1954 also Forest Service for windblown timber cf Pinus horizontalis in Canterbury...]  
> At the first bluff he sidled up the spur but he go off the discs and had to bash through lawyers and windfalls. *(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56)*  
> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. *(1988,Hills, The,8)*

### windie
  
noun. a windsurfer  
> ... the launchies [were] now confronting `yachties' (of a miniature variety) ... the eventual result being the setting up of a windsurfing association. Not only that; the windies charged an entrance fee for the next race, ... *(Jan 1985,Metro,43/101)*

### winding engine
  
noun.  
> WINDING ENGINE: Used for hauling from quartz or coal mine, stationary, it wound endless ropes round large drum. *(1961,Friends in Chains,190)* [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 190]

### winding house
  
noun.  
> **RICHARDS** The only other means of communication with the surface [above the goldmine] was primitive. A long wire, known as the `knocker line', extended down the shaft and was accessible at each level. Each pull rang a bell in the winding house. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,5)*

### windjammer
  
noun. a homosexual male  
[NOTES: ca1945 Wellington students]

### windler
  
noun.  
> This cool, bright Sunday morning has drawn a good-sized group of "windlers", a term coined by Aucklander Mark Steele to describe fun sailing of the modern-yacht variety...We differentiate ourselves from competitive model-yacht sailors because windling is more about camaraderie and good fellowship... The America's Cup crew in San Diego were known to windle away lay days with mock matches in the marina. *(Nov 1996,North & South,19)*

### windling
  
noun.  
> This cool, bright Sunday morning has drawn a good-sized group of "windlers", a term coined by Aucklander Mark Steele to describe fun sailing of the modern-yacht variety... We differentiate ourselves from competitive model-yacht sailors because windling is more about camaraderie and good fellowship. *(Nov 1996,North & South,19)*

### window
  
noun. a gap in fur  
[NOTES: fur trade]  
> The possum has a well-founded reputation for ferocious if not vicious mating which leaves pelts with dark bruising, known in the trade as "ink spots" and gaps in fur, called "windows". *(Feb 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17)*

### window
  
noun. an area where a continuous miner is operating  
> In an area where a continuous miner was operating - called a "window" in North Island mining parlance but, curiously, a "garage" by South Island miners - Pastar pointed to an indentation in the wall the size of a breadboard. *(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/43)*

### window
  
noun. a patch of bare animal skin where wool or fur is missing.  
> PRACY & KEAN The Opossum in NZ 44 Inkspots (dark patches) or 'windows' (patches of missing fur), regardless of other conditions, grade skins down. *(1969)*

### window board
  
noun.  
> <i>Window board-</i> Horizontal board fixed internally at the foot of a window opening. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,131)*

### Windpipe of the Pacific
  
phrase. Cook Strait  
> **REEVES** The Strait, it may be mentioned, is still playfully termed, `the windpipe of the Pacific'. *(1898,Long White Cloud, The,77)*

### windsurfie
  
noun.  
> Locally there are now 1000 surfies and 1000 windsurfies, though nationally there are far more of the former. *(Dec 27, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27)*

### Windy
  
noun. a shortened form of Windy Wellington, Windy City, a nickname for Wellington -  
> [...windy Wellington] *(Dec 14, 1870,Evening Post,2)*  
> As `Windy's' bleak and wintry blast/ Tore off our tottering hood at last. *(Jul 7, 1942,Wall Newspaper 13th Field Ambulance)*  
> [Hdg] No Chance of Losing `Windy' Title *(Oct 31, 1972,Evening Post,1)*

### windy
  
noun. a windy buoy  
[NOTES: ca1940? and in use in 1949 cf OED poke 2. US Bag or bladder filled with air, used by fishermen as a buoy: 1887 fisheries US]

### windy buoy
  
noun.  
> **SORENSON** Methods [groper] fished: Motor trawl, longline and handlines; `Windy Buoys'. ... Cook Strait area is ... where the principal catches are made in the winter months on `Windy Buoy' set lines. *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,27)*  
> Set netting, drag netting, dan lines and windy buoys, set lines, long lines, and hand lines:  These methods of fishing are discussed under the heading of each port where they are employed. *(Mar 16, 1938,Evening Post,9)*  
> WANTED, 2 Moki Nets, 6 Windy Buoys. *(Feb 22, 1944,Evening Post,3)*

### Windy City
  
noun. Wellington  
[NOTES: windy city]  
> **O'BISO** We are now in Wellington, called the Windy City, and they are not kidding around. *(Nov 9, 1982,First Light,40,1987)*  
> Hello, Basil,' I said, `what are you/ doing back in the Windy City?'/ `Hello, Biddy.' Ignoring my question./ *(Sep 20, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38)* [NOTES: (Bridger `Domestic Incident')]  
> It is freely stated in the Windy City that Willy can read two proofs at one time in his sleep. *(Apr 12, 1913,NZ Observer,4)*  
> Talking of Wellington, I must congratulate that windy city on having about the tallest talker, or rather writer, I have met with for a long time. *(Sep 10, 1870,Otago Witness,13)*  
> Wellington sees the necessity of making a bold display before the Admiral, and so, if possible, biasing his judgment in favour of the windy city. *(Nov 29, 1887,Star, The,3)*  
> CONSTABLE SHEEHAN, who was very recently transferred to Auckland from Wellington, was married to a lady from the windy city on Tuesday last. *(Apr 19, 1890,Observer (Auckland),6)*  
> Vince Byrne, the popular Greymouth barister [sic] and solicitor, has beent [sic] spending the week in the Windy City on public and private business. *(Jul 14, 1914,Grey River Argus,5)*  
> After the June meeting he will sell out, pack up and resume his old "possy" in the windy city. *(Apr 10, 1930,NZ Truth,16)*

### Windy Wellington
  
noun.  
See: Windy  
> **HYDE** She said: `Blowy old place, isn't it? Windy Wellington. . . . [sic] But I like the wind. *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,29)*  
> Weeping Wellington! This phrase is for the nonce more appropriate than the usual descriptive one of 'windy Wellington.' *(Feb 8, 1890,NZ Observer,1)*  
> I spent a day or two in the Capital of the Colony, which might now well change the name of 'Windy Wellington' to the 'City of Noises!' *(Jun 8, 1907,NZ Free Lance,1/4)*  
> [Hdg] WINDY WELLINGTON *(Jun 18, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21)*  
> For the most of the two weeks when the spot was doing its stuff in Windy Wellington for the most part had fine weather with little wind. *(Mar 14, 1942,Evening Post,6)*  
> Notwithstanding so much is said about windy Wellington, yet the breeze of Wellington is preferable to the damp of Nelson. *(Sep 9, 1862,Colonist (Nelson),1)*  
> A Wild Week for William F. in Windy Wellington. *(Sep 4, 1920,Observer (Auckland),13)*  
> It well deserves the title of Windy Wellington . *(Dec 21, 1920,Otago Witness,65)*  
> They talk about Windy Wellington. *(Jan 15, 1910,NZ Truth,1)*

### windyful
  
adjective.  
> This month, they hope to inspire "windyful Wellington tales". *(May 24, 2001,Dominion,24)*

### wine biscuit
  
noun. a thin, sweet biscuit  
> **MANSFIELD** Grandma shook her head. `Nothing, thank you. We've got a few wine biscuits, and Fenella has a very nice banana.' <i>Ibid.</i> 527 But they had known they were going to marry each other ever since they walked in the Botanical Gardens with their nurses, and sat on the grass with a wine biscuit and a piece of barley-sugar each for their tea. *(1922,Stories,473,1984 Alpers)*  
> The good people at Griffins Foods Ltd inform me that wine biscuits take their name from the (possibly) 18th-century practice of eating these thin, sweet biscuits with wine. *(Jul 29, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48)*

### wine cask
  
noun. a cardboard container containing a plastic bladder of wine, with a serving tap attached  
See: also cask, chateau cardboard

noun.

### wine crawl
  
noun.  
> **McDERMOTT** Part of Auckland experience is to organize a `wine crawl'. *(1976,How to Get Lost & Found in NZ,40)*

### wine geek
  
noun.  
> As any weathered wine geek knows, the best strategy for touring a wine region is to come up with enough diversions to keep everyone else in the party who isn't obsessed with wine content enough to allow sufficient tasting time for those who are. *(Jan 9, 2002,Dominion,11)*

### wineberry
  
noun. also called makomako a small tree or shrub notable for its clusters of berries. <i>Aristotelea serrata</i> (formerly <i>A. racemosa</i>), =MAKOMAKO n2, =(NEW ZEALAND) CURRANT  
> <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wine berry *(1870,TrNZI,2/122)* [NOTES: Armstrong Botany Canterbury]  
> **KIRK** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wine-berry *(1889,Forest Flora)*  
> **FEATON** ARISTOTELIA RACEMOSA (Hook.,F.) ... The `MAKO-MAKO'.- ... In some districts the tree is known as the `Wine-berry', and wine has been prepared from its abundant and juicy fruit. <i>Ibid.</i> 177 Makomako [Maori name] .. Wine-berry [Settlers' name] .. Aristotelia racemosa .. Small tree *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,62)*  
> **VEITCH** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wine-berry *(1896,Traveller's Notes, A)*  
> **HUDSON** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wine-berry *(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wine-berry, n. Tutu</i>. In Australia, the name is given to <i>Polyosma cunninghamii</i>, Benn., <i>N.O. Saxifrageae. </i> *(1898,Austral English,513)*  
> **KIRK** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wineberry *(1899,Students' Flora)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wineberry *(1906,NZ Flora)*  
> (And) <i>A. racemosa</i> wineberry *(1907,AJHR)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** In bush clearings, [<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>] is one of the first plants to come up, and would, on this account, be termed by the Americans a `fireweed.' ... It goes by different names in different districts. In Otago, it is the New Zealand Currant, or Moko-mok'. In Canterbury, it is called the Wine-berry. In the North Island, it is the Mako-mako. *(1907,Plants of NZ,246,1940, p. 258)*  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wineberry *(1908,AJHR,C-14)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wineberry *(1908,AJHR)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park]

> **COCKAYNE**  *(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The)*  
> <i>makomako</i>, a tree (Aristotelia racemosa), wine-berry. <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> wineberry *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **LEVY** ... these gave protection to many secondary growths, including wineberry, lacebark, konini, mahoe, bracken fern, water fern, and later, blackberry. *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,13,1970)*  
> <i>wine-berry</i> See <i>tutu,</i> for which it is another name. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),445)*  
> **Mc;LINTOCK** Wineberry .. .. makomako .. .. <i>Aristotelia racemosa</i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **TURNER** Morris's reference to wineberry seems to suggest that it is a name for tutu, and he suggests further that <i>tutu</i> is especially a South Island word. This is not correct. My father was cutting wineberry on his North Island farm when Morris's dictionary was still quite new and he also knew tutu, but he has not heard the two associated. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,166)*  
> <i>wineberry [s] Aristotelia serrata</i> (Forst.) Oliver *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,87)*  
> **MOORE & IRWIN** <i>ARISTOTELIA SERRATA, makomako</i>. ... the settlers' name, wineberry, may have arisen otherwise since the flowers vary `in colour from a faint rosy flush to deep claret' (Laing and Blackwell 1907). *(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,68)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Aristotelia serrata</i> (J.R. & G. Forst.) Oliv. Common name: <i>Wineberry </i> Maori name: Makomako ... This handsome shrub is common on bush burns and the outskirts of forests. ...It was known as <i>A. racemosa</i> (A.Cunn.) Hook.f. but the Forsters' name has priority. *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,50)*  
> **BURTON** Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i> ) and the small, sweet berries (known as<i>konini</i>) of the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7)*  
> **McGILL** The township was now under tute, which Bill identified as a wild brambleberry with clusters of wineberries. [?what is meant here/ that tute is a bramble-like plant, and that its berry clusters are used to make wine; or that they a like WINEBERRY (Aristotelia) clusters??] *(1985,G'day Country,154)*  
> <i>wineberry n. NZ </i>= MAKOMAKO. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,664)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 548]]  
> 188 Makomako (wine berry or currant tree) slab. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,19)*  
> **CROWE** The small berries... were also said to have been used by the European settlers for making a jelly, and I know of some folk who have made wineberry jam. There has also been a suggestion that the fruit was used, as the name implies, in the colouring of wine. *(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,66)*  
> A quick-growing tree, the wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrate</i>) has panicles of rose flowers in srping followed by berries which attract a variety of birds. *(Nov 5, 1999,Dominion,12)*  
> On his side of the fence the property was clear with the exception of a small patch of wineberry (shrub) ... *(Sep 17, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,5)*  
> The fringes of the forests glowed with the red of the wine-berry. *(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15)*  
> Before Europeans arrived in New Zealand, you would have had to content yourself with berries of native hinau, fuchsia and wineberry. *(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42)*

### Winebox
  
adjective.  
See: Winebox noun  
[NOTES: also l.c. w]  
> The subsidising of previous cupchallenges appears to have been too closeto the wine box for comfort. *(Jan 28, 1996,NZ Tablet,24)*  
> Let them cling to their round table andsoggy wine-box connections and drift offon an ebb tide to the oblivion that theyso richly deserve. *(Apr 18, 1996,Evening Post,4)*  
> "All the tribes are in the back pocket of some winebox type or other," said one. *(Feb 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17)*  
> Act leader Richard Prebble is confident of success on July 27. In anticipation of a victory party on election night, he was spotted by reporters buying a dozen chardonnay. "It's of winebox proportions," he said. *(Jul 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> Before the BNZ debacle, Winston Peters orwine box revelations, considerable carehad gone into nurturing the corporate..identity. *(Feb 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,A16)*  
> From filing a GST return to constructing a winebox merry-go-roud or an Enron-sized pyramid scheme, there's no denying the utility of people who are good with numbers. *(Jun 1, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7)*  
> [The firm] was involved with Wineboxdeals. *(Oct 14, 1995,Dominion,21)*  
> Mr Scott easily earned himself the titleof the most maddening witness of theWinebox commission of enquiry. *(Oct 14, 1995,Dominion,21)*  
> [Hdg] Dissension is fermenting nicelyin the winebox inquiry. *(Jul 25, 1994,Dominion,2)*  
> It is unlikely Cook Islanders will see last Sunday's TVNZ Frontline programme which outline two of the wine boxdeals. *(Jun 3, 1994,Independent (Auckland),3)*  
> Around 600 copies of the Winebox documents detailing Cook Islands tax deals have been sold throughout New Zealand since the papers were madeavailable to the public last month... The Department incorporated the winebox papers into reviews already under way. *(Jul 5, 1994,Dominion,7)*  
> On 16 March 1994 the honourable memberfor Tauranga tabled in this House acollection of papers that have becomeknown as the wine-box papers. *(Aug 25, 1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,542/3095)*  
> I consider the select committee to be ahighly inappropriate forum for inquiringinto matters arising out of the wine-boxpapers. *(Aug 25, 1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,542/3096)*  
> [Hdg] Finance and ExpenditureCommittee- Wine-box PapersInvestigation. *(Sep 6, 1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,542/3410)*  
> [Hdg] Winebox inquiry committee faceslimits. *(Jul 5, 1994,Dominion,7)*  
> With the Winebox inquiry startingtomorrow, Winston Peters should bepreparing to take his place in the sun.He's gone from the winebox to the dogboxin the space of a week. *(Nov 6, 1994,Sunday Star Times,C8)*  
> This will primarily be to suit theprofits of the shareholders of the bigfood chains - people who are in thewinebox industries, I guess. *(Nov 23, 1995,Evening Post,4)*  
> It has been that crazy with the first"wine-box" book to hit the market. *(Oct 13, 1995,Independent (Auckland),8)*  
> [Hdg] Random thoughts on the Wineboxyear. *(Jan 4, 1996,Evening Post,6)*

### Winebox
  
noun.  
[NOTES: The term arose when about Sep/Oct 1992 Mr Stephen Lunn of Auckland put some documents into a cardboard winebox and then released them to the public. The term winebox inquiry was first used of the Parliamentary Select Subcommittee on Finance & Expenditure when it began looking into the matter and was later transferred to the Davidson High Court (Cook Islands) Commission. When faced with a court writ on publication, the Independent ran a fable on a mythical country called [South] Wogistan in which the basic details were revealed. The term Wogistan is said by the Independent in last week's issue in a column by Chalkie (? Berryman) to have gained some currency in circles interested in the Winebox affair and may be worth picking up - I have the references. It's very PC. The first published use of the term wine box of documents may have been made by Paul East in Parliament [NZPD, p. 539]]  
> It seems that somewhere out there - in a winebox, perhaps - the incontrovertible proof of Timberland's wisdom is gathering dust, withheld from public view by an oppressive government. *(Apr 24, 2000,Dominion,12)*  
> Peters might pride himself on being able to put two and two together and mnake a Winebox, but some things about his campaign don't add up. *(Jul 20, 2002,Weekend Herald,A23)*  
> That's a whole winebox of a question. *(Oct 14, 1995,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: Chef speaking to Brian Edwards]  
> [Hdg] Jack in the Winebox *(Oct 14, 1995,Dominion,18)*  
> Mr Scott had been making disparagingremarks about the department's role inthe Winebox. *(Oct 14, 1995,Dominion,21)*  
> Inland Revenue is not permittingemployees to talk to the media about theWinebox. *(Oct 14, 1995,Dominion,21)*  
> [Hdg] Tax dodgers 'wine box' goes toParliament. *(Mar 18, 1994,Independent (Auckland),1)*  
> I am also advised that this wine box ofdocuments was made available to theSerious Fraud Office some 18 months ago. *(Mar 22, 1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,539/587)*  
> Nobody can be absolutely certain thatthey are the same documents that wereleft in a wine-box at the offices of theSerious Fraud Office a year or 18 monthsago. *(Mar 22, 1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,539/623)*  
> Mr Sturt said the office had examinedboth the "wine box" of documents anddocuments received from Mr Peters onFebruary 24. *(Mar 24, 1994,Dominion,2)*  
> Both men.. told the House that thedocuments were part of the so-called"wine box" of papers. *(Mar 31, 1994,Independent (Auckland),1)*  
> The Winebox records transactions or proposed schemes including Brierley Investments and its former associate IEC, the BNZ, Fay Richwhite...[He] said the company had nothing to hide, with documents in the Winebox relating to the "normal course of business."...None of that sum relates to the winebox... Millions of dollars is still at stake in investigations relating to the CookIslands wine-box. *(Jul 5, 1994,Dominion,7)*  
> Winston Peters didn't find the famouswine box by accident. *(Sep 1994,Metro,74)*  
> In September 1992.. a brown paper bagfilled with documents was delivered tothe office [of the Independent]... "Wedidn't know he had them." she says of thedocuments which would come to be known asthe contents of the celebrated wine-box. <i> Ibid.</i> 77 Anyhow, just how many wine boxes werethere? Berryman and McManus certainly never had anything arrive in a wine box. <i>Ibid.</i>80 [In 1992] Lunn says that he was then sent copies of the documents in four separate envelopes from overseas. He photographed them, placed one set in a cardboard wine box, and, one Saturday, left them on the dining room table of an apartment DavidLloyd then owned in Westminster Court. *(Sep 1994,Metro,76)*  
> [Hdg] Yet another winebox. *(Oct 28, 1994,In Touch (Wellington),1)*  
> [Hdg] SFO kept the Winebox open. *(Nov 8, 1994,Independent (Auckland),1)*  
> Stephen Lunn stashes [a set of documents]in a wine-box at his home. *(Nov 18, 1994,Independent (Auckland),39)*  
> The celebrated Winebox of documents hasbecome accustomed to life in thespotlight since rumours about itsexistence and contents first circulatedin Auckland in the late 1980s. *(Feb 10, 1995,Dominion,7)*  
> Or is it another example of a wineboxbeing needed to ensure an agency of thestate is diligent in its duties? *(Jan 2, 1996,Dominion,6)*

noun. winter flying/flights from Christchurch to McMurdo Station in Antarctica
