> On the subject of Winfly ... the Winfly mail closes in Christchurch on 28 August. *(Jun 1969,NZ Antarctic Record,2)*

### wing
  
noun. [historical] pence as in eighteen wing  
[NOTES: 1950 =18 pence : barber 15-20 years ago [wing =penny English slang I.A.G.]]

### wing
  
noun. (of a wing dam)  
> **BAINES** I first cut a trench in the rock right across the bed of the creek, into which I placed a squared spar. I did the same up the good or rock side of the stream. On the other bank I made a `wing' by driving piles [for a flood-dam]. *(1874,Edward Crewe,174)*  
> **REED** After the bottom had been secured, a start was made on the wings, as the walls on either side of the gate were called. *(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,206)*

### wing
  
noun. (of a stockyard or wing fence)  
> **STRONACH** The killing pen is difficult there. The wing was broken down, the pen was near the sheep yards, and the manager and all the rest of the musterers were drafting sheep. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45)*

### wing
  
noun. an arm, esp. if lost or injured  
See: cf. wingie

### wing
  
verb. be winged: to lose a limb  
See: cf. wingie  
> I meant to do some reminiscences of Wingies I have Seen Winged. *(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,23)*

### wing
  
noun. part of a temporary docking yard, made from scrim or wire netting attached loosely to temporary posts, which wrap around ewes and lambs to force them into a yard.  
See: wing fence, scrim  
> MOWAT Diary Oct 23 I put up a netting wing above bank on top side ... *(1908)*  
> Livestock in NZ 46 The pen may be made pear-shaped against a fence with the wing to catch the sheep extending from the open end (wide end.) *(1919)*  
> NZ Farmer Oct 1. 1349 A wing should also be erected, starting from the entrance of the holding yard, and widening as it extends into the paddock to form a lead into the yard. *(1926)*  
> STRONACH Musterer on Molesworth 45 They have to be run into a small pen, sometimes with one 'wing' fence, sometimes with two, and quite often with none .. the killing pen is difficult there. The wing has broken down. *(1953)*  
> STEVENS Sheep I 58 .. another [coil of netting] is used for a generous wing (to prevent breaking as the sheep are forced into the yard) *(1958)*

### wing
  
noun. [mustering] the side or flank of a large mob of sheep.  
See: -  
> STUDHOLME Te Waimate 123 In driving a big mob of scary sheep across country it was usual to flank it with a man on each wing.. *(1940)*  
> NEWTON High Country Days 33 At last the lead of the mob shot out onto the flat. A man appeared on either wing and swung them towards the yard. *(1949)*  
> ibid. 67 Once on the flat, the two men on the wings rode slightly forward of the mob. *(1949)*  
> SINCLAIR Boot in the Stirrup 54 We drew lots to see who was to drive the mob into the station. Three were needed, one for each wing and one for the tail. *(1973)*  
> RICHARDS A Pioneer's Life 26 On the drove home the huntaway would always remain at the rear of the flock while the header would be watching the wing and holding the lead so it would not swing out too far. *(1989)*

### wing
  
noun. the outer tooth on the comb of a shearer's handpiece.  
See: -  
> BOWEN Wool Away! 86 .. a wing really puts a small brake on the shearer's hand  .. it is a good practice to have the outside wing bent out as it is not so much in the wool, and does collect the odd 'whisker' that would otherwise be missed. Don't overbend it into an ugly wing - it needs only a slight bend. *(1955)*

### wing
  
noun. a channel or barrier that encourages sheep to enter a pen.  
See: -  
> HALL A Shepherd's Year 59 You're not allowed to let the gate go. The dog must bring the sheep to the pen and push them in with your help as a wing on the gate. *(1987)*

### wing
  
verb. [mustering] to control the sides or flanks of a mob of stock, from a wing position.  
> NEWTON High Country Days 67 ..with a dog winging the mob on either side. *(1949)*  
> MCLEOD Many a Glorious Morning 155 All Jack and I had to do was to wing the sides and occasionally check the lead to let the weaker ones keep up. *(1970)*  
> HALL A Shepherd's Year 60 I'd been winging sheep up a face and meant to bring them down, but they rounded a ridge before my huntaway got a head on them ... *(1987)*  
> KERR High Times in the High Country 163 We guided the mob up through the pegs, heading dogs winging them, an old dog in the lead. *(2000)*

### wing
  
verb. [dog trials] to form a wing by holding on to a gate and waving a hill pole.  
> HALL A Shepherd's Year 59 At the pen you open the gate and branch out from its end to wing the sheep in. *(1987)*

### wing dam
  
noun.  
See: also wing  
> Many ... who held river claims worked very successfully by wing-dams, consisting of bags of sand laid into the strream, so as to cut off a portion of its bed, which, being drained by pumping, was paddocked out and passed through the cradle. *(1863,AJHR,D6/18)* [NOTES: (Pyke Goldfields Reports)]  
> **PYKE** The Dunstan gold workings were of two kinds - technically known as `beach claims' and `bank claims' ... The former were on the sandy margins of the river, and were often extended by means of `wing-dams' and other ingenious devices far into the rushing waters. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,18)*  
> **BATHGATE** They built a wing-dam, ... them stakes is the remains of it - and they did get splendid gold. *(1874,Colonial Experiences,133)*  
> **VEITCH** Many favoured a `wing dam' when working in very shallow beaches. Bags filled with earth formed a 12 feet by 20 feet square. The water enclosed in this square was then baled or pumped out and the area worked dry. *(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,30)*  
> .. although "wing dams" may do to fossick about the edges of the river, it will require an expenditure of labour and capital to cut a race and bye wash, and erect an embankment to turn and work it systematically. *(Oct 8, 1862,Otago Daily Times,5)*  
> The wing dam referred to was weak, the ground being dredged away from behind, and had caved in, showing that it was made of nothing but loose sand and brushwood and not high enough to prevent a freshet from going over. *(Jul 18, 1893,Southland Times,3)*  
> The Grey River was the first to be pegged off, then the low-lying terraces and creeks that, although known to contain payable gold, could not be worked on account of there not being sufficient fall for drainage - the creeks having been tested in places by putting in wing dams, others by long tailraces. *(Jun 2, 1900,Otago Daily Times,9)*  
> They are 12 feet down below the level of the stream, and a wing dam was necessary to keep the mud out of the concrete. *(Nov 28, 1928,Evening Post,12)*  
> As the company's claim manager has the wing dam for commencing paddocking well in hand, no delay is anticipated in getting out the first paddock at the mouth of Moke Creek. *(May 14, 1934,Evening Post,14)*  
> Let a temporary wing dam, a littlehigher than the depth of the water, beplaced at the mouth, and projecting intothe stream so as to drive the water downin sufficient volume to produce.. a floodin the race and cause a scour from oneend to the other. *(May 1, 1899,NZ Times,2)*  
> **PRESHAW** Groins and wing dams were constructed at considerable expense. *(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,176)*  
> **STAFFORD** The wing dam was completed during the 1907-08 year. *(1988,New Century in Rotorua, The,31)*

### wing dog
  
noun. [droving and mustering] a dog that controls the wing or flank of a mob of sheep.  
> RICHARDS A Pioneer's Life 26 Once the mob is on the move the header and wing dog will control the lead and flank while the huntaway will watch the rear, often without a single command. 85 The 'wing dog' and huntaway also play an important and intelligent role on any drive. The wing dog is ever on the alert and watching for any sheep that may try to stray from the mob by following a track or climbing a bank. *(1989)*

### wing fence
  
noun. an angled fence or fences that channel stock into an entrance to yards, pens or a race.  
See: wing  
> **STRONACH** They [sheep for killing] have to be run into a small pen, sometimes with one `wing' fence, sometimes with two, and quite often with none. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45)*  
> **STRONACH** They [sheep for killing] have to be run into a small pen, sometimes with one `wing' fence, sometimes with two, and quite often with none. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45)*  
> STRONACH Musterer on Molesworth 45 They have to be run into a small pen, sometimes with one wing fence, sometimes with two and quite often with none .. The killing pen is difficult there. The wing has broken down. *(1953)*  
> MCCASKILL Hold This Land 83 Burning was abolished, wing fences were erected high up the slopes to enable the block to be spelled from stock. *(1973)*  
> NZ Historic Places 84 Feb 33 A short 'wing fence' off the northeast corner would have helped the musterers to funnel the sheep into the yards. *(2002)*  
> Each jump has a wing fence leading to it. *(Nov 11, 1887,Star, The,3)*  
> Wing fences of netting would be run to the bridge at the Hawke's Bay approach, making that side rabbit-proof. *(Jul 26, 1945,Evening Post,6)*  
> One door of the slaghterhouse [sic] was missing, and part of the wing fence had been taken away. *(Sep 27, 1893,North Otago Times,3)*  
> This dealt with the birch stringers and caps advertised for, with the hiring of one of the PWD cranes from Otira, recommended new planking at back of transit shed, connecting latrines with drainage system and new gates and wing fence at cattle yards, all of which were dealt with and adopted or authorised by the board. *(Oct 7, 1903,Grey River Argus,3,3)*  
> The lower portion of West street culvert has been cleaned out and wing fences have been erected on either side of a culvert in South street. *(Jul 11, 1919,Feilding Star,3)*  
> Excavating of Stock Yards and erection of wing fences. *(May 12, 1925,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3)*  
> Access from Jackson Street to Shandon Golf Club Lease. - The filling has been completed and the wing fence set back affording considerable improvement. *(May 10, 1933,Hutt News,4)*

### wing fence
  
noun.

### wing heading
  
noun. [of sheepdogs] working on the sides of a mob of sheep while they are moving.  
> MILLS ET AL Practical Guide to Handling Dogs & Stock 42 This kind of off-balance work is often brought into use on a farm, especially when driving a mob through open country. It is closely akin to what is known as 'wing heading.' *(1964)*

### wing leader
  
noun. a dog that sits on the wing or flank of a mob, keeping boundary as the mob moves.  
> MCGREGOR A Dog's Life 23 There are two types of leading dogs. One is the dog that is capable of getting the sheep to follow while he leads in the front of the mob, and the other is known as the wing leader. The latter will take the lead when necessary but will also run back to keep the wing of the mob on either side from drifting. *(1980)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary II 3 wing leader: a dog, really only needed much in the early days of no fences, which travelled not at the head but at the flank of a driven mob tucking back any animals liable to stray aside. A good leader nowadays is often intelligent enough to watch for side roads, broken fences and open gates, and to scamper back without being bidden to reclaim anything that does stray aside. *(1981)*

### wing man
  
noun. [mustering or droving] a person whose position is on the side of a mob as it moves.  
> SHARPE Country Occasions 123 Fanning out into a rough line, with one wing man on the crest of the range and the other down near the valley floor ... *(1962)*

### wing moss
  
noun.  
See: .  
> **MARTIN** Equally common on moist forest soils or on decaying logs are two species of WING MOSS (<i>Pterygophyllum</i>) with ... scale-like leaves overlapping to form flattened branches ... *(1961,Flora NZ,69,1929)*

### wing three
  
noun. [abbreviation; informal; Sport] a wing three-quarter  
> **SLATTER** Gone are the days of the wing threes streaking for the corner flag. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,80)*

### wing(ed) thistle
  
noun.  
See: -  
> A noxious weed, new to Auckland province, wing thistle, had made its appearance inthe Waikato. *(Dec 4, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/180 : 7)*  
> Winged Thistle (<i>Carduus pycnocephalus</i> ) . .. Leaves with deeply-cut spiny margins. which are developed downwards, so as to form spiny wings down the angles of the stem, hence the name - winged thistle. *(1894,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,II/69)*  
> Winged Thistle (<i>Carduus pycnocephalus </i>) . *(1895,Leaflets for Farmers,No. 18/7)*  
> [The] statement that the winged thistlewas valuable to farmers for use as fodderand for improving pasture land, wasrefuted by.. the Agricultural andPastoral Association. *(Apr 12, 1916,Hawke's Bay Herald,5)*

### winge
  
verb.  
See: whinge

### winged thistle
  
noun. a thistle of the genus Carduus; C tenuiflorus or C pycnocephalus that have winged stems [often used as a stock food.]  
> J of Ag September 15. 327 Winged thistle is rapidly spreading throughout the higher lands and is proving of immense value in such country as Central Otago, where large areas were practically denuded of all vegetation until the advent of this most useful fooder plant. *(1913)*  
> J of Ag May 20. 313 The introduction of winged thistle (Carduus pycnocephalus) is to be recommended, as it affords both feed for stock and shelter for other plants that may chance to establish. The sowing of cocksfoot, yarrow, wild white clover, and Poa pratensis is to be recommended after the winged thistle has established itself. *(1921)*  
> NZ Farmer Aug 1. 1001 It [the paddock] was very bad with horehound, wing thistle, and ink weed. *(1927)*  
> NZ Farmer Feb 1. 16 Winged thistle is an annual averaging 2ft to 3 ft in height and has a fairly small root system. *(1951)*  
> NZ Farmer Sept 24 45 Fodderkleen will give specific control in all field brassica crops...as well as difficult-to-kill weeds such as nodding, winged and Scotch thistles. *(1970)*  
> LEVY Grasslands of NZ 300 Chemical weedkillers .. are as a useful adjunct in the control of camp weeds, and it is very obvious that, without the control of smother weeds such as wing thistle ... [wing thistle] *(1970)*  
> Early and late, morn and noon, he must be prepared to labour, and behold! like thieves in the night, docks will shoot up sly green blades; spring's balmy breezes will waft the winged thistle awns; like treacherous white snakes the correl roots will creep and twine below the surface ... *(Dec 30, 1882,Otago Witness,4)*  
> From the Agricultural Department, suggesting that winged thistle be included in the schedule of Noxious Weeds Act. - Received. *(Nov 1, 1905,Tuapeka Times,3)*  
> The Winged thistle and the milk thistle (which also is known by the names of Variegated, Blessed, Holy, Maria's and Our Lady's)[.] *(Jan 3, 1895,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*

### winger
  
noun. a stockman or musterer who controls the wing or flank of a mob of sheep or cattle.  
See: wing man  
> STUDHOLME Te Waimate 123 There were considerable beds of tutu in many places and the 'winger's' job included steering the mob clear of these. *(1940)*

### winger
  
noun. a wing forward  
> **BARR** At this stage it might not be out of place to mention that wing-forwards were played for the first time in 1876 [the year Canterbury sent a touring team as far as Auckland]. Thus early the `winger', who was to cause a great deal of controversy in later years, was much in evidence. *(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,165)*  
> **FANNING** No treatise on forwards can be complete without a reference to `wingers'. ... the scrum's Satanic wings ... [Long ago elderly gentlemen] went to see the old-fashioned `wingers'. Their joy was like the thrill of the alleged Spaniard, who watched a game in the heyday of `wingerism' : `Caramba! eet ees splendida! Eet ees to me your bonsa or bosca.' *(1910,Players and Slayers,13)*

### winger
  
noun. -  
> **STUDHOLME** In driving a big mob of scary sheep across country it was usual to flank it with a man on each wing who would guide the leaders in the right direction, the rest following. ... There were considerable beds of tutu in many places and the `winger's' job included steering the mob clear of these if possible, as hungry sheep are easily affected by this poison, specially in moist weather. *(1940,Te Waimate,123,1954)*

### wingerism
  
noun.  
> **FANNING** No treatise on forwards can be complete without a reference to `wingers'. ... the scrum's Satanic wings ... [Long ago elderly gentlemen] went to see the old-fashioned `wingers'. Their joy was like the thrill of the alleged Spaniard, who watched a game in the heyday of `wingerism' : `Caramba! eet ees splendida! Eet ees to me your bonsa or bosca.' *(1910,Players and Slayers,13)*

### wingfish
  
noun. <i>Pteraclis velifer</i>  
> **GRAHAM** Wingfish <i>Pteraclis velifer</i> (Pallas) *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407)* [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]  
> **PAUL** <i>Wingfish Pteraclis velifera</i> A spectacularly different and unmistakable fish ... anal fins very large and sail-like ... *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,94)*  
> **PAUL et al.** [index to common names 283-293] wingfish 1407-8 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### wing-forward
  
adjective.  
> The first score was got by Johnson, who was playing wing-forward, backed up by Barnicoat dribbling the ball over the Poneke line, and thus secured a try for Wellington. *(Aug 13, 1883,Evening Post,2)*  
> E. Whiteman will take Morse's position as wing-forward. *(Jul 24, 1896,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> The following team has been chosen to represent Wellington against Wairarapa on Saturday next:- Full-back, Sidells .. wing-forward, Fitzgerald ... *(Jun 21, 1920,Evening Post,4)*  
> A wing-forward and a 2-3-2 scrum will be played. *(Jun 1, 1939,Evening Post,6)*  
> McCourt, who was never in the scrum, but gave a great exhibition of old-style New Zealand wing-forward play, was a continual menace, his covering in both attack and defence being a big factor in Ulster's fine showing. *(Nov 15, 1945,Evening Post,8)*  
> Wing-forward play as we know it now inWellington.. has developed purely andsimply into a bustling match between themen on both sides of the scrummage. *(Mar 12, 1904,Free Lance,20)*

### wingie
  
noun. a person (esp. a serviceman) who has lost an arm  
> THE INCONVENIENT `WINGY' [title] ... a lady sitting comfortably in a tram-car, saw a one-winged soldier hanging to a strap, and advised him thus : `You men should wait for a car that has seating accommodation.' *(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,23)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>Wingy.</i> A man `minus' a <i>wing </i>(arm): New Zealanders': in G.W. Cf. <i>limby</i>, q.v. *(1937,Dictionary of Slang,961)*  
> **HENDERSON** Van, George and Tom were all `wingies', suffering from smashed arms. Ray and I were `limbies', both having lost the left leg ... *(1945,Gunner Inglorious,75)*  
> Six cheerful `wingies' (arm amputees) delegates at the present Dominion conference of the N.Z. War Amputees' Association. *(May 16, 1957,Evening Post)*  
> **HILLIARD** Dad cut his meat for him because his arm was in a sling ... Dad took him into the kitchen to meet ... the cook. `A wingie, I see,' Alf said. `Not that I know much about it - I was too old for the Boer War.' *(1965,Power of Joy,123)*

### winging-forward
  
noun.  
> [HWO's cit] The practice of playing winging-forwards is almost universal. *(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/237)*

### wingy
  
noun.  
See: wingie

### wink
  
verb. eye-clip  
See: also wig  
> <i>Wink, wig.</i>- Other names for<i> eyeclip</i> (q.v.). (C.) *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### wink
  
verb. to eye-clip a sheep.  
> Press (Acland Gloss) Oct 14. 15 Some people speak of eye-clipping as Winking. *(1933)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 91 wig or wink: to eye-wink or eye-clip a sheep so that it does not become wool-blind. *(1981)*

### winner on the day
  
phrase.  
> It promises to be glamorous, edgy and a bit wacky. No doubt sport will be the winner on the day. *(Aug 31, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),37)*  
> In the beginning was nothing except a furious little spark which ignited, there was one almighty showdown between matter and anti-matter and the universe was the winner on the day. *(Oct 6, 2001,Dominion,2)*

### wino
  
noun. a wine connoisseur or expert  
> Even experienced winos are not immune to this failing. [He] recalls one options final when he guessed that a wine was a French riesling. It turned out to be a chardonnay. *(Jun 4, 1997,Capital Times,15)*

### Winston
  
noun. a nickname for the new 10 cent coin issued in 2006  
> Radio Hauraki's morning pirate crew have christened the new 10c coin the "Winston" - because it is brown, its tongue is poking out and it is 90c short of a dollar. *(Aug 1, 2006,Dominion Post,B4)*

### Winston First
  
noun. [Politics] the New Zealand First political party  
[NOTES: A satirical term for a party where the leader takes centre stage.]  
> Doug Woolerton of Winston First says he sometimes has two 20-minute showers a day. While that may have something to do with his political party always being in the shit, will he do anything about this stupidity? *(Oct 15, 2008,Capital Times,6)*  
> It [the Clark government] is now too scared to pick a fight with the silly and backward-looking folks of Winston First and United Future. *(Jul 9, 2006,Sunday Star Times,C8)*

### Winston Peterry
  
adjective.  
> Resorting to personalities, which he has done in no uncertain terms, is unbecoming and the weapon of the weak. It's a bit "winston peterry". *(Sep 17, 1998,City Voice,22)*

### Winstongate
  
noun. [Politics] the 2008 scandal around the non-disclosure by Winston Peters of donations made to him and to the NZ First Party by millionaire Owen Glen and others, and implication of concealed knowledge by the Labour Government.  
> I'm pleased that Helen Clark and Labour are campaigning on trust. So let's see - paintergate, corngate, speedgate, Winstongate, seabed-and-foreshoregate ... *(Sep 27, 2008,Dominion Post,B4)*

### Winstonville
  
noun. political oblivion  
> Sadly for the Alliance, its Cinderella status in government is not endearing it to the public. Its highest poll tally recently has been less than six per cent. It is heading for Winstonville. *(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*

### winter
  
verb. to spend winter in Antarctica  
See: also winter over  
> New Zealanders, Englishmen and a Scot, are included in the team selected to winter at Scott Base through 1971. *(Sep 1970,Antarctic,5(11) : 450)*

### winter
  
verb. to take an off-season (winter) job  
[NOTES: ca1950s at least (H. Wilford, Hawkes Bay)]  
> **COLIN GORDON** This is a seasonal job and gives rise to phrases like: `Chaps winter at a certain job (e.g. shearing) in the <i>off-season</i>, and come back regularly. *(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works]

### winter
  
verb. (of stock) spend the winter  
See: cf. summer  
> **WHEELER** `Our 45,000 halfbreds summer high on the Nevis block,' says Frank, `and winter far below on sunny spurs.' Compare summer country. *(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,7)*

### winter
  
verb. to carry [livestock] over the non-productive season; [of livestock] to be so carried. [specifically used to indicate land productivity and value.]  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 313 For decades when the run was at its maximum in size, the hogget flock of 6000 or 7000 was autumned and wintered on self-sown un-topdressed suckling. *(1921)*  
> TAIT Farms and Stations of NZ I 14 The country winters two sheep to the acre. *(1956)*  
> ibid 139 No supplementary feed is necessary to winter the herd. *(1956)*  
> GASCOIGNE From the Top of a Hill 13 .. even broken-mouthed ewes will winter quite well and rear a good lamb. *(1965)*  
> Daily News Nov 16. 17 The fertiliser is going on, management is changing and we're learning to winter better. *(2000)*

### winter
  
verb. to carry stock through the winter period  
> Over the past few years Harmony has wintered 675 Romney ewes, 180 ewe hoggets.. and three horses. *(Nov 29, 2002,Dominion Post,C9)*  
> **Newton** the place is now wintering 5000 sheep . . . *(1973,Big Country,29,1977)*

### winter block
  
noun. a part of a high country station beneath the snow line where sheep can be safely grazed in winter.  
> J of Ag Sept. 261 Another approach involves concentrating the sheep on one or more of the winter blocks for this six-week period and spelling the others. *(1961)*  
> MACNICOL Beyond the Skippers Road 37 Sheep drifted down from their winter blocks seeking young green food about the terraces and often got 'bluffed.' *(1965)*  
> ANDERSON Over the River 132 Once again, the drafting and eye-clipping, dipping and crutching, turn the yards into an uproar before the flocks are driven away to their winter blocks ... *(1966)*  
> WHEELER Historic Sheep Stations of the South Island 16 Drafting, crutching, eye-clipping and dipping will be carried out before sheep are placed on safe winter blocks. *(1971)*  
> HOLDEN Station Country I 13 It was common to see stags on our best winter blocks.. *(1993)*  
> In the event of the winter blocks being taken from the Crown tenants next year, I am persuaded that the mountains will soon be abandoned. *(Apr 29, 1882,Otago Witness,12)*

### winter comb
  
noun. a shearing comb that does not shear close to skin, allowing sheep some protection.  
See: snow comb; cover comb.  
> In the Paddock Aug 2 Genuine winter combs or winter combs plus lifters must be used. *(2001)*  
> In the Paddock June 1 Winter combs have nine teeth and a sled on every second tooth. *(2002)*  
> Wool Grower Spring 22 The use of winter combs with attached 'lifters' is becoming more common in colder areas. *(2002)*

### winter country
  
noun. [Rural] land that is north-facing and at sufficiently low altitude for stock to safely graze during winter.  
See: safe country, wintering country  
> **MORRIS** <i>Winter Country</i>, in New Zealand (South Island), land so far unaffected by snow that tock is wintered on it. *(1898,Austral English,513)*  
> **PERRY** The lower [high] country, which can be used all the year round without serious risk of loss from snow, and which usually consists of low safe hills or steep country with a sunny aspect, is termed `winter country'. *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,88)*  
> **BURDON** The safety of winter country depends not so much on its low altitude as on whether it slopes steeply and brokenly towards the north, so that it receives what heat there may be in the weak winter sun. *(1938,High Country,156)*  
> **BAKER** ..and the expressions summer country and winter country of farming usage. *(1941,NZ Slang,46)*  
> <i>May</i>: All cattle to be mustered on to winter country. *(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,356)*  
> **WALL** Good winter-country, where sweet grasses grow. *(1950,Century of NZ's Praise, A,84)* [NOTES: [c1912]]  
> **SHARPE** `Winter country', as it is known in the ranges, is the warmest on any sheep-run. *(1962,Country Occasions,175)*  
> **MACASKILL** Practically the whole of the sunny faces (the winter country) had been in possession of rabbits for some time. <i>Ibid.</i> 165 The proposed use of 50 tons of 1080 and pollard on the winter country this coming spring, will cover about half the rabbit-infested area of the station. *(1969,Molesworth,122)*  
> **MOORE** All these stations in the mountain tussock lands would be divided by fencing into what was known as `summer' and `winter' country. The summer country was mostly comprised of the higher ranges and those lying away from the sun where the winter snowfall was heavy. Sheep were run on this range country through the summer months only, and brought down to the lower country - the winter country - in the late autumn. This consisted of rolling downs, the lower hills, and faces lying to the sun where snow did not lie. *(1969,From Forest to Farm,21)*  
> **WHEELER** In the South Island the terms `summer' and `winter' country have a very real meaning for the station owner. *(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,92)*  
> **MORRIS** Winter country, in New Zealand (South Island) land so far unaffected by snow that stock is wintered on it. *(1898,Austral English,513)*  
> Of all those stations possessing country above 3,000 ft, the grazing-ground is more or less sharply divided off into what is called 'summer country' and 'winter country.' *(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7)*  
> A good deal of snow fell in the back country and snow-raking had to be resorted to, but sheep are now safely mustered on to winter country. *(Jun 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,522)*  
> The slopes facing north or north-east determine the amount of winter country- i.e., country on the lower slopes of which the flock can be maintained during the period May-August reasonably safe from snow, or where the snow does nbot lie deeply for a long period. *(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,100)*  
> **SHARPE** 'Winter country' as it is known in the ranges, is the warmest on any sheep-run. .. it is the sunny side of a range which carries the rather grim appellation of 'winter country.' *(1962,Country Occasions,175)*  
> That by making arrangement with other runholders to make common cause for certain runs, as against other runs, I succeeded, ultimately, after two severe contests, before the Waste Lands Board, with several opposing counsel, in not only saving the said license, but in gaining an addition of other country of great value - as winter country - which the said James Ure Russell then informed me, was equal to about 5000 acres in extend, and added several thousand pounds to the value of his run. *(May 12, 1866,Otago Witness,3)*  
> R.S. 36086 Freehold, containing about 1695 Acres, good winter country, well fenced, and close to "Three Springs." *(Jan 17, 1900,Timaru Herald,3)*  
> These lands should be so cut up as to adjoin lands in Class B, and the purchasers and lessees of lands in Class B would probably lease these lands of inferior quality and higher elevation to work in with their winter country. *(Jun 2, 1876,Otago Witness,2)*  
> I think 1000 or 2000-acre blocks, properly surveyed, containing summer and winter country, disposed of on the deferred pastoral payment system, would be quite large enough for any family to live on except the very worst high mountain land, which might be let in 5000-acre blocks. *(May 21, 1881,Tuapeka Times,3)*  
> Now the cold season is approaching, so most of the sheep go on to "winter country" and "the farm. *(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),III/109)*  
> **NEWTON** Usually steep sunny faces were favoured for winter country. *(1966,Boss's Story, The,58)*

### winter country
  
noun. an area suitable for pasture throughout the winter, also occas. wintering country, country suitable for wintering stock on (cf. summer country)

### winter dip
  
noun.  
> **MEEK** WINTER DIP: The dipping of the sheep in sheep dip, for the prevention and destruction of ticks. *(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111)*

### winter feed
  
noun. stock food that is grown on a pastoral property, and possibly dried or conserved for winter.  
> DUPPA in Crawford (1949) Sheep & Sheepmen of Canterbury 39  Burn off portions of the run for winter feed to destroy tuft grass. *(1860)*  
> J of Ag March 15. 224 The common trouble in the bush district is the scarcity of winter feed. *(1912)*  
> Press Dec 2. 59 29 main paddocks - 46 ha hay, baleage, greenfeed and winter feed. *(2000)*

### winter field
  
noun.  
[NOTES: gumdigging]  
> Gum-digging may be roughly divided into two classes - viz., that on the `Winter-fields7 [check form], or the high tea-tree ranges where the ground is too high [check hard] to work in dry weather, and that on the `summer-fields', or low swampy situations, where digging would be impossible during the wet season. *(1908,TrNZI,40/70)* [NOTES: Walsh]  
> The traffic is often greater in the winter than the summer, as men who have other work in the summer go out to what is termed "winter fields." *(Aug 5, 1893,Northern Advocate,4,4)*  
> It should be borne in mind, however, that the winter fields are becoming greatly restricted in area, and that most of the gum comes from land that it is only possible to work in summer. *(Jul 12, 1902,Auckland  Star,4)*

### winter field
  
noun.  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> But from the head of Wakatipu there were glowing accounts of a most likely winter field, on which men were at the time simply nuggeting out with knives, very heavy gold being found under large stones and in crevices. *(May 2, 1863,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> The most will probably be made of fine weather, for it is not a winter field owing to the snow lying heavy on the ground or plateaus. *(Mar 24, 1887,Southland Times,3)*

### winter it
  
verb. to stay for the winter  
> ... those who have decided on `wintering it' at the diggings appear to have no reason to regret the step they have taken. *(Jul 8, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4)*  
> **J.G. WALKER** We were talking to a man ... who says he knows of a good creek for gold ... He talks about packing stores over there and wintering it and has offered to lay us on. We may go over and try it if we do not find a good opening on the river. *(Jan 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker))* [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

### winter migrant
  
noun.  
> **BULLER** By the settlers it [sc <i>Zosterops</i>] has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye, or Silver-eye, in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird', or `Winter Migrant'. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/82)*  
> It is the white-eye, the silver-eye, the ring-eye, the wax-eye, the blight-bird, or the winter migrant. *(Oct 10, 1908,Evening Post,13)*

### winter milk
  
noun. a supply of milk by cows that are not dried off in the autumn.  
> Daily News April 6. 19 Meanwhile those dairy farmers who supply winter milk and traditionally calve halve half their herd in February, March or April are having a bonanza. In Northland where winter milk production is a major farming feature, the autumn calf drop will be 20,000-30,000. *(2000)*  
> Dominion Post July 20 B9 Dairy staff required for 400 cow winter milk farm .. *(2002)*

### winter milk
  
verb. to milk cows through the winter season [as in town supply herds].  
> Dairy Exporter May 1. 42 Cows that are to be winter milked are separated from the main herd ... *(1945)*

### winter milker
  
noun. a cow that is milked throughout winter, probably on a town supply farm.  
> Dairy Exporter May 1. 42 ..I turn the cow in with the winter milkers for attention at the evening milking. *(1945)*

### winter milking
  
noun. the milking of cows throughout winter for factory or town supply purposes.  
> Daily News July 5 Future for many lies in winter milking .. Rain and mild temperatures in May helped with grass growth, but the real key to the winter milking operation is the grain storage and meal feeding facilities .. He [Mr Begg] believes winter milking may become a practice of the future because, instead of having to pay premium prices for peak rights, farmers will draw the season out to a year-round operation. *(2001)*

### winter muster
  
noun. [Rural] a gathering of stock for drafting, shearing etc during the winter season.  
> **SHARPE** .. by the end of a winter muster, horses were a bit thin. *(1966,Fiordland Muster,127)*

### winter off
  
verb. to graze stock off a home property during the winter months to conserve pasture growth.  
> Dairy Exporter Sept 11 5 It is the general practice in the district to winter off. *(1961)*  
> Dairy Exporter June 13 .. part of the herd is wintered off. *(1977)*  
> Dairy Exporter Sept 57 All cows are wintered off the property on nearby farms. *(1997)*  
> Fencepost.com Expert Farmer May 18 ..the control treatment was managed on conventional lines for the farm and the district, the others had its cows wintered off - basically from June 1st until 2-3 weeks before due calving date. Finally, not all the herd needs to be wintered off. *(2001)*

### winter over
  
verb. to carry stock throughout the cold season when there is a likelihood of limited growth and feed; [of stock] to be so carried.  
> HALL A Shepherd's Year 36 Without the summer hay crop it would be impossible to winter-over so many stock beacause of the snow which often covers the ground, and the altitude and frosts. *(1987)*  
> NOLAN Droving Days 26 About the mid-1920s a number of cows were driven down the Cascade river to winter over in bush near the mouth. *(1998)*

### winter over
  
verb. to spend the winter in the Antarctic  
> **Herrick** I couldn't have imagined wintering over. *(1997,Year On Ice, A,13)*  
> **BILLING** His mother wrote that.. she had seen his arrival at McMurdo Sound in an American plane on TV and why did all the men who wintered over look so morose? *(1965,Forbush and the Penguins,62)*  
> The 1959 base party was relieved during the 1959-60 season and replaced by a party of 14 to winter over during 1960. *(1960,AJHR,H34 : 122)*  
> **Henderson** About fourteen New Zealanders winter-over here. *(1962,One Foot at the Pole,145)*

### winter party
  
noun. a wintering-over party in the Antarctic  
> **BILLING & MANNERING** New Zealand's winter party in Antarctica normally consists of three scientists or scientific technicians at Cape Hallett and a staff of twelve to fifteen at Scott Base. *(1964,South : Man and Nature in Antarctica,45)*

### winter pugged
  
adjective. [Rural] [of land, soil sole or sward] trampled by herds in wet winter conditions.  
> Long term reduction in productivity from winter pugged pasture is much greater and more prolonged than is generally appreciated  .. while a winter pugged paddock may have grass as long and as vigorous as those that had no damage, the probability is that there will be nowhere near the pasture density. *(May 18, 2001,Ferns,Expert Farmer)*

### Winter Show
  
noun.  
> **HENDERSON** Only now swims into perspective that prisoner-of-war reunion dinner in the gaunt tin castle, the Winter Show Building in Wellington, at least thirty years ago. *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,174)*  
> Visits of groups of children to the Wellington Winter Show by train were becoming events keenly awaited for year by year. *(1961,Carterton District Centennial Celebration,9)*  
> &#65279;The gentlemen now moving in Wellingtonhave wisely perceived that a winter show,held under a roof, is that which is bestsuited to the special circumstances ofthis city. *(Oct 9, 1899,NZ Times,4)*

### winter-active
  
adjective. [of forage crops, clovers and grasses] productive over winter.  
> J of Ag March 57 The technique of band-spraying to introduce the winter-active ryegrasses Tama, Italian, and 4709, proved to be successful. *(1979)*  
> Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants 9 More leafy and less winter-active than other aphid-resistant cultivars. *(1995)*

### winter-hungry
  
adjective. [Rural] [of farm stock] short of feed during slow winter growth.  
> **GASCOIGNE** There is not much in building a fence across flat land to keep in a few cows, but to build one on rough hill-country solidly enough to keep sheep and lambs in - and the winter-hungry steer out - is a work of high skill. *(1965,From the Top of a Hill,104)*

### wintering block
  
noun. a large paddock or holding beneath the snowline where wethers or other dry stock are grazed with little attention.  
> HOLDEN Station Country 38 Later the ewes went to their lambing blocks, the wethers to their wintering blocks. *(1993)*

### wintering country
  
noun. [Rural] land that carries stock in winter.  
> **A HOPE** I began the winter with 20,000 sheep ... on a block of wintering country, near Fairlie Creek. *(1895,Letter)* [NOTES: c1895-1896 A. Hope Letter in 1967 Harper Kettle on the Fuchsia 145]  
> While there are some advantages in having the wintering country adjacent to the dairy farm, it's about making the use of different classes of land. *(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,15)*  
> Mr Mitchell explained that 1500 acres of the land was flat and was worth aGBP2 per acres, and could be sold for that.   To do so, however, would be to spoil the sale of the higher lands, to which the portion he referred to made excellent wintering country. *(Feb 10, 1883,Southland Times,2)*  
> .. 400 acres in best grasses, 6 paddocks, fencing in good order, well watered by streams, seven-roomed modern house, stable, etc, winters upwards of 2a sheep and good wintering country. *(Sep 30, 1902,Evening Post,7)*  
> Exceptionally good wintering country. *(Jan 31, 1916,Wanganui Chronicle,8)*

### wintering country
  
noun.

### wintering pad
  
noun. a reinforced land area where stock can be fed with supplementary feed during winter.  
> NZ Farmer July 8. 53 Land drainage, wintering pads and more subdivision are all part of a successful all-grass system. *(1971)*  
> NZ Farmer Aug 1. 3 Clive Roberts a dairy farmer at Kopurahi near Ngatea feeds his cows on a wintering pad. *(1996)*  
> Daily News Oct 12. 18 Sheltered all weather wintering pad and feed bins.. *(2000)*  
> Daily News Nov 25. 38 .. the farm has excellent races, a 500 cow concrete wintering pad ... *(2000)*  
> Daily News Oct 31. 20 First farms with improvements like this are hard to find. 24 bale rotoflow cowshed, excellent racing and farm buildings. Has own wintering pad. *(2001)*  
> Daily News Feb 21. 21 Stay out of the mud! Concrete those wintering pads ... *(2002)*

### wintering paddock
  
noun. an area on a high country station where sheep are safely grazed from the time of the autumn muster until after the spring thaw.  
> BARNES Country Calendar 115 It is the time when musterers and their dogs head for the tops to bring the sheep down to the wintering paddocks. *(1987)*

### wintering party
  
noun.  
See: also winter party  
> The wintering party at the base had not seen a sunrise since March 18, engineering manager Jonathan Leitch said from Scott Base yesterday. *(Aug 18, 2001,Dominion,4)*

### wintering system
  
noun. a form of stock rotation to provide optimum feed during the winter months.  
> J of Ag April 40 We decided that, in order to ensure sufficient spring feed for the milkers, the slow rotation wintering system was the only one to adopt. The wintering system required was one which would provide absolute control of feed at all times. *(1968)*  
> ibid 41. This wintering system proved perfect for the situation. *(1968)*

### wintering-off
  
noun. the grazing of stock on a separate area for winter, or part of winter.  
> J of Ag Jan 15. 13 Although the system of wintering-off has increased total production, it has some radical weaknesses. This [conservation of surplus pasture] no doubt, is due to the practice of wintering-off, a major factor in producing the amount of hay and ensilage made. *(1945)*  
> Dairy Exporter Sept 11 5 He had increased his topdressing, but felt that he was losing some of the advantages of it because of his wintering-off policy. *(1961)*  
> NZ Farmer April 11. 59 This wintering-off was partly an outcome of the uncertainty at that stage concerning how soon the neighbouring property would be taken over. *(1968)*  
> Wanganui Chronicle Sept 12 17 He is still expanding with 500 cows to be milked this season, a new drystock farm to be developed for grazing heifers and wintering off ... [wintering off] *(2002)*

### wintering-over
  
adjective.  
> **HENDERSON** 'Can men catch colds down here?' 'Oh yes, especially the wintering-over group just after the first lot of summer visitors has come in, or when a relief arrives.' <i>Ibid.</i> 125 A chap wintering-over needs a fairly high degree of built-in native intelligence - that's what you want, all right. *(1962,One Foot at the Pole,97)*

### winterless North
  
noun.  
> Girls mature very quickly in the"winterless north," but this approach towomanhood generally brings with it a lossof complexion and good looks. *(Feb 5, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4)*  
> Here, in the winterless northern tip of the country, the possibilities [of entertainment] are endless. *(Dec 18, 2000,Evening Post,15)*  
> It's all part of the special community in the winterless north, he says. *(Jan 13, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B12)*  
> Up in the Winterless North I am learning how good the month may be. *(May 10, 1921,Quick March,19)*  
> **MILLS** I experienced ... bleak, wintry conditions in the supposedly `Winterless North' ... *(1960,Sheep-O,92)*  
> **SLATTER** He knew the winterless north was a tourist gimmick, the Hibiscus Coast hallucinatory, winter was winter anywhere in the land. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,23)*  
> **JOHNSON** And a blazing log fire even in the winterless north brings a feeling of deep content. *(1970,Life's Vagaries,74)*  
> **THOMAS** Nevertheless the `Roadless North', to use the term in general use at that time [c1904], was still well named. It was far more accurate then than the term `Winterless North' which came later. *(1970,Way Up North,72)*  
> **MARSHALL** The Member of Parliament for Kaitaia, Colonel Allan Bell, promoted the slogan, `Go north, young man', and pushed the claims of the winterless north [c1920s] - a claim which we who lived there sometimes had cause to doubt. *(1983,Memoirs,24)*  
> OUR CUSTOMERS up in the Winterless North and down in Southland benefit at sale time. *(Jan 21, 1915,Feilding Star,1)*  
> Colonel Allen Bell, lived in the Auckland district for many years, and then moved to Kaitaia, in what he always referred to as "the winterless north." *(Oct 15, 1936,Evening Post,10)*  
> Thousands of New Zealanders do not know of this delightful spot in New Zealand's winterless north. *(Apr 23, 1940,Evening Post)*  
> No one has seen the "winterless north"until they have crossed the ruggedMaungamuka range. *(Mar 29, 1950,NZ Truth,19)*

### winter-over
  
adjective.  
> **KNIGHT** May I also take this opportunity to wish your winter-over party a full and successful season in their studies. *(1988,Icebound,71)*

### winter-overer
  
noun.  
> Now I'll hand you over to my fellow winter-overers to tell you a bit about themselves. *(1993,Scott Base Newsletter,1 (winter) : 2)*

### winter's bark
  
noun.  
> **HECTOR** <i>Drimys axillaris,</i> Forst. Horopito, Pepper-tree Winter's Bark. --A small slender evergreen tree, very Handsome. Whole plant aromatic and stimulant. *(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129)*

### winter-safe
  
adjective. [Rural]  
> With split-calving to take advantage of winter-safe sandy paddocks and irrigation in summer they are closing in on a target of 1000 kilograms of milksolids a hectare. *(May 11, 2006,Dominion Post,C7)*

### Winton disease
  
noun. a liver disease of cattle and horses.  
> I regret to say that the disease known in this district as the Winton Disease has increased very much, and there have been a number of reported cases of horses dying through it. *(1894,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,II/43)*  
> The mortality amongst horses, known as the "Winton Disease", still continues in certain localities during the spring and summer months. *(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/31)*  
> A disease has made its appearance amongst cattle, especially in the Wallacetown and Makarewa districts, and quite a number have died during the last season through it. The disease is similar to that known as the "Winton Disease" amongst horses. *(1896,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,IV/32)*  
> The mysterious disease that is known as the "Winton Disease" has broken out again with fresh virulence [sc. amongst horses in Southland], and the area over which the animals are affected has been considerably extended.<i>Ibid.</i> IV/41"Winton Disease". This disease seems to be prevalent in a certain part of the colony called Winton, from whence the name is derived. It appears to have carried off many horses and cattle during the last ten or twelve years... Some have an opinion that ragwort is the cause of the disease, while others are opposed to this view. *(1896,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,IV/32)*  
> A large section of the Annual report.. ofthe Agricultural Department deals veryexhaustively with a disease in NewZealand known as the "Winton disease,"and in Canada as Picton disease. Thenature of the complaint consists of achronic inflammation of the liver ofhorses and cattle known as bipatic [sic;= ?  hepatic] cirrhosis, the counterpartof which in the human subject is known as"gin-drinker's" or "hob-nail" liver. *(Dec 9, 1903,Otago Daily Times,17)* [NOTES: Attributed to cattle eating ragwort,ragweed or stinking willie, also knownearlier as staggerwort or stammerwort]  
> The animals which seem most susceptible to its ill effects are horses and cattle, and it has been practically proved that ragwort is the cause of the so-called `Winton' disease. *(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,416)*  
> J.A. Gilruth was the first to show that the hepatic cirrhosis occurring in horses and cattle, the so-called `Winton' disease, in certain districts of New Zealand could be reproduced by feeding these animals on fresh or dried ragwort. *(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/638)* [NOTES: (Hosking and Brandt)]  
> <i>winton disease n, usu cap W</i> [prob. fr. <i>Winton</i>, town in southern South Island, New Zealand] : cirrhosis of the liver in horses and cattle resulting from the chronic poisoning by toxic constituents of ragworts and other noxious plants eaten in the pasturage - compare WALKABOUT DISEASE *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2623)*  
> NZ Farmer June 192 In New Zealand we have had a string of so-called 'mysterious' diseases. There is the well-known Tauranga sheep disease, the 'Waiau doze', in Southland the 'Winton horse disease' the cattle disease of the Northern districts last year and recently the Rotorua 'bush cattle disease.' *(1898)*  
> Farmers Union Advocate July 18 Ragwort is the cause of the disease known as cirrhosis of the liver in cattle and horses, and popularly known in the south as Winton disease. *(1903)*  
> Hawera Star Feb 17. 5 The question whether the ragwort weed was connected with the deadly ailment known as Winton disease was hotly debated two years ago (says the Southland Times.) *(1906)*  
> J of Ag May 15. 416 It has been practically proved that ragwort is the cause of the so-called "Winton" disease. *(1912)*  
> There was some talk among bystanders of the Winton disease having possibly reached Timaru, several cases of sudden deaths among horses and cattle being mentioned. *(Mar 28, 1889,Star, The,3)*

### Winton disease
  
noun. ragwort poisoning  
[NOTES: 1952 June 7 R. Mason letter (Wellington) Winton disease: Poisoning by ragwort. [Index to Journal of Agric. should give a date.]]

### Winton weed
  
noun. ragwort.  
> NZ Farmer March 315 In the same district a goodly amount of ragwort (originally known in Southland as Winton weed) has also been allowed to flower. *(1916)*

### WINZ
  
abbreviation. [acronym] Work and Income New Zealand  
[NOTES: Now (strictly) just Work and Income, a service of the Ministry of Social Development (TD 02.06)]  
> [Hdg] Dobber's name release prompts Winz review.... After being told that he was under investigation for benefit fraud, [he] asked the Department of Work and Income for his personal files. *(Jan 16, 1999,Evening Post,1)* [NOTES: Cross referrenced with <i>dobber</i>]  
> [Hdg] Police asked Winz to stop benefit. *(Apr 24, 1999,Evening Post,1)*  
> [Hdg] Make Winz bosses accountable for jet - MP. Top-level Works and Income managers should be held accountable after $165,000 was spent to charter a jet for a staff conference, says Green party MP Rod Donald. *(Jul 17, 1999,Evening Post,1)*  
> [Hdg] Winz 'held boot camps at hotels'. Work and Income NZ is understood to have held at least seven "boot camp" motivational conferences for staff in the central North Island this year. *(Jul 19, 1999,Dominion,3)*  
> [This letter claimed] office furniture was being discarded because it had brown trim rather than black trim and that witch-hunts were going on at Winz. *(Jul 30, 1999,Dominion,8)*  
> [Hdg] Ya Winz some, ya lose some... McGurk says Winz staff are made to feel personally accountable for decisions they make. *(Aug 10, 1999,Evening Post,7)*  
> <i>Seven Days Alone with Rebecca</i> deals with three certainties, death, cockroaches and slow service from WINZ. *(Mar 15, 2000,Capital Times,9)* [NOTES: Check date]  
> [Hdg] This is the WINZter of Our Discontent. *(Apr 3, 2000,Salient,20)*  
> **Dave Armstrong** I bet you're probably getting some sort of benefit from Ngati Welfare...I've got a mind to call up WINZ and dob you in. *(2007,Six Pack 2, The,106)*  
> Winz is a mess. This is not big news... The following is my first ever contact with a happy little cog in the Winz machine. *(Sep 23, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8)*  
> Some - many - have lost their jobs, had their benefits cut, been tipped out of their state houses and pursued by some Winz madperson. *(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*  
> It's not Winz any more. And they haven't gone back to good old Social Welfare, because that has Connotations. For at least the next five minutes, it's to be called DWI... Actually, it's the Department of Work and Income. *(Aug 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> ...the band's top-selling album guarantees her a place not on the rich list but in a WINZ queue. *(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13)*

### winze
  
noun.  
> **HENDERSON** WINZE: Quartz mine chute leading down from working stope to truck line. *(1961,Friends in Chains,190)* [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 190]  
> But Steel had already disappeared down the winze. ... The whole roof of the drive fell in. *(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,107)*  
> **BL.YTH** <i>Winze</i>: Sinking on the ore body. *(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11)*  
> ...work was abandoned. partly because working-costs were high, but more particularly because the main winze fell in. *(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,16/111)*  
> **McCARTHY** To get down to drive each level winzes were sunk down the 150 feet, and it was in sinking the winzes (almost like the passes mentioned earlier) that the big money was made. *(1973,Listen...!,54)*  
> **McARA** <i>Winze</i>: A small-section shaft sunk by working downwards from the level, using a hand-held jackhammer-drill. A compressed-air-operated winch was mounted on a platform at the top and a bucket used to remove the spoil, which was tipped from the platform into a truck. The men took turn about excavating in the winze and driving the hoist. The main hazards were: the danger of the bucket or a piece of rock falling; premature explosion while a man was climbing out of the winze; or drilling into an unexploded charge. Ventilation was supplied to the winze by a venturi and ducts. *(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),337)*  
> Winze : A small section shaft sunk from a level. *(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14)*  
> The winze in the Port Russell section of the mine has been sunk on the hanging wall of the lode. *(Apr 19, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,5)*

### WIPA
  
abbreviation. Wellington Independent Practice Association  
> The Wellington Independent Practice Association (Wipa) was contracted a year ago to run free health clinics at Porirua's four colleges. *(Jun 25, 2001,Evening Post,3)*  
> The Wellington Independent Practise Association expect the new regional health proposal to have little impact on GP services in the northern suburbs... Ms O"Malley said the WIPA, which represents the Johnsonville, Newlands and Onslow medical centres, would be canvassing all the issues, but their submission was still in the early stages. *(Jul 18, 2001,Norwester,2)*

### wipe
  
verb. to dismiss, dispense with  
> Any question of slapping us on a 252 can be wiped here and now. *(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,54)*

### wipe
  
verb.  
> [Hdg] <i>Pre-cut Houses `Wiped' For Importing </i> An Auckland [pre-cut house importing] firm ... says it has been `completely wiped' by the cutting of overseas exchange funds. *(Jan 21, 1953,Evening Post)*

### wipe
  
noun.  
> **BOWEN** Grinding does not take long with the machine, ... Machine men only stop a few times a run to change cutters, compared with blade men giving their shears several wipes in a run. *(1955,Wool Away!,9)*

### wipe out
  
verb. to suspend (a jockey)  
[NOTES: (horse racing)]  
> ... recently Duggan was wiped out for foul riding. *(Sep 10, 1910,NZ Truth,272/2/4)*

### wiper
  
noun. a person who washes and wipes sheep carcases  
> **GORDON** The carcases are washed and wiped by a <i>wiper</i>, then a <i>stringer</i>, generally a boy, puts strings on the front legs and necks, and they go over to<i> the clock</i> which counts them. The carcases then go to the <i>pusher-up</i> who pushes them along to the scales to be weighed and graded. A <i>stamper </i>stamps the carcases and a <i>ticket-boy</i> puts a grade ticket on them out at the scales after the <i>grader</i> has graded them for weight and size. *(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

### wiper kick
  
noun.  
[NOTES: more commonly wipers kick (q.v.)]  
> **SLATTER** The wet weather first five-eighths ... with sure hands and a practised wiper kick to the corner flag that presented his wings with tries. <i>Ibid.</i> The Coach had the halfback and the first five working up the sideline with short kicks and ... he had the backs exhibit their varied roles, the wiper kick to the wings, the scissors move ... *(1968,Pagan Game, The,44)*

### wipers kick
  
noun.  
See: also wiper kick  
> Or one of those wipers kicks that wentbehind the other wing and bobbled aroundtill.. someone got there? *(Jan 9, 1994,Sunday Times (NZ),30)*  
> But the Waratahs struck back straight away with a Grey wipers kick collected on the full... *(Apr 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2)*  
> This time, the wipers kick... That wipers, he doesn't do it very well. *(Apr 18, 1992,Radio NZ Sporting Annual)*  
> **ALLEN & McLEAN** Whatever kick he was making - the hook back.. or the long one we used to call 'Wipers' pitching ahead of the openside wing.. - Mac was totally concentrated on the task. *(1970,Fred Allen on Rugby,82)*  
> One of Mehrten's best weapons is his'wipers' kick which turns the defence andpushes play to the opposite corner,setting up an attacking lineoutopportunity. *(Apr 16, 1995,Sunday Star Times,B3)*

### wire
  
verb. to hang (flax) on a wire line or fence to dry (cf WIRING)

> WANTED - An experienced man for Sledging and Wiring Flax. *(Apr 9, 1907,Marlborough Express,1)*

### wire fence
  
noun.

noun. <i>Gleichenia</i>

### wire in
  
verb. to begin vigorously; to attack; to hoe in (to)  
> **BARKER** We were politely told by Sandy to `wire in' [to a meal] - diggers' phraseology for an invitation to commence. *(1867,Station Life in NZ,191,1974)*  
> **PYKE** You'd best wire in [sc start working]. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,36)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** The Cocky, unsuspecting elf, agreed to wire in [and kill rabbits]. *(1950,Farm & Station Verse)* [NOTES: [1891 Starky] in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station verse [p??]]  
> <i> v.int.(slang)</i>. eat heartily. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>wire in v.i. (Sl.)</i> eat heartily. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*

### wire into
  
verb. to get stuck into  
> **MONEY** [I] hunted up a mate ... [and] `wired into' the work. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,147)*  
> **COTTLE** I wired into the boss like one o'clock. *(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,94)*  
> As soon as I was settled properly, I wired in to the driest part of the swamp, intending to drain it, only there didn't happen to be any outlet for the water, ... So I started stumping, instead. *(1924,Me and Gus,3,1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm)*  
> **LAWLOR** Ye can wire into those pears. *(1938,House of Templemore, The,117)*

### wire moss
  
noun.  
See: .  
> **MARTIN** The subalpine forests are sometimes carpeted for square yards at a time with WIRE MOSS, another giant moss hearing the cumbrous name of <i>Dendroligotrichum dendroides. </i> *(1961,Flora NZ,1929)*

### wire plant
  
noun.  
> **WHS ROBERTS** `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura known as `wire plant' (Olearia virgata). There were also manuka and koromiko. Maori-heads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. We did not pass any cabbage trees to-day ... we proceeded slowly through tomatakuru scrub, speargrass, Spaniard, snowgrass, and other native grasses ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' *(1856,Diary)* [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18 Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856]  
> We call these trees and shrubs that can resist browsing by birds 'wire plant' in recognition of their tough, sinuous stems and their uniqueness to New Zealand... A very few wire plants are able to cope with mammals and this accounts for why they remain common. These have spines (for example matagouri) and the ability to form impenetrable hedges... Wire plants are perhaps best recognised by the tugging test - take a shoot of less than three millimetres in diameter, and pull. If it is hard to break then it is probably a wire plant! *(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/6)*  
> There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura, with a thin, short, needle-like leaf, known as "wire plant: (Olearia Virgata). *(Oct 3, 1894,North Otago Times,1)*  
> **ALLEN** <i>Olearia virgata</i> [fam. Compositae]. Slender divaricate shrub up to c 2.5 m tall; found in lowland shrubland and boggy ground from lat. 37  southwards. *(1982,Flora NZ,I/671)*

noun.

### wire strainer
  
noun. any of various kinds of usu. patented devices for tightening fence wires

> I have just returned from a tour of New Zealand. While there I saw and was much delighted with your fence wire-strainer. *(Feb 13, 1890,Otago Witness,19)*

> Daily News May 11. 47 Clearing sale: .. Hunter chain fence, gate hinges and dogs, wire strainers, wool hooks ... *(2002)*

### wireweed
  
noun. The wireweed (<i>Melanthalia abscissa</i>) is another abundant North Island seaweed especially of surge channels, easily recognised by its bushy habit and wiry leafless stems, almost black when dry. a seaweed.  
> **MARTIN** A little lower down where the seaweeds are permanently submerged we have such plants as the GRAPELET, the WIRE- WEED, and OX-TONGUE; the FLAP-JACK and GIRDLE WEEDS. In rather deeper water ... we have the (<i>Lessonia</i>) and the OARWEED, the GRAPEWEED, and numerous representatives of the red algae. *(1961,Flora NZ,5,1929)*

### wireweed
  
noun. <i>Polygonum</i> pp., =hogweed; =knot grass, =mat weed, =pig weed, =road weed, ?wire-grass, wire vine  
> **COCKAYNE** Even experts cannot agree on the nativity of certain species, and discussions have taken place, as in the case of the wireweed <i>(Polygonum aviculare). </i> *(1910,NZ Plants,126)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WIREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>), also called road weed, mat weed, knot grass, [pig weed,] and hog weed, is common in cultivated land all over the country. It is an annual, with stems up to 2ft. in length trailing over the ground. The stems have swollen nodes (`knot grass') and are very tough (`wireweed'). *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,61,1967)*  
> **WILSON** [reference to McKenzie Country, c1870s-1880s] ... a harsh brown weed that dried in autumn and blew away in balls hanging on to fences. We called it wire weed. *(1950,My First 80 Years,15)*  
> **LEVY** Wireweed - <i>Polygonum aviculare. </i> *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,Appendix II 358,1970)*  
> <i>wire weed [=] wire vine [s] ..wireweed [s] Polygonum aviculare</i> agg. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,88)*  
> **TAYLOR** WIREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>) ... This European weed is widespread in New Zealand in gateways, driveways, and other places where its tolerance to treading enables survival. *(1980,Weeds of Crops,122)*  
> Polygonum aviculare, the so-called wire-weed, and the var. Driandri, are abundant everywhere. *(1904,TrNZI,36/213)*  
> Mr Bankin at the same time was using a drag harrow with chisel teeth for clearing his ground of couchgrass and wireweed, or hogweed as we called it. *(Dec 1, 1892,Otago Witness,11)*  
> Yes, fifteen young sparrows to be fed on insect pests until they can take seeds of fathen, wireweed, tares, all sorts of weeds, grass grubs, centipedes - fifteen young sparrows to be fed;  and they eat a lot. *(Aug 31, 1931,Evening Post,7)*  
> Wireweed Polygonum aviculare. *(Dec 11, 1942,Ellesmere Guardian,5)*

### wireworm

> If anybody ... has occupied himself with farming he will be only too well acquainted with what are known as `wireworms'... *(1893,TrNZI,25/199)*

### wiring
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (flaxmillers') obs.]  
> ... by the use of thorough hot-water washing, grassing can be eliminated and wiring cut down to a two-days' process, thus saving land and time. *(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/239)*

### wiring diagram
  
noun. a diagram illustrating theinterconnections of complex financialtransactions; hence (transferred) the schemeitself  
> The editors of the Independent walked the director and his accountants.. through the deal, referring to our handwritten notes and wiring diagrams we had, ourselves, constructed. *(Mar 7, 1997,Independent (Auckland),14)*  
> Hon. Winston Peters... Both arehandwritten wiring diagrams. One islabelled "JAP Bank".. and another islabelled "Telecom NZ". *(1994,NZ Parliamentary Debates,745)*  
> **WISHART** He [sc. Winston Peters] told Parliamentof another "straight-out fraud" in a newtransaction - a wiring diagram entitled"JAP Bank". *(1995,Paradise Conspiracy, The,243)*  
> The wiring diagram of the $100 millionredeemable preference share transaction,involving Bank of New Zealand, CapitalMarkets Ltd and shelf companies PanacheHoldings and Potonga Group, was displayedin front of Mr Peters on the computerscreen. *(Jun 7, 1996,Dominion,5)*  
> I am not putting myself up as an expertto go through it transaction bytransaction. But it is a wiring diagramthat would confuse an electrician. *(Jun 6, 1996,Dominion,8)*  
> Procedurally, the commission is becomingas complex as one of the wine-box wiringdiagrams. *(Nov 24, 1995,Independent (Auckland),44)*  
> [He] later admitted that he didn't understand the tax credit transactions and found a wiring diagram confusing]. *(Sep 1, 1996,Sunday Star Times,C5)*  
> Real power... went to.. the men who devised the deals and wrote the wiring diagrams that helped the corporates make money.<i> Ibid.</i> 32 He took the documents to lawyers and accountants far more versed than he in the wicked ways of tax evasion, and eventually figured out how the wiring diagrams worked. *(Sep 13, 1996,Independent (Auckland),22)*  
> The editors of the Independent walked the director and his accountants.. through the deal, referring to our handwritten notes and wiring diagrams we had, ourselves, constructed. *(Mar 7, 1997,Independent (Auckland),14)*  
> **WISHART** He drew an example of one such deal on a whiteboard, a wiring diagram of a Cook Islands-type tax loop. *(1995,Paradise Conspiracy, The,88)*  
> While the wiring diagrams considered by the court are among the most complex Chalkie has ever clapped eyes on, like most winebox transactions the deals can be spelt out in relatively simple terms. *(Aug 30, 2000,Independent Business Weekly,32)*  
> He demonstrated a wiring diagram on a whiteboard. *(Sep 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21)*

### witch
  
noun. megrim, mahue (<i>Caulopsetta</i> sp.)  
> Megrim ...Brill, Mahoa, Witch <i>Caulopsetta scapha </i> *(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197)* [NOTES: Waite]  
> <i>Caulopsetta scapha </i> Forster. This species referred to as the `brill' in my former list, is a totally different fish. This specific name belongs to the megrim, or witch, a common form, not sold as a food fish on account of its lean, bony character. *(1913,TrNZI,45/232)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]  
> CAULOPSETTA SCAPHA (Forster). <i>Megrim</i>. Known as `witch' or `lantern-fish' at Napier. Taken by trawlers in sandy localities as far north as Hauraki Gulf. Not popular as a food fish. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (unknown North Auckland Peninsula). *(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/121)* [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]  
> **SPEIGHT** The `commonest and most widely distributed of the flat fishes of New Zealand, and yet, unfortunately, the least valuable,' is the Megrim or Witch (Plate II. fig. 1). It is our only left sided flat fish, and is a very thin species full of long bones. *(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,201)* [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 201]  
> <i>Caulopsetta scapha </i>(Forster). Witch (megrim). *(1938,TrRSNZ,68/407)* [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]  
> **GRAHAM** NEW ZEALAND MEGRIM or WITCH (MAHUE) <i>Caulopsetta scapha </i>(Bloch and Schneider) ... In Napier it is known as Lantern-fish, but for what reason I am unable to say, unless it is from the peculiar transparency when it/is held up to a strong light. Another name given to this useless fish by local fishermen, in Port Chalmers at least, was that of Cadger's-fish. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,186,2nd)*  
> **NATUSCH** [caption] Fig. 66: FLATFISHES. I, Turbot 2. Brill (both <i>Colisteum</i> spp). 3. Flounder, <i>Rhombosolea</i>. 4. Sole, <i>Peltorhamphus</i>. 5. Megrim or witch, <i>Caulopsetta</i> (eyes left!). 6. Lemon sole, <i>Pelotretis. </i> *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,218)*  
> **AYLONG** <i>WITCH </i>(Megrim, mahue) <i>Arnoglossus scapha</i>... The witch is a thin-bodied left eye flounder with an oval shape ... However, they have no commercial value as they have thin watery flesh and many fine bones. *(1982,Collins Guide,307,1984)*  
> **AYLONG** <i>WITCH </i>(Megrim, mahue) <i>Arnoglossus scapha</i>... The witch is a thin-bodied left eye flounder with an oval shape ... However, they have no commercial value as they have thin watery flesh and many fine bones. *(1982,Collins Guide,307,1984)*  
> Ironically, the most abundant of all New Zealand flounders, the witch or megrim, is useless for eating because of its multitude of long thin hair-like bones and thin watery flesh. The Maori name for this fish - mahue - says it all. It translates as `to be left behind'. *(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37)*  
> [species name] witch .. [scientific name] <i>Arnoglossus scapha</i>.. [other name(s)] megrim *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> **PAUL** <i>Witch Arnoglossus scapha </i>Also called megrim, both names derived from similar European flatfishes. Found only in New Zealand. ... A related but smaller species,  the crested flounder <i>Lophonectes gallus</i>, of northern coastal waters, can be distinguished by a `crest' of long white dorsal fin rays. *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,145)*  
> **PAUL et al.** [index to common names 283-293] witch 2217-21, 2237 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### with one's number still wet

Don't know how some of you jokers with your number still wet get on. They've got you well slogged. I'm the only joker around here who knows what's going on. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,102)*

### within cooee
  
phrase. within call, within hailing distance; hence, within reach  
> Often before he gets within coo-ee of it - he will find himself beset by an ill-clad and evil-smelling mob of hooligans all yelling fit to raise Lazarus. *(1916,On the Anzac Trail,60)*  
> ... daughter is safe when the gent is within cooee distance. *(Dec 23, 1905,NZ Truth,27/4/8)*  
> **THOMSON** He knew he had only to keep within `cooee' of Dennis and he would have him `clean beat' in the end. *(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,16)*  
> **WHITE** .. I am terribly nervous if any of my party get [54] beyond `cooee' or call. *(1905,My NZ Garden,53)*  
> ... no auriferous reef could possibly be found within `cooee' of the spot where Michael's pegs were placed. *(1930,NZ Short Stories,219)* [NOTES: Philps]  
> ~ A long-distance call adopted by Australian settlers from the natives; also used in New Zealand. `Within cooee' means within calling distance. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),96)*  
> **SYDER & HODGETTS** To remain within call. .in 4;`Don't go far. Keep within cooee, so if you yell, I can hear you.' *(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],633)* [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 633]  
> **LELAND** <i>within cooe</i>: I think this is on loan from the Aussies. Originally it meant within calling distance, but now it usually just means HANDY or around. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,113)*  
> <i>cooee or cooey ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. inf. </i>calling distance (<i>esp. in</i> within (a) cooee (of)). *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,246)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 162; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 104]]  
> **ROSIER-JONES** `Everyone seems to be moving away from the pa. ... At least we're still within cooee of the pa. ...' *(1985,Cast Two Shadows,89)*  
> No other oyster in the world comes within cooey of the Bluff oyster. *(May 13, 1988,Contact (Wgtn),2)*  
> We fussed about all day and by night got all necessary things within cohi as we say here, that is, able to put your hand on them at a moments notice ... *(Jun 15, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,190,1950)*  
> **BRACKEN** Within a long `cooey'/ Av ould Wanganui/ Ye couldn't raitch, darlint, yer out in the could. *(1880,Paddy Murphy's Budget,60)*  
> **SARGESON** ... the town shows no sign of a hill anywhere within coo-ee. *(1964,Wrestling with the Angel,81)*  
> **LELAND** Coo-ee: I think this one is on loan from the Aussies. Originally it meant within calling distance, but now it usually just means HANDY or around. `Is your mate Bazzer within cooe?' Is your friend Barry around? (see: Aussie, Bazzer, mate) *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,113)*  
> **IRELAND** The street lights were so dim he got lost several times within cooee of his own front door. *(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,11)*  
> Since the Seddon administration, no other government can come within "cooee" of the first Labour Government's record for endeavour performance. *(Jan 20, 1938,NZ Observer,7)*  
> It is really most interesting to watch the women when they get within coo-ee of a mirror. *(Dec 20, 1913,NZ Observer,21)*  
> Out of over a score of bombs not one landed within "cooee" of the mark. *(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(10) : 26)*

### Wiwi
  
noun. [Maori] a French person  
See: DNZE for other citations  
[NOTES: also Wi-wi; metonym from French oui oui (yes)]  
> **Grace** 'We believed her,' she said. 'Seen her with that Wiwi, that Dutch, that Swedish, whatever.' *(2001,Dogside Story,171)*

### wiwi
  
noun. a native reed or swamp plant of the Juncus spp. growing on low-lying flats.  
[NOTES: also wi]  
> Low-lying grass lands will, if neglected, be smothered in wiwi (<i>Juncus effusus</i>) in a few years. *(1911,TrNZI,43/201)*  
> GRANT & FOSTER NZ: Report on Agric Conditions 9 Much of the land here is covered with 'wiwi', a dwarf kind of native grass, but this when sold yields as good crops as the fernland. *(1880)*  
> NZ Farmer April 289 Mr Bedford pointed out how a strong growth of paspalum was growing in amongst the wiwi. *(1908)*  
> Dairy Exporter Aug 1. 52 Unfortunately one still sees flat areas over-run with niggerheads, wiwi (native rush) and coarse clumps of grass. *(1936)*  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ II 291 After being drained, the flats were thickly covered by "wiwi" the native reeds, which were ably dealt with by H.F.Kemp using a fine team of horses and a swamp plough. *(1958)*  
> .. there shall be levied in respect of every building, constructed wholly, or in part of raupo, nikau, toetoe, wiwi, kakaho, straw or thatch of any description, and situate within the boundaries so defined, the sum of twenty pounds. *(Oct 31, 1857,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> Description of Land - 337, about a acre swampy, kahikatea bush, the rest open country, 28 acres flax and wiwi, undulating land ... *(Jun 16, 1881,Waikato Times,3)*  
> The plains are covered with a dry grass called "wiwi." *(Jul 10, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> **CROWE** Sea rush is a true rush and a native of New Zealand and Australia... The Maori name, wiwi, is also used for knobby clubrush. *(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,50)*

### wiwi swamp
  
noun.  
> You then come to three hills situated about a quarter of a mile back from the bank of the river on a flat of ti-tree, flax, and wiwi swamp. *(Dec 12, 1863,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> Chiefly raupo and wiwi swamp, on main road, near Te Puke. *(Jan 19, 1900,Auckland  Star,8)*  
> FARM PROPERTY, situated about 9 miles from Omaha, 200 acres good flax and raupo swamp of first-class quality, 450 acres wiwi swamp of inferior quality, and balance fern spurs, half of which are of good quality clay soil. *(Sep 9, 1925,Rodney and Otamatea Times,7)*

### wiwi-covered
  
adjective.

These beds of rushes ... are dry in summer and are then a favourite resort for the `Swamp-Sparrow', as this bird [sc fernbird] is sometimes called. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/60)*

> Anything good is wizard; ... *(Apr 4, 1952,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7)*

### wobbler
  
noun.

Palmer said that was a different thing, Drink was a curse; the churches were not, an opinion strongly opposed by the crowd, who called him a `wobbler,' and told him to answer to question *(Sep 5, 1908,NZ Truth,168/5/3)*

### wobbly
  
noun. a temper tantrum (esp. in phr. throw a wobbly)  
[NOTES: recorded earliest in NZ]  
> **DAVIS** What's the matter with you? You look queer. Ain't going to throw a wobbly, are you? <i>Ibid.</i> somebody could get hurt if one these days he went berkers or threw a wobbly in the middle of a job. *(1964,Watersiders,29)*  
> <i>to have/throw a wobbly:</i> to show anger *(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> **LELAND** <i> throw a wobbly</i>: Young children are often specialists at this, but their adult counterparts are often capable of a spectacular TEMPER TANTRUM. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,103)*  
> <i>throw a wobbly </i> *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> **STEWART** I did this once and the other bloke really threw a wobbly. All I could say was, `Sorry! ...', while he made nasty remarks ... *(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,40)*  
> <i>LThrow a wobbly</i>: The expression was not known to an Australian friend, but was to a NZ waitress (at the table where they were dining). *(Nov 1986,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: Kathleen Coleridge (Wgton)]  
> **McGILL** <i> wobbly</i> tantrum;<i> throw a wobbly</i> have a tantrum; can apply tp person or machine; eg `Every time I ask her to do something, she goes and throws a wobbly.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> Allegro Agitato [sc. a racehorse] still "has her odd wee way" and is always likely to "throw a wobbly", as Williamson put it. *(Apr 25, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),B17)*

phrase.
