### wobbly possum disease
  
noun. [Rural] a neurological viral disease of possums [ in development for use in biological control.]  
> **MAF** Wobbly possum disease was first diagnosed at the AgResearch possum colony at Invermay in early 1995. *(1997,BLANK ENTRY)* [NOTES: Mafnet/press/archive/190597]  
> **NSS** The virus causing wobbly possum syndrome has been isolated and is related to the exotic Borna Disease virus which is endemic in Europe ... [wobbly possum syndrome] *(Dec 1997,BLANK ENTRY,3)* [NOTES: Newsletter 4]  
> **DOC media release** A virus called "wobbly possum disease" has previously occurred in New Zealand, with the main symptoms being scouring and wasting away of the hindquarters, feeding during the day, trembling and bobbing of the head, unsteady gait, and lethargy. *(Aug 5, 2005,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: accessed 04/01/2006]

### wobbly possum virus
  
noun. Borna virus causing disorientation and lack of coordination in possums  
> The wobbly possum disease virus, first diagnosed in 1995, causes possums to come out in daylight and makes them disorientated and uncoordinated. *(May 20, 1997,Dominion,1)*  
> Eggs, meat and meat products did not transmit wobbly possum or Borna disease viruses, Biosecurity Minister Simon Upton said in Parliament yesterday,. *(May 21, 1997,Dominion,7)*  
> Wobbly possum disease - thought to be caused by a virus - has been tentatively diagnosed in an Otago possum, according to the Department of Conservation. *(Sep 8, 2005,NZ Herald,A3)*

abbreviation. Warrant of Fitness for a vehicle

> One informs you that "fines don't go away until you pay"'; the second lists the penalties for car offences, from $40 for parking on the footpath to $200 for failing to display a current WOF. *(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35)*

### wog
  
noun. an offensive term for a dark-skinned person  (esp. Egyptian or Arab), hence occas. used for a Maori  
> TVNZ said "Poms" was a term used affectionately by Kiwis and Australians, and it rejected the complainants' view that the word was comparable to terms such as "Chinks",  "Wogs", or "Horis". It agreed expressions such as "Pommy bastards" and "whingeing Poms" were terms of abuse. Authority chairman Sam Maling noted the Human Rights and Equal Opportunity Commission's ruling that the words "Pom" and "Pommy" were unlikely to offend of insult. *(Mar 24, 1999,Dominion,1)*  
> **YSKA** There was deep suspicion of the "wogs" which extended even to the Victory cigarettes from India issued to the soldiers. *(1990,NZ Green,44)*  
> That's not a Wog, son.  That's a Thirty-Niner! *(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(11) : 22)*

### wog
  
noun. a (minor) illness or infection  
> **MITCHELL** <i>the wog</i>: Don't be alarmed if your girl friend is in bed with the wog. She has a virus infection known clinically as `the bot.' *(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,184)*

### Wogan's wagon
  
noun.  
> His [Wogan's] name is likely to continue, attached to the `Wogan's Wagons' or mobile [x-ray] units, the first of which for the Christchurch area was landed in the South Island just before he died. *(Aug 1955,Dominion)*

### Wogistan
  
noun. A mythical country, not a thousand miles from New Zealand; Warren Berryman's invention to make statement about NZ business affairs which would otherwise be likely to lead to injunction against publishing or libel suits  
> In Wogistan dwelt Ali Baba, a man well-known among the republic's pleaders. Ali and his band had friends in high places. *(Feb 14, 1992,Independent (Auckland),00)*  
> Were there any such place, Wogistani public affairs would be carried out in a manner not altogether dissimilar to those in this green and pleasand land... So it came to pass that dozens of Wogistani punters were pulled into film partnerships, including one starring the Thin White Duke, who was making part of the movie in Southern Wogistan. *(Feb 14, 1992,National Business Review,00)* [NOTES: Check page]  
> It was an issue that Wogistani authorities would devote their entire attention to over the next 24 hours. *(1995,Paradise Conspiracy, The,232)*

### wok up
  
verb. cook in a wok  
> ...I brought pressure to bear and coerced the kitchen, working in top form this night, into wokking up a half plate of an irresistable-sounding special of green-lipped mussels. *(Jul 26, 2003,Dominion Post,F6)*

### wol
  
noun. a wally, a fool  
> <i>wol [?a wally, a fool] </i> *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### wolf spider
  
noun. [Environment] <i>Lycosa bilaris</i>  
> There's a wolf spider that lives in the scoria, but I hear no howls this morning. *(Oct 23, 2004,Dominion Post,E14)*  
> The common wolf spider (<i>Lycosa bilaris</i>) shelters in the base of tussock plants. *(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/46)*

### Wolf Wolf Go Home
  
phrase.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland<i>: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay</i>: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; <i>Canterbury</i>: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago</i>: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. *(1981,History of Children's Play,231)*

### wolfing
  
noun. the uneven combining of sheaves during harvesting.  
> Weekly News Aug 12. 74 If cut with a binder the bands are cut and the sheaves spread a little to prevent 'wolfing.' *(1936)*

### wolloper
  
noun. [Maori]  
[NOTES: cf walloper, universal; wolloper used in relation to kitchen work and to threshing.  Note US base ball use.]  
> One of the parties to the suit was at last called upon for an explanation, and he, with a look of contemptuous pity for those who had to confess such gross ignorance by enquiring, explained that "chaffy," "baggy," and "strawwolloper" were the respective names of artists who attended to the wants of a threshing machine. *(Sep 26, 1884,Hawke's Bay Herald,4)*  
> WANTED - Bag Sewers, Bagmen, Stackmen, Wollopers, Mill Cook. *(Jan 28, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,1)*  
> It is said that most of the leading Conservatives have [been] receiving lessons in Court etiquette from an old white bait dredger, who was once a kitchen wolloper in a gentleman's residence in at Picadilly, and that they are now able to bow as elegantly as a spavi[ned] Turk. *(Jan 5, 1892,Inangahua Times,2)*  
> The kitchen pot wolloper too, with her blue mottled elbows, was there in abundance, and rang out her dulcet notes of welcome on the morning zephers with as much enthusiasm as her more favored sister with the Sealskin ulster, more antique skirt, and pink complexion. *(Oct 24, 1892,North Otago Times,4)*

### woman
  
exclamation. a cry in gambling by tossing a coin - the tails or Britannia side  
> **DILKE** Instead of `change', [the Hokitika newsboy] cocked up his knee, slapped [?slapt] the shilling down on it, and said `Cry!' I accordingly cried `Woman!' and won, he loyally returning the coin, and walked [walking] off minus a paper. *(1868,Greater Britain,1/332 [?331])*

### woman
  
noun. invariant plural form of 'woman' now being reflected in print  
[NOTES: cf. 1837 Apr 6 Piraki Log (ed. Anson 1911) 51 This Day one of the Newzeland Whoman got Young Boay]  
> Police were called at 1 am on May 21 because the woman were fighting and throwing glasses at each other. *(May 31, 2000,Norwester,00)* [NOTES: Check page]

### Women's Land Service
  
noun. an organisation  of land girls during the Second World War.  
> NZPD June 17. 838 The Land Service was formed about a year ago, with the idea, firstly, of helping farmers who were desperately in need of help. These facts alone were a reason for the formation of the Women's Land Service. *(1943)*  
> J of Ag Oct 15. 252 Recruits from the Women's Land Service have proved themselves keen and conscientious to a degree, and have established a splendid record for enterprise and resourcefulness. *(1943)*  
> HANCOCK NZ at War 305 .. this scheme was remodelled in September, 1942, as the Women's Land Service ... *(1946)*  
> BARDSLEY Land Girls 13 Finally, in September 1942, when women pressured the Government to improve the service, it listened. The Women's Land Service was instituted. *(2000)*

### Women's Division
  
noun. part of a prison set aside for women inmates  
> **MacKENZIE** <i>women's division</i> part of Mt Eden prison set aside for female inmates in the 1950s. Later a maximum security division *(1980,While We Have Prisons,99)*

### Women's Division
  
noun. a women's organisation (WDFF)  
> **FARRELL** Miria Love is telling Joan Shaw that shedoesn't like the way she conducts Women'sDivision meetings. *(1992,Skinny Louie Book,22)*

### Women's Institute
  
noun.  
[NOTES: Previously Country Women's Institute, aka Institute]  
> ... the public pronouncements of the executive of the R.S.A., the observations on public morals from the Women's Institute. *(Sep 1952,Landfall,207)* [NOTES: Pearson `Fretful Sleepers']  
> Women's Institute is the largest women's organisation in New Zealand. *(May 12, 2010,Horowhenua Chronicle,8)*

### Women's Land Corps
  
noun.  
> The Women's Land Corps, as it was first known, was formed as part of the Women's War Service Auxiliary in 1940 to help meet the acute shortage of land labour. In 1942 it was reorganised as the Women's Land Service, with better pay and conditions, plus a full working kit and dress uniform as an added attraction... The WLS was the "ugly duckling" of the auxiliary service, despite their years of arduous work and extraordinarily long hours. *(Apr 9, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener))*

### Women's Land Corps
  
noun. an organisation of land girls during the Second World War (in 1942 organised as the Women's Land Service).  
> HANCOCK NZ At War 305 Womanpower for the land was first organised under the WWSA in the form of a Women's Land Corps ... *(1946)*  
> Listener 14 The Women's Land Corps, as it was first known, was formed as part of the Women's War Service Auxiliary ... *(1983)*  
> BARDSLEY Land Girls 12 While neither Government nor farmers seriously and wholeheartedly supported a comprehensive women's land service prior to September 1942, women nevertheless took the initiative and joined the Women's Land Corps. *(2000)*

### women's magazinisation
  
noun.  
> So we're left with the galloping women's magazinisation that's occurring on shows such as <i>Sunday, 60 Minutes</i> and <i> 20/20</i>. *(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78)*

### Women's Refuge
  
noun. a community house where women ill-treated in marriage or other relationships may go for safety, support and aid  
> One of the support groups I personally get most out of is the women's refuge... *(Sep 27, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35)*

### WONAAC
  
abbreviation. Women's National Abortion Action Campaign

### wonderberry
  
noun.  
> **HILGENDORF** BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>), is called also white nightshade, mother-of-thousands, black tomato, black potato, wonder berry, and by a serious confusion deadly nightshade and bittersweet. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967)*  
> The thing [sc. a flogging] leaves a broad weal ... soft, pulpy and the colour of one of those wonderberries. *(Nov 1950,Here & Now,16)*

### wonderberry
  
noun. black nightshade (Solanum nigrum).  
> J of Ag 121 Unfortunately, black nightshade has been given a variety of common names such as wonderberry, mother of thousands, deadly nightshade etc., and this last name has led many people to believe it has poisonous properties. *(1960)*  
> HILGENDORF Weeds in NZ *(1967)*

### Wonderland of the Pacific
  
phrase.  
> **PINFOLD** Some of the names given to it [sc New Zealand] are very suggestive and often throw light upon its position, character and destiny. It has been called the Britain of the South; the Wonderland of the Pacific; and God's Own Country. Maoriland is another name by which it is known and indicates the native race ... *(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,13)*

### wongawonga
  
noun. the eastern Australian climbing plant <i>Pandorea pandorana</i>, Family Bignoniaceae, with cream spotted flowers  
> It will be entered from the parking area at the front of the house through a pergola supporting a wongawonga vine. *(Feb 25, 2000,Dominion,13)*

### wonk
  
noun.  
> **NEWBOLD** If he wasn't going too well, the audience let him know about it. `Get off, ya wonk!' they'd yell ... But if the speaker could cop the swish, and give a bit in return (the more insulting the better) this would be met with loud cheers. [check cit] <i>Ibid.</i>76 As far as Whippet was concerned I was a new inmate,[77] and Whippy's experience was that new wonks didn't have any idea about what was going on in the world ... *(1982,Big Huey, The,71)*  
> **Newbold** WONK (n) Stupid, graceless person, moron. British naval slang about 1947. *(1982,Big Huey, The,255)*

### won't buy the bairn boots
  
phrase. said of anything (esp. money) obviously insufficient for its purpose.

### WONZ
  
abbreviation. Wools of New Zealand.  
> NZ Farmer Sept 4 15 Stuart Edwards, as senior technologist at WONZ says comparisons between shower and plunge dipping and handjetters favour the jetter. *(1997)*

### woo
  
noun. a (lovers') cuddle; a petting (session)  
[NOTES: 1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 27 WOO (3) -->1953 13 M A1 Thames DHS 27 (THE) WOO [M13-15 13] -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 27 WOO (5) -->1960 17C M B7 Hamilton H.S. 27 WOOING]  
> **GASKELL** Lena and Ted were sitting ... with their backs against the wall and an arm round each other ... `Having a good woo?' I said. *(1947,Big Game,34)*  
> **McGILL** <i>woo</i> a kiss and cuddle and maybe more, without intending to woo or pay court to and seek everlasting love; c.1930; eg `Had a bit of a woo with some sheila, next thing I know, it's morning and I've slept the night in the hydrangeas. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*

### wood
  
noun. trees as a land-cover; (also pl) used chiefly by unacclimatised British visitors and emigrants, until replaced by later (or `colonial-experienced) BUSH: and also (?following the early classic of Sir J. D. Hooker) in later floral handbooks (e.g. 1889 Featon Art Art Album of New Zealand Flora), and botanical writing: it has never been indigenously or popularly adopted (as FOREST has in technical writing): often found in comb. or as attrib. in early collocations which have survived into modern use, possibly reinforced by the existence of such combinations in other Englishes: WOODHEN, WOOD PIGEON  
> **PARKINSON** The country ... is entirely covered with wood, and full of a sort of supple-jack, that it is difficult to pass through it; ... *(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,115)*  
> **MARSDEN** Our way lay through a wood composed of various kinds of timber together with the noble pine. *(1815,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,99,1932)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** Cyclosorus penniger (Forst.f.) Copel. Common name: Gully fern ... This common fern is found in woods throughout New Zealand *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,28)*

### wood
  
noun. trees as a land-cover  
[NOTES: ?esp. in botanical; seems Cheeseman's usual word]  
> **PARKINSON** The woods abound with divers kinds of birds, such as parrots, wood-pigeons, water hens; ... *(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,115)*  
> **FORSTER** The brow on the larboard side of the Ship, which a few days ago was (<i>Ocydromus troglodytes</i>), a rare specimen. [check???] *(Apr 24, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/266)*  
> The Wild fowl are Ducks, Shaggs, Cormorrants, Oyster Catchers or Sea pies, Water or wood Hens, which are something like our English Rails, these inhabet [sic] the Skirts of the Woods and feed upon the Sea beach they [sic] are very like a Common Hen and eat very well in a Pye or Fricasee. *(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/121,1961)*  
> **FORSTER** [128 refers to a tree of myrtle genus used for tea during Cook's former voyage, and used now] Though the spruce and the tea-trees alone afforded articles of refreshment in Dusky Bay; we found the woods full of trees of various kinds ... *(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/130)*  
> **McNAB** The animals I left [at Dusky Sound] had fed themselves upon what they found in the woods, ... *(1908,Historical Records,1/179)* [NOTES: 1793 Nov 19 Captain Raven to Lieut.-Governor King in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/179]  
> **KENDALL** After some little conversation we proceeded on our way over some swamps and exceeding high hills. We observed no woods near us of any magnitude, and the tops of the hills were generally fertile and covered with good soil. *(Jun 13, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,61,1934)*  
> **McCRAE** impenetrable forest, is now clear & airy, & contains an Observatory, a forge, a green hut for the woodcutters & a pen for our Sheep; and more than an acre of ground is cleared of the woods; ... *(1820,Journal,20,1928)*  
> **SHEPHERD** The Tree Fern is a very striking object in the woods, it grows from 20 to 40 feet in hight [sic] and from four to eight inches in diameter in its stem, which is of a spongy brittle nature. *(1826,Journal)* [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 356]  
> **MARKHAM** [M. prefers forest and woods; bush occas.] When in the Woods she looked out for the (Coo Coupers) [<i>kukupa</i>] or Pigeons, and had a sharp eye. *(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56,1963)*  
> [Boultbee uses this more than BUSH] The distance we had to go was about 20 miles, part of the way led through woods, and part along Sandy beaches. *(1835,Boultbee Journal,72,1986)*  
> **POLACK** The bushes are literally filled with them [fantailed flycatchers], their varied note resounding through the woods until sunrise. *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/126 fn)*  
> ... the woods are skirted by a ... growth of the handsome Lace-bark tree <i>(Plagianthus Lyallii)</i> ... *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5)* [NOTES: (Hector)]  
> ... the once silent woods now resound with the blows of the felling-axe ... the Wattle-bird is no longer to be found in its old haunts; it seeks shelter amongst the higher parts of the bushy gullies ... the condition of the forest is changing under the effects of ... constantly-recurring bush fires. ... The Kokako loving a moist temperature will probably soon entirely forsake its ancient places of resort. *(1874,TrNZI,6/146)* [NOTES: (Potts)]  
> **HAY** The Weka ... is found plentifully in the woods. Settlers call it the `bush-hen'. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/219)*  
> **BULLER** In the strictest sense of the term, New Zealand is without `song-birds'; but such species as the Tui, the Korimako, and the Piopio possess vocal powers of a very respectable kind, the compass and variety of their notes adding greatly to the charm of the New-Zealand woods. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/xliii)*  
> The name <i>kakarika</i> [sic] (indicative of colour) is applied alike to the green lizard and to the green Parrakeet of our woods. *(1894,TrNZI,27/95fn)* [NOTES: [Morris]]  
> ... in our wood thirty or forty years ago ... [Buller also uses <i>bush</i> and <i>forest</i>] *(1899,TrNZI,31/12)* [NOTES: Buller]  
> The oldest material supports pure woods of <i>Corynocarpus </i>(karaka). *(1912,TrNZI,44/213)* [NOTES: Ashton]  
> Grows in damp woods ... *(1912,TrNZI,44/340)* [NOTES: Ironside]  
> <i>P. rotundifolia</i> Hook. is plentiful, not in dry woods, as stated in the Manual ... *(1915,TrNZI,47/79)*

### wood
  
noun. in floral and faunal names (in senses related to woods, and thus often = `wild', but not the material wood)  
See: groundsel, sage, sorrel

### wood and water joey
  
noun.  
[NOTES: ca1940 Havelock W.H.B. Orsman]  
> "I've been choppin' firewood for ye, boss.   Reglar wood and water Joey ..." *(Nov 1, 1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,60)* [NOTES: Year is incorrect.]  
> Too often in middle-class families it is the mother who is the Gibeonite (the wood and water Joey), and no shame is felt by her daughters that it is so. *(Feb 16, 1906,Grey River Argus,1)*  
> "Has anyone seen the water Joey?   I suppose he has gone over to the pub." *(Nov 4, 1927,Ellesmere Guardian,5)*  
> ".. Yes, sir, I've spent thousands amongst the business people, and see what I've come to - wood and water joey at a shanty." *(Sep 14, 1871,Tuapeka Times,5)*  
> A son of an Irish nobleman, who spent some years of his life in this colony, was, in turns, a private in the Colonial Defence Force, a billiard marker, wood and water Joey on a sheep station, a lawyer's clerk, a cab driver, and lastly, cook on board a small coaster sailing out of Auckland. *(May 6, 1886,Feilding Star,2)*  
> **BOLDREWOOD** `I'm not going to be wood-and-water joey, I can tell ye.' *(1888,Robbery under Arms,198)*

### wood block
  
noun. a chopping block; a wooden block used for chopping or splitting short lengths of firewood on a chopping block (here, an old stump)  
> **MANSFIELD** He simply threw it [the newly beheaded duck] away from him ... and jumped round the wood block. *(1920,Stories,249,1984 Alpers)*  
> **CRUMP** The stack of firewood stood waist-high beside the chopping block. There was little enough to see ... I'd sat on the wood block, dredged out the letter Jim Reed had given me, and worked the flap open with a knife. *(1960,Good Keen Man, A,14)*  
> Coil chain, wood blocks, lanterns. *(Sep 12, 1863,Otago Daily Times,1)*

### wood block paving
  
noun.

### wood chipboard
  
noun.  
[NOTES: 1989 Charles Ransom notes: Wood chipboard - To OED and Supp. this is a pasteboard, but Macquarie has it. US has <i>chipboard</i> in this sense but not <i>wood chipboard.</i> This term is so often shortened to <i>chipboard</i> in NZ that it seems a doubtful starter]  
> <i>Wood chipboard</i>- Common name given to wood particle board. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,131)*

noun.

noun. a weta

### wood duck
  
noun.

A `wood duck' is a person who walks onto the yard and says he will buy a car - without even so much as a struggle. *(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,2/1)*

noun. the edible mushroom <i>Auricularia polytricha</i>

### wood heap
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wood-heap, woodheap]

> **GROVER** I gratefully followed him out to the woodheap. He chopped and I talked ... *(1967,Another Man's Role,13)*  
> **HENDERSON** ... slinging the burden of today so casually up on the wood-heap, .. *(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,60)*  
> .. the axe was generally left on the wood heap at the hotel, and so were others too ... *(Oct 17, 1862,Otago Daily Times,5)*  
> The prisoner had evidently gone to the wood heap, and taken the weapon into the house with him, with the intention of committing murder, if necessary, to effect his object. *(Jul 25, 1872,Tuapeka Times,4)*  
> The prisoner had evidently gone to the wood heap, and taken the weapon into the house with him, with the intention of committing murder, if necessary, to effect his object. *(Jul 25, 1884,Tuapeka Times,4)*  
> Rubbish from the wood heap may be applied safely and with advantage to the soil in which young fruit trees are to be planted. *(Jun 25, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> The evidence at the enquiry disclosed the finding of an axe at the wood heap with blood stains on its head. *(Apr 26, 1898,Colonist (Nelson),3)*

### wood pigeon
  
noun. [Environment] (also called kereru, kuku or kukupa) a large, beautiful and distinctively coloured New Zealand bush pigeon; the bird has rarely been called <i>bush-pigeon</i>: the early use of the general English <i>wood pigeon</i> probably precluding it  
> The chief native birds are wood pigeons, kakas, wild ducks, tuis, shags, and all kinds of sea birds. Wood pigeons are very pretty birds and make good eating. Most of them have pink eyes. *(Mar 27, 1890,Otago Witness,42)*  
> The wood pigeon, he feared, was vanishing. *(May 26, 1910,Evening Post,7)*  
> We plunge down into  the wood, accompanied only by a rustle of wind scattering leaves and the beat of a wood pigeon's wings. *(1992,North & South,120)* [NOTES: Check month]  
> This genus is worthily represented in our lists by a fine Wood-pigeon - <i>Carpophaga Novae Zelandiae ...</i> *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/13)* [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]  
> CARPOPHAGA NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE. - Wood Pigeon (<i>Kuku</i>). *(1886,TrNZI,18/103)* [NOTES: (Reischek)]  
> **CRAIK** Among them are many sorts of wild ducks, large wood-pigeons, sea gulls, rails, parrots, and parroquets. <i>Ibid.</i> 247 I shot a few wood-pigeons. *(1830,New Zealanders, The,187)*  
> The following is a list of the birds shot .. <i>Platycercus auriceps,</i> yellow-top paroquet; <i>Nestor meridionalis,</i> brown parrot; <i>Carpophaga Novae Zealandiae</i>, wood pigeon; <i>Haematopus unicolor</i>, black oyster catcher, or red bill; <i>Haematopus longirostris</i>, pied oyster catcher; <i>Ocydromus australis</i>, Weka, or wood hen; <i>Casarca variegata,</i> Paradise duck ... *(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39)* [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]  
> Finally, his eyes rested on a large wood pigeon lying still on the floor. *(Feb 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> Mr Dix also fenced a piece of bush around his home to protect it from stock to "see more birdlife on the Chathams" [sic], especially wood pigeon (parea) around  my home". *(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 13)*  
> The words 'native game' include wild duck of any species, bittern, pied stilt, plover, wild geese, wood pigeon.. and any other native animal and bird which shall from time to time be proclaimed to come within the provisions relating to native game. *(May 2, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> **BAYLY** There are plenty of large Wood Pigeons much the same as Wood Pigeons in England. *(Apr 12, 1773,Journal,2/206)* [NOTES: 1773 Apr 12 William Bayly's Journal in 1908 Mcnab Historical Records 2/206]  
> **WALES** Of Fowl .. large and very beautiful Wood-Pidgeons; .. [fn]3. The Kereru ... *(1773,Journal)* [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in 1961 Cook's Journals 2/786]  
> The principal sorts are large brown Parrots with white or greyish heads, green Parroquets, with red foreheads, large wood Pigeons, [ed. note [Kereru or Wood-pigeon, <i>Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae.</i>]] brown above with white bellys [sic], the rest green, and the the bill and the feet red. *(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/806,1967)* [NOTES: (Queen Charlotte Sound) 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806]  
> **PARKINSON** The woods abound with diverse kinds of birds, such as parrots, wood-pigeons, water hens ... *(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,115)*  
> **BAYLY** There are plenty of large Wood Pigeons much the same as Wood Pigeons in England. *(Apr 12, 1773,Journal)* [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]  
> **BAYLY** During our ramble [in Queen Charlotte Sound] I saw Wood Pigeons, Parroquets, Grey Parrots, Poey Birds, & Quail & vast variety of singing Birds but no animal great or small or any fruit Trees of any kind whatever. *(Nov 15, 1773,Journal)* [NOTES: 1773 Nov 15 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/213]  
> Large wood-pigeons. *(1785,Cook's Third Voyage,38)* [NOTES: 1785 Hawkesworth's Cook Third Voyage 38 [check Cook Jnl]]  
> **YATE** <i>Kukupa </i>- A large wood-pigeon, very plentiful in New Zealand. *(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62)*  
> **POLACK** <i>Wood-pigeons</i> are very numerous in the woods, from January to April; they are delicious nutriment. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/297)*  
> The only things worth shooting are the wood-pigeon and wild-duck. *(1841,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement)* [NOTES: 1841 Establishment of New Plymouth Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 78]  
> **HODGSKIN** Wood pigeons are found in abundance .. much larger .. and more beautiful in plumage than our English pigeons. *(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,28)*  
> **HEAPHY** The wood-pigeon is in New Zealand very large .. The plumage of the bird is very beautiful, and the flesh is excellent .. Those of most service for the table are the various kinds of water-fowl, and the parrot and wood pigeon. *(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),47)*  
> **TAYLOR** .. kereru, kukupa (kuku, Carpophaga Noval Zelandiae), the wood-pigeon *(1855,Te Ika a Maui,406)* [NOTES: (Morris)]  
> **WAKEFIELD** <i>Kukupa (Columba spadicea)</i>.- A large wood-pigeon, very plentiful in all the forests. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,163)*  
> Carpophaga Novae Zelandiae. Pigeon. Kuku. *(1869,TrNZI,1/160)* [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]  
> **HUTTON** 56. CARPOPHAGA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Wood-pigeon. Kuku. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,21)*  
> **KENNAWAY** In the bush were numbers of wood-pigeon, large and in good condition ... *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,58)*  
> <i>Carpophaga novae-zealandiae</i>. - Kereru, Kuku, Pigeon. Both Islands, and Chatham Islands. *(1886,TrNZI,18/113)* [NOTES: (Martin)]  
> **BULLER** During the autumn months .. large numbers of Wood-Pigeon resort to these grounds .. and at this season become exceedingly fat. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/232)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Kereru</i>, n. <i>wood-pigeon. </i> *(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,59)*  
> **COWAN** Wood-pigeons (<i>kukupa</i>) and <i>kaka</i> parrots are found in the forests .. and the handsome paradise-duck (<i>putangitangi</i>) and blue mountain duck (<i>whio</i>) are numerous in the streams and lakes [of Western Southern Alps]. *(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 64)*  
> **MONCRIEFF** New Zealand Wood Pigeon *(1948,NZ Birds,64)*  
> **MONCRIEFF** New Zealand Wood Pigeon *(1948,NZ Birds,64)*  
> **SOLJAK** Another fearless bird is the wood pigeon, one of the finest in the world for its size, beautiful plumage and strength of flight. *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Pigeon, wood .. kereru, kuku, kukupa .. <i>Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae. </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **FULLER** WOODPIGEON, PRESERVED (HUAHUA) The kereru or native woodpigeon <i>(Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae)</i> is so tame .. that the species was in danger of extinction. *(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,78)*

### wood range
  
noun.

> **HOWE** ... Mum sweated over a wood range ... preparing hash, stews, roasts and vegetables for the ever-hungry bushmen. *(1964,Stamper Battery,7)*  
> **BRAMBLEY** When we were first married we did our cooking and baking and heated water on the wood range. *(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,19)*  
> **HARTLEY** The wood range was the only article salvaged from the cottage.. *(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,40)*  
> THE LATEST NEW IMPROVED CHRISTCHURCH COAL OR WOOD RANGES (ALL SIZES). *(Nov 1, 1892,Poverty Bay Herald,1)*  
> COOKING BY GAS.   IS fast superseding the old method of coal or wood ranges. *(Oct 8, 1907,Wairarapa Daily Times,2)*  
> With a Quirk gas cooker installed, the wood range can be entirely dispensed with. *(Jun 18, 1918,Evening Post,13)*  
> A reversion to the use of coal and wood ranges is predicted unless something more reliable is provided. *(Sep 1, 1920,Evening Post,7)*

noun.

> (Black Stewart Island) The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, bell bird or mocker; <i>Petroica albifrons</i>, wood robin; <i>Petroica macrocephala</i>, yellow-breasted tit; <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, pied fantail; *(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39)*  
> <i>Petroeca longpipes</i>, Less. Wood-robin. *(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,29)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> PETROICA LONGPIPES, <i>Less</i>. - Wood Robin (<i>Tototowai</i>). *(1887,TrNZI,19/186)* [NOTES: (Reischek)]  
> **BULLER** Oct. 29th. Found Wood-Robin's nest with two eggs. Oct. 31. Visited Robin's nest; four eggs. Nov. 3. Agreeably surprised to find egg of <i> Eudynamis taitensis</i> placed among the rest; ... *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/131)* [NOTES: 1888 W.W. Smith in 1888 Buller Birds of NZ 1/131]  
> <i>Miro albifrons</i> (South Island Robin). The wood-robin is an almost constant attendant ... in the bush or about the tents. *(1889,TrNZI,21/215)*

### wood rose
  
noun.  
See: also wooden rose, flower of Hades, pua reinga  
[NOTES: aka pua o te reinga, woodrose, <i>dactylanthus taylorii</i>]  
> Cages are being used to protect a rare endangered native plant, the wood rose, from predators in Taranaki. *(May 24, 2003,Press, The,19)*  
> A few hours later, John Morrison found a pate tree with some wood rose, <i>Dactylanthus taylorii</i>, growing from its root system... *(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/28)*  
> **MARTIN** ... many [endemic genera] bear no close relationship to any other known plant. Such, for instance are ... the root parasite (<i>Dactylanthus</i>) responsible for the wood roses often sold as curios. *(1929,Flora NZ,26,1961)*  
> <i>Bats</i>, by Alina Atklins (Reed), is charmingly illustrated and tells the lovely local tale of the special relationship between our rarest little native bat and the endangered wood rose, <i>Dactylanthus</i>. *(Dec 22, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45)*  
> Many new populations of threatened plants found in the past five years include the wood rose (<i>Dactylanthus taylorii</i>) (11 plants found  this year in the Wairarapa, the first-ever recording of the plant there), <i>Olearia gardner<</i>, the 'flooded forest' <i>Coprosma pedicellata</i> (more than 10 new populations), heart-leaved kohuhu <i>Pittosoporum obcordatum</i> (from one to four Wairarapa populations), the mistletoe <i>Tupeia antarctica</i> (not known from Wairapapa until six years ago and now at 15 known sites), and the small herb <i>Mazus novaezealandiae</i>) (more than 10 populations. *(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/16)*  
> &#65279;It has neither roots nor green leaves,and is found only in New Zealand.  Maorinamed it Pua o te reinga, Europeans callit the Flower of Hades, or Wood Rose, andto the botanist it is Dactylanthustaylorii. *(Jun 1992,North & South,18)*  
> This inbcludes a native broom on Mahurangi Island, Cook's scurvy grass on the Coromandel Islands and Dactylanthus (also known as wood rose) a highly unusual, parasitic plant in the Coromandel Ranges. *(Jan 28, 2011,Hauraki Herald,11)*  
> Dactylanthus taylorii has a special place n New Zealand's native flora as the country's only fully parasitic flowering plant.  Also known as pua o te reinga, wae-wae-atua, the wood rose or flower of Hades, it is hard for the untrained eye to spot as it nestles among tree roots. *(Apr 9, 1997,Waikato Times,21)*  
> Growing on the forest floor of Pati Tapu Bush is an extremely rare plant, the woodrose, or dactylanthus. .. Large numbers of woodrose were gathered from forests, and after they were boiled and varnished, they resembled a wooden rose and often took pride of place among home decorations. *(Jan 22, 2011,Dominion Post,A13)*

noun. oxalis, shamrock

> **HILGENDORF** OXALIS (<i>Oxalis corniculata </i>), sometimes called wood sorrel, or yellow wood sorrel, or yellow sour weed, is a small perennial common in lowland pastures over both islands. It is a native of New Zealand, but is found over most of the rest of the world as well. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,116,1967)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WOOD SORREL [BERMUDA BUTTERCUP] (<i>Oxalis [pos-caprae </i> syn. <i>O.] ceinua </i>), also called shamrock, is a common weed in Auckland gardens and orchards. ... It grows about 3 inches high, *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,117)* [NOTES: [1967 replaces WOOD SORREL with BERMUDA BUTTERCUP]]

noun. a box near a range (stove) for holding firewood

noun.

### wooden
  
verb. to flatten; to knock out, lay out  
[NOTES: Also wooden out.  ca1953 July W Cameron p.c. Used frequently, e:g: If you don't watch out for the door it'll wooden you.]  
> **LANCASTER** He'll wooden more of you if you scare him. *(1904,Sons o' Men,252)*  
> .. Battye picked up a piece of timber that had once done duty as a handle to a domestic article, and literally `woodened' Gibbons out. *(Dec 23, 1911,NZ Truth,339/5/3)*  
> ... a twig no bigger'n yer big finger flew back and woodened him out. *(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,31)*  
> **KIRK** [The block of ice] `woodened' him out, and he lay there quite flat. *(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,126)*  
> **SLATTER** `I gotta get away.'`Ya bloody piker! A man oughta wooden ya out. I spose you're tearin off to see some sheila if the truth's known.' *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43)*  
> **SLATTER** Woodened out the five-eighths with a straight arm tackle. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,172)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wooden</i> to hit, used by Frank S. Anthony's <i>Me and Gus. </i> <i>- wooden out</i> to punch somebody to the floor; eg `He took a swing at me and missed, then I woodened him out.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> **HULME** And some cunt woodens me. *(1986,Te Kaihau,35)*  
> Immediately after Hawke's Bay scored, and then Strang was woodened out for a few minutes with one on the chin. *(Feb 24, 1902,Feilding Star,2)*

### wooden aspro
  
noun. [humorous]

> **McGILL** <i>wooden aspro</i> a truncheon across the head, as encountered most often in prisons. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*

### wooden house
  
noun. the common usage in NZ for what would be called in most parts of the US frame house; and possibly more usuallytimber housein the UK

### wooden rose
  
noun. = wood rose q.v.  
See: -  
[NOTES: also wood flower]  
> **MOORE & IRWIN** <i>DACTYLANTHUS TAYLORI.</i> About 1850 Rev. Richard Taylor discovered this strange plant near Hikurangi ... A common practice is to remove the whole rhizome by boiling and scraping, so displaying the fluted end of the root, the so-called `wooden rose'. *(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,86)*  
> Although the Canterbury Museum no longer houses a botanical collection, it still has a box of those rather bizarre botanical specimens known as `wood flowers' or `wooden roses' retained for their curiosity rather than their scientific value. Wooden roses are the deformed roots of trees or shrubs, the deformation being caused by the attack of another plant, the root-parasite, Dactylanthus taylori. It is found most abundantly on the volcanic plateau in the centre of the North Island, but also in other parts of the same island. *(Feb 7, 1986,Press, The,18)* [NOTES: (Beverley McCulloch)]  
> **DAWSON** These `wooden roses', as they are called, are prized as curios. <i>Dactylanthus</i> is restricted to New Zealand ... *(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,93)*

### wooden spoon
  
noun.  
[NOTES: NZ variant of Eng term; booby prize]  
> Still I am far from undervaluing a degree if a man has deserved it;  but, seeing that so many numskulls have passed, just escaping the "wooden spoon," I attach no intrinsic value thereto. *(Jan 6, 1858,Wellington Independent,3)*  
> Those heroic souls who cut a corner by going through the Caversham tunnel deserve the Victoria wooden spoon for foolhardiness. *(Sep 27, 1873,Otago Daily Times,6)*  
> Private Armstrong wins the wooden spoon for the lowest score in the match. *(Jul 30, 1898,Timaru Herald,3)*  
> The result will not affect the Sole Cup, which has been won by Civil Service, but the match will serve as a "decider" for the claimant to the "wooden spoon." *(Jul 30, 1909,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> The meeting of the competitors for the wooden spoon of the senior championship - Selwyn and Wellington - produced a good even game, better to watch than its neighbour, Poneke v. Berhampore, at Kilbirnie. *(Jul 18, 1821,Evening Post,4)*  
> Victoria thus retains the tournament wooden spoon as well as the athletics wooden spoon. *(Apr 15, 1936,Evening Post,11)*

### wooden spooner
  
noun.  
> One expected better things from last year's premiers and not such good things from last season's wooden spooners or thereabouts. *(May 2, 1906,Otago Witness,60)*  
> Lastly, Melrose - the quondam champions - have succeeded in three years in holding both first and last postion [sic] on the list - champions, 1908;  wooden spooners, 1910. *(Aug 20, 1910,Evening Post,14)*  
> Week by week the championship table shows that the decision to split the senior teams into an A and B grade next season is a very wise one, for though the wooden spooners have on occasions put up startling performances against those teams which fight away for first place they do not maintain the high standard of play, and, being up against so great a lead of points, may become disheartened and casual about turning out to play. *(Jul 23, 1921,Evening Post,12)*  
> Sydenham, one of the wooden spooners, almost upended Old Boys in a very patchy game on Saturday. *(Jun 19, 1902,NZ Truth,18)*

### wooden tongue
  
noun. (? a general term for a cattle disease)  
> **McLEOD** Some years ago I was riding round one of the blocks when I saw a young steer so thin that you could have `cut your fingers on his spine'. When I got home I told the head shepherd to go and shoot it for the dogs, though there was little enough meat on it for dogs to eat. `Bring me back the head,' I said, `it looks to me as if it might have wooden tongue.' Wooden tongue is a disease which cattle can pick up through pricks or mouth / wounds; it can be spotted by swellings on the side of the face above the mouth. ... *(1980,Tussock Ranges,168)*

### woodener
  
noun. a blow

> Unfortunately for Terry, the Bandsman would occasionally pull up suddenly, fairly prop, in fact, and land a woodener on the scion of the much-sugared family. *(Jan 27, 1912,NZ Truth)*

noun.

noun.

### woodgrub
  
noun. huhu

> He drove the axe savagely into the rotten wood. It split lengthwise and fell apart, baring the wet sawdust tunnels of woodgrubs. He put the inch-long grubs on top of the chopping stump, their white wrinkled bodies curling blindly in the foreign sunlight; then spattered them one by one with the back of the axe. *(May 1956,Numbers,1/2/8)* [NOTES: Baxter]

### woodhen
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka wood hen, wood-hen; undefined Rail - weka; incl Cook's 'the hens'.]  
> The Hens, as we called them were a sort of Rail, which would stand & stare at us untill[sic] we knocked them down with a stick, so that at length it was accounted rather a descredit[sic] to have shot one. *(1773,Cook's Journals,2/787,1961)* [NOTES: 1773 Wales in Cook Journals (1961) 2/787]  
> The Wild fowl are Ducks, Shaggs, Cormorrants, Oyster Catchers or Sea pies, Water or wood Hens, which are something like our English Rails, these inhabet [sic] the Skirts of the Woods and feed upon the Sea beach they [sic] are very like a Common Hen and eat very well in a Pye or Fricasee. *(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/121,1961)*  
> The Wild fowl are Ducks, Shaggs, Cormorrants, Oyster Catchers or Sea pies, Water or wood Hens, which are something like our English Rails, these inhabet [sic] the Skirts of the Woods and feed upon the Sea beach they [sic] are very like a Common Hen and eat very well in a Pye or Fricasee. *(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/121,1961)*  
> **FORSTER** ... he had either observed one of the numerous wood-hens, which are brown ... *(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/156)*  
> **BEL;L** Some of the Officers who had been away shooting return'd with some Birds, that proved acceptable - though they were only Wood Hens <i>Ibid.</i>  48 - and in the space of two hours shot eleven Brace of Parrots, Wood Hens, & Curlews; - <i>Ibid.</i>) 50 The Best kinds were Ducks, Curlews, a bird very much resembling a Wood Cock & Sea Pies; - Shags, Wood Hens, & Parrots were in great abundance ... the Wood Hen very much resembles the Common Barn door Fowl in England *(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',45)*  
> **McNAB** [At Dusky Bay] Ducks, wood-hens, and various fowls they have procured in great plenty. <i>Ibid.</i> Nov 19, 1/179 Ducks, wood-hens, and various fowls they have procured [at Dusky Sound] in great plenty. Tea they made from the spruce and tea-trees. *(1908,Historical Records,1/179)* [NOTES: 1793 Nov 2 Captain Raven to Lieut.-Governor King in 1908  McNab Historical Records 1/179]  
> Milford [139] Haven is a wild romantic looking place - ... the woods are abundantly supplied with game, as woodhens, green birds, emus &c - these birds are of large size, ... and as they cannot fly, they are easily overtaken with dogs. n.b. check differences in cit. in actual journal *(1826,Boultbee Journal,1979)* [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 138-139 [check date - 1826, 1828 or c1835???]]  
> **SHEPHERD** He shot several ducks Red bills a wood hen and killed a hare seal. ... cockles Mussels & oisters are plentiful and excellent.[check spg???] *(1826,Journal)* [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 357]  
> The birds are not remarkably musical - the linnets are rather so; The Bell-Bird, is of a purple colour, with a white spot on the breast, shaped like a bell. The Saddle back, is a brown bird with a bright red spot on its back like a saddle, these two last species, are about the size of a thrush. There are green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, greenbirds and woodhens, which are birds of excellent flavour. *(1835,Boultbee Journal,104,1986)* [NOTES: c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 104[check date - 1826, 1828, c1835???]]  
> **DIEFFENBACH** 55. <i> Ocydromus Australis</i>. Strickl. ... Weka or Weka-weka of the natives of Cook's Strait, Wood-hen of the Settlers. <i>Dieffenbach. </> *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/197)* [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/197 55.]  
> **STEPHENS** Kaka, Wood Hen, and Pigeon ... *(1843,Journal and Letters (Stephens))* [NOTES: 1843 Samuel Stephens Journals and Letters (ATB:TS)]  
> **SHORTLAND** ... more or less convenient for catching eels or wekas; [fn] This bird is described by Captain Cook by the name of the water or woodhen. *(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,206)*  
> **BARNICOAT** The valley was full of wood-hens. *(1845,Journal,153)*  
> **WAKEFIELD**  *(1845,Adventure in NZ,ii/95)*  
> We have great varieties of birds here; there are pigeons, wood-hens, paradise ducks, in great varieties; ... *(Jul 20, 1848,Letters from Otago,15,1978)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** 163 <i>weka</i>, or wood-hen (<i>Ocydromus Australis</i>). This bird frequents the retired parts of the country, and especially the forests.<i>Ibid.</> 247 <i>Quail</i> abound to a remarkable degree throughout the fern and grass districts. <i>Wild hogs</i>, also, and the <i>Wood-hen</i>, called <i>Weka</i> by the natives, ... are numerous .. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The)*  
> Shot 2 pigeons and caught a woodhen. *(Mar 28, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958)*  
> Woodhens, blue whistling and paradise ducks, formed our chief food in the latter part of our expedition. *(Mar 1851,Lyttelton Times,7)*  
> **MACKIE** Our dog started a wood hen and numerous coveys of quails; ... *(Mar 18, 1853,Traveller under Concern,89,1973)*  
> **BUTLER** The night was warm and quiet, the silence only interrupted by the occasional cry of a wood-hen and the rushing of the river, ... *(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/54)*  
> **BUTLER** The wood hen is a wingless bird marked not wholly unlike a hen pheasant but with a short bobtail instead of a long one. *(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960)*  
> **HAAST** The woodhens were sometimes also a great nuisance *(1948,Julius Von Haast,215)* [NOTES: c1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 215]  
> **THOMSON** Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). *(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15)*  
> **MONEY** ... thanks to Howitt's lessons, we could get ducks, crows, and wood-hens in abundance). *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,30)*  
> **HUTTON** 84. OCYDROMUS AUSTRALIS. Sparrm. Wood Hen. Weka. ... 85. OCYDROMUS EARLI. Gray. Wood Hen. Weka. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,31)*  
> **PYKE** `We-ka! we-ka! we-ka!' [a's and e's-acute] Three times the plaintive cry of the `wood-hen' was heard. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/82,(1889,1974))*  
> Hdg] Notes on the New Zealand Wood-hens (Ocydromus). By Capt. F.W. Hutton, C.M.Z.S. *(1874,TrNZI,6/110)*  
> **HAAST** The principal inhabitants ... were a great number of Woodhens <i>(Ocydromus australis)</i> generally of large size ... <i>Ibid.</.> 98 ... greedily sucking the fat of the woodhens they are able to catch. *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,36)*  
> **PYKE** Weka is the native name of the Wood-hen, or, as it is sometimes called, the Maori-hen. *(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,51 fn)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Weka</i>, n. ... wood-hen. *(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,226)*  
> **MOFFATT** [I] stripped some bark off a cedar or totara tree [in Nelson], I am not sure which, built a maimai big enough to crawl into, managed to get a fire, caught and roasted a woodhen, boiled some flour in my pannikin, ... [c 1860]. <i>Ibid.</> 68 Next day [near Lake Rotoroa, Nelson] I caught some woodhens and enjoyed the first good meal for a week. I also saw a few ducks and heard some kiwi. *(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,61,1979)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Woodhen, n.</i> a name given to several birds of New Zealand of the Rail family, and of the genus <i>Ocydromus;</i> some of them are called by the Maori name of  <i>Weka</i> (q.v.). The species are - Black Woodhen - <i>Ocydromus fuscus</i>, Du Bus.; Maori name, <i>Weka-pango. </i> -->Brown W. - <i>O. earli,</i> Gray. -->Buff W. - <i>O. australis</i>, Gray; called also <i>Weka. </> North-Island W. - <i>O. brachypterus,</i> Buller; called also <i>Weka. </i> -->South Island W. - Same as <i>Bluff W.</i>; see above. *(1898,Austral English,516)*  
> It is a peculiar fact that no wood-hens (wekas) were seen ... by our party. *(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/17)* [NOTES: (Hamilton Poor Knights)]  
> **COWAN** The <i>weka</i> is a wingless bird, grey brown in colour, about the size of an ordinary fowl, and is often called the wood-hen, or `Maori-hen'. `As cunning as a Maori hen' is a familiar New Zealand bush simile. *(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 63)*  
> **REISCHEK** I shot at him, and the dog brought me a giant wood-hen *(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,34,1933)*  
> <i>Wood hen</i>. Still in use but more frequently called <i>weka. W.h.,</i> however, may survive, as it is often used figuratively. `Running like a <i>w.h.'</i> (being very busy), `sheep no bigger than <i>w.hs.,</i>' etc. Their feathers make the best pipe cleaners in the world, and <i>w.h</i> (1951, weka] oil was excellent for anything, metal or leather. `The silence only interrupted by the occasional sharp cry of a wood- hen.'--Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p. 53. *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **AYSON** Woodhens (Maori hens we called them [in Dunedin c1850s]) were also very numerous. *(1937,Pioneering in Otago,31)*  
> **BEATTIE** Weka - the familiar `woodhen' of Captain Cook, and `Maori hen' of the settlers. *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64)*  
> <i>weka,</i> a ground-bird (Ocydromus sp.), wood-hen, Maori hen; it has wings, but too small for flight, but it can dodge and run well; and whilst its long toes are unwebbed it swims well, and voluntarily takes to the water to investigate distant isles in the great lakes: a most inquisitive and amusing bush-companion, and a courageous mother. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Weka</i>, n.<i> Woodhen, gallirallus australis</i> and <i>G. hectori. </> *(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,481)*  
> <i>wood-hen</i> (see <i>weka.</i>) *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),447)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Woodhen .. .. weka .. .. <i>Gallirallus </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WEKA <i>Gallirallus australis Other names:</i> Woodhen, Woodrail. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,102)*  
> <i>woodhen n. </i>see weka. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>woodhen n.</i> See <i>weeka </i> *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> **HENDERSON** The little blue ornament remained very dear to her, very close and comforting, among the scrub, manuka, and the ancient remembering cries from the stalking brown woodhens. *(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,18)*  
> Those great crashing bush-drives, when one tree was aimed to hit another partly-cut tree, which in turn smashed into another half-severed tree, and so on smashing and roaring downhill to the gully's bottom, ... the bush snails dying, the woodhens confounded, the brown pebbled creek and all its reflections from the beginning shrinking back in dismay *(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,12)* [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]  
> <i> Weka Gallirallus australis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ...<i> North Island Weka G.a. greyi </i> BULLER, 1888). <i>Western Weka G.a. australis </i> (SPARRMAN, 1786).<i> Buff Weka G.a. hectori </i> (HUTTON, 1873).<i> Stewart Island Weka G.a. scotti</i> (OGILVIE-GRANT, 1905). OTHER NAMES:  <i>Woodhen, woodrail. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,168)*  
> Not much noise either, except for the woodhens calling up the creek ... *(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,32)* [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]  
> <i>wood-hen NZ </i>= WEKA. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 550]]  
> **GRACE** "An' that fussy old wood-hen'll be in, first thing tomorrow, asking for 'the memento of my poor dear 'usband'." *(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,113)*  
> **THE MIXER** He's like a woodhen, quick to dodge when a list is put about. *(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,59)*  
> In the extensive Alpine region, which lies between the Waiau and Haast, the latter of which marks the scope of the present paper northwards, some 10 or 12 years since kakapos and kiwis were almost as plentiful as many of us can remember the weks, or woodhen, to have been in the open country. *(Aug 27, 1891,Otago Witness,15)*  
> No Maoris were to be found;  but the tracks of wild dogs were seen on the sand, and these wretched pests had killed all the woodhens in the neighbourhood. *(Jul 16, 1918,Grey River Argus,3)*  
> **DAVIES** Wood-hens or waikas [sic], are a greatstand-by in the bush. Their cry can beimitated, and a man knowing theirlanguage and character can catch themeasily. They call each other by name,pronounced "weeka," the latter syllablebeing shrill and prolonged, an octavehigher than the first note. *(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,263)*

### woodhen
  
noun. ?=kiwi (a mistake for weka)  
> **HARPER** At night, for supper, we had a fresh delicacy, a roasted Kiwi, locally called a `wood-hen,' rather like a hen pheasant, one of the surviving specimens of the Apteryx, or `wingless' bird, in New Zealand. It has a rudimentary wing, of one joint only, but is a ground bird, and if well cooked, so as to get rid of its superabundant oil, by no means uneatable. *(Nov 4, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,48,1914)*

### woodhen
  
noun. .  
[NOTES: weka, fig, indicating busyness or plain-ness]  
> <i>Wood hen</i> .Still in use but more frequently called <i>weka. W.h</i>., however, may survive, as it is often used figuratively. `Running like a <i>w.h.</i>' (being very busy), `sheep no bigger than<i> w.hs</i>.,' etc *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> Defendant published a statement in the Te Whare Kura, which was held to ridicule the bishop, by alleging that he was "a woodhen in peacock's feathers." Yes,entered under the 'fig' headword. *(Sep 28, 1912,Hawera & Normanby Star,5)*

### woodhen fat
  
noun.  
[NOTES: see woodhen oil]  
> **HENDERSON** The Chaffeys recommended woodhen fat to everyone with great enthusiasm. *(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,70)*

### woodhen oil
  
noun.  
[NOTES: weka oil]  
> There is an oil that the people make here, which is said to be a certain cure for rheumatism and similar complaints.   It is made from the roots of flax (phormium tenax) and woodhen oil. *(Aug 26, 1870,North Otago Times,2)*  
> PURE WOODHEN OIL for Sale at HARRIS' Variety Store, Hardy street. *(May 10, 1894,Colonist, The,3)*  
> "What is woodhen oil?" he asked counsel ..   "It is made from the fat of the weka and is regarded as a cure for rheumatism," he continued ... *(Jan 28, 1929,Evening Post,8)*  
> Another weka stew graced the festive board at dinner-time that day, but as the repast too frequently resolved itself into a vulgar struggle between the repasters and the defunct birds for the possession of a gutta-percha-like fragment of flesh tied on to a leg bone, the incidents of the orgie [sic] are subjects scarcely meet for publications among these disjointed memoirs. *(Mar 17, 1896,Mataura Ensign,2)*  
> A MIXTURE made from the roots of flax and woodhen oil is said to be a cure for rheumatism. *(Sep 9, 1870,Colonist (Nelson),3)*  
> PURE WOODHEN OIL for Sale at HARRIS' Variety Store, Hardy street. *(May 10, 1894,Colonist, The)*  
> The rock is granite or gneiss, and it is covered with an elastic mossy soil, not very favorable to the pedestrian, under such conditions as those on which it was traversed by Mr Beverly, even with a liberal use of seal's or woodhen's oil as a substitute for Warren's blacking. *(Dec 21, 1867,Otago Daily Times,5)*

### woodie
  
noun. a wooden-bodied car built on an old chassis  
[NOTES: (CC Ransom notes: US WOODIE was the old station wagon that had a varnished wooden body. The first ones were all like that and they became woodies when they became rare.) -->ca1964 a student's answer to a question on local English in the University Entrance English paper that year included: `A woody is a wooden bodied car that we build up.' -]

### woodiness disease
  
noun. a viral disease of passion fruit.  
> J of Ag March 15. 169 Woodiness disease: the condition referred to as 'woodiness' is due to a virus. Affected fruit rinds are hard, distorted and often have characteristic cracks and eruption. *(1943)*

### wooding
  
noun. getting firewood etc. for a ship  
[NOTES: cf. wood v.]  
> **J. BISCOE** I intended to have gone through Decastreaux Channel understanding it to be a convenient & safe place for wooding, &c. ... *(Oct 10, 1831,Journal)* [NOTES: 1831 Oct 10 J.Biscoe Journal in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 414]

### wooding and watering
  
verb.  
> **FORSTER** This situation ... was particularly adapted to the conveniency of wooding and watering ... *(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/126)*  
> the Cooper was sent on shore [at Dusky Bay] to sett up the Casks - and a Party was sent Brewing Spruce Beer. Some were employed Wooding & Watering. *(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',45)*

### woodlouse
  
noun. a terrestrial isopod  
> If anybody ... has occupied himself with farming he will be only too well acquainted with what are known as `wireworms'; while anybody who grows flowers in pots has had troubles with the little animals called `woodlice [<i>Oniscus</i> sp.].' *(1893,TrNZI,25/199)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Papapa ... 4. Armadillium vulgare, wood-louse, slater</i>. [so 1957/259] *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,302)*  
> **DUFF** During the recent flood in this valley rushes and seeded cocksfoot were black with woodlice which had climbed to escape the rising water. *(Feb 11, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,19,1961)*  
> **NATUSCH** What we call slaters or woodlice the Americans call nowbugs;in one part of England they are `tiggy-hogs' and in another `bibble-bugs'. *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,19)*

### woodpecker
  
noun.  
> **FALLA et al.** CHATHAM ISLAND WARBLER <i>Gerygone albofrontata Local name: </i>Woodpecker. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,212)*

### woodrail
  
noun.  
> **FALLA et al.** WEKA<i> Gallirallus australis Other names</i>: Woodhen, Woodrail. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,102)*  
> <i>Weka Gallirallus australis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ... <i>North Island Weka G.a. greyi </i> BULLER, 1888).<i> Western Weka G.a. australis </i> (SPARRMAN, 1786). <i>Buff Weka G.a. hectori </i> (HUTTON, 1873). <i>Stewart Island Weka G.a. scotti</i> (OGILVIE-GRANT, 1905). OTHER NAMES: <i>Woodhen, woodrail. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,168)*  
> What is the <i>rail ... Weka</i> also called wood-rail or woodhen *(Oct 14, 1986,University Challenge Massey/Canterbury)*

### woodroof
  
noun. <i>Asperula perpusilla </i>  
>  *(1869,TrNZI,1/)*

### woodser
  
noun.  
> **BENNETT** `A clumsy woodser' is clear in its general meaning, though its origin is obscure; <i>wood</i> is often, however, used disparagingly. *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,92)*

### wood-slab
  
adjective.  
> This small cottage, with its wood-slab roof.. was the love nest to which he had brought his wife. *(Dec 14, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,35)*

### woodslips
  
noun.  
> <i>Woodslips-</i> Pieces of wood built into masonry joints instead of plugging. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,131)*

### woodwool slab
  
noun.  
> <i>Woodwool slab- </i>Wood shavings, mixed with cement and compressed to from a slab. Sometimes used in thermal insulation. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,131)*

### woody
  
noun.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. *(1972,Folkgames of Children,174)*

### woody wool
  
noun. a fungal infection, mycotic dermatitis, in sheep skin and wool.  
> J of Ag June 20. 411 Apart from any consideration of the effect of prolonged dampness on the fleece, such as bacterial staining or even mycotic dermatitis ('woody wool') and on the animal's general health, it means a needless waste of feed for the sole purpose of warming and drying the skin and wool. *(1938)*

### woofer, wwoofer
  
noun. a worker with WWOOF (q.v.)  
> There is also a sprinkling of WOOFers (Workers on Organic Farms) - travellers from Holland, America, Austria and Germany who help tend the gardens in return for board. *(Mar 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,5)*  
> Se he went overseas and went "wwooffing", being a willing volunteer on organic farms. .. A couple of Wellington organic growers take in wwoofers, he says. *(Sep 2, 1999,Evening Post,11)* [NOTES: check spelling]  
> [The residents] include a mobile family of five, passing through by house bus from Nelson, as well as travelling "wwoofers" - willing workers on organic farms. Wwoofers are required to work about three to four hours daily for their board and lodging. *(Jul 22, 2000,Evening Post,29)*  
> Pam often shares her skills with Willing Workers on Organic Farms, or WOOFers, as they're commonly called. In return for board and food, WOOFers do a few hours' gardening or farm work daily. *(Aug 30, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),Inside & Out Supplement : 7)*

### woofter
  
verb.  
> Another with a starring role in this MMP-distorted government's law and order policy is Harre, wooftering away with the fairies on Section 59 which gives parents the rights to use "reasonable force" when disciplining a child. *(Oct 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C6)*

### woofterish
  
adjective.  
> <i>Woofterish on Aids</i> [heading] Of all your editorial comments ... surely the effort on Aids and the Bishop of Christchurch takes the biscuit for wooly [sic] woofterism. *(Dec 30, 1988,Dominion,8)*  
> By banning the woofterish gloves-onscrapping,.. the Minister may wellinspire a resurgence of the bare-knuckleclassicism New Zealanders once held dear. *(May 22, 1993,Evening Post,5)*

### wook wooks
  
noun. a local or boyhood variant =woop woops, reported by Turner (1966). (But cf woop-woops at cit boohai) -  
> **TURNER** <i>Up the boo-ay</i> means `altogether wrong'. This seems to me a commoner meaning for boo-ay than `backblocks', `remote rural districts', mentioned by Keith Sinclair, in the <i>Pelican History of New Zealand,</i> with suggested derivation from the settlement at Puhoi. Since the backblocks are already called the <i>woop woops</i> (the form given by Partridge who says it is mainly restricted to New South Wales: I know the word but think of it as <i>wook wooks</i>), the sticks, the <i>tea tree</i> and <i>the cactus</i> some semantic differentiation is not impossible. It must be pointed out that my meaning for <i>up the boo-ay</i> is already supplied by <i>up the chute</i>, however. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,122)*

### wool away
  
exclamation. a call made to pickers-up by shearers about to send a shorn sheep down the port-hole or requiring pieces, bellies or locks to be swept away before a fleece is completely shorn.  
> **McKEE WRIGHT** `All aboard! All aboard!' is the cry./ They're a ripping lot of shearers in the shed;/ ... /`Wool away! Wool away!' is the cry/ And the merry game of busting is begun!/ They're going sheep and sheep, for Big Mick will do or die,/ And the fleecy boys are kept upon the run./ ... /[fn] In old-time sheds these calls indicated the beginning and of a run. *(1897,Station Ballads)* [NOTES: 1897 McKee Wright Station Ballads `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33]  
> <i>Wool away!</i>-The shearer's call to the fleece picker to take the fleece off the board. *(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15)*  
> **NEWTON** The call of `wool away' had lagging fleecies dashing to rescue fleeces before the shearer would be out with his next sheep. *(1949,High Country Days,53)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** Is the ringer all set for two-fifty day - / `Wool away, and a bag for guts!'/ *(1950,Farm & Station Verse,185)* [NOTES: 1949 Stronach `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 185]  
> **BOWEN** `WOOL AWAY.' When the fleece has not been cleared off the board by the `fleeco' and is in the shearer's way, a shearer will make this call. So may I also, having come to the end of the textbook on shearing, say to all readers WOOL AWAY! *(1956,Wool Away!,158)*  
> The shearer's call for the fleece-o to clear wool from the board. *(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)* [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]  
> WOOL-AWAY: The shearers' call for the Fleece-o to clear wool from the board. *(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20)*  
> **SUTHERLAND TAYLOR** .. he felt the anger of a fleeco was directed at him for getting stuck in when the shearer cried, `Wool away!' and disdainfully kicked aside the ungathered fleece *(1981,Sunrise,46)*  
> **CHARLES** When a shearer finished a sheep he would call, `Wool away, Jack!' or when his pen of sheep was getting empty ... he would yell, `Sheep ho, Joe! *(1981,Black Billy Tea,72)*  
> <i>Wool-away!</i>: Call make when a fleece has not been cleared off the shearing board and is in the shearer's way. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> Even more excited hooting and hollering can be heard from the shearers when one of their number gets ahead of the rousie and has to yell `woolaway', almost always with a glint of triumph in his/her eyes. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,32)*  
> <i>wool away:</i> The call of the shearer who wants the picker up to carry away a fleece. This has been done after each sheep, and fleeces are not left lying around on the floor while another sheep is being shorn. *(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/29)* [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]  
> WRIGHT in Woodhouse (1950) NZ Farm & Station Verse 22 Wool away! Wool away is the cry ... *(1897)*  
> NEWTON High Country Days 53 The call of 'wool away' had lagging fleecies dashing ... *(1949)*  
> MEEK Station Days in Maoriland 84 In a New Zealand shearing shed, when the fleece has been shorn from a sheep, the shearer cries 'Wool Away!' And he ripped and he tore and he clipped and he swore, and he snorted 'wool-away!' *(1952)*  
> BOWEN Wool Away! [title]. *(1955)*  
> SINCLAIR Boot in the Stirrup 6 This was not easy when I was working in the shearing sheds, because the shouts of 'Sheepho' or 'Wool Away' had a very dampening effect on thoughts of true love. [wool-away] *(1973)*  
> WOOL AWAY!   TAR! *(Jun 24, 1916,Observer (Auckland),1)*

### wool away
  
exclamation. the call to the fleece picker when a fleece has not been cleared off the shearing board and is in the shearer's way

### wool bale
  
noun. a fabric-covered bale of wool, usu. a. a bale of wool (cf bale n, fadge) occas. B. =wool pack, the empty jute or fabric receptacle) a. 1;  
> **PAUL** Remember to make the doors wide enough to admit a wool-bale packed. *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90)*  
> My employment was chiefly in packing the woolbales which is very heavy work. You put from 90 to 120 fleeces in a bail which way [sic] from 300 to 350 lbs. It is hard to press the wool into so small a compass. We sheared some 850 sheep to-day. *(Nov 24, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,67,1950)*  
> **BUTLER** When we got down to the wool-shed we lit a tallow candle, and having stuck it in an old bottle we sat down upon the wool bales and began to smoke. A wool-shed is a roomy place, built somewhat on the same plan as a cathedral, with aisles on either side full of pens for the sheep, a great nave, at the upper end of which the shearers work, and a further space for wool sorters and packers. *(1872,Erewhon,Ch. II)* [NOTES: [here 1901 edn in Penguin Oct 1935 p.24]]  
> **KENNAWAY** [He] was taken down to town on top of the wool-bales on his dray. *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,167)*  
> **MANSFIELD** He walked onto the wharves, past the wool-bales, past the loungers and the loafers to the extreme end of the wharves. *(1913,Stories,133,1984 Alpers)*  
> <i>Wool bale, wool pack</i>.- Becomes a<i> bale of wool </i> when filled and pressed and a <i>fadge</i> when loosely filled. (C.) *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **MEEK** WOOLBALE: A jute pack in which the wool is baled. Also called a `woolpack'. *(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111)*  
> **ANDERSON** Kath was sitting on a bale of wool beside Mr Smith. <i>Ibid.</i> 154 A detective was looking round a wool and skin buyer's.. and happened to notice the Algidus brand on two bales. *(Sep 23, 1924,River Rules My Life, A,88)* [NOTES: 1983 edition]  
> **MARSH** A heap of folded wool bales that had fallen across the floor.. had not been disturbed. *(1945,Died in the Wool,00)* [NOTES: 1948 edition : pages missing]

### wool bale
  
noun. a wool-pack filled with wool.  
> in MUNDY (1857) Our Antipodes 31 Long caravans of drays .. laden with wool bales ... *(1852)*  
> PAUL Letters from Canterbury 89 Remember to make the doors wide enough to admit a wool-bale packed. *(1857)*  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ ! 57 The 26-horse pack train took out the year's wool clip pressed into 'pockets', much smaller packs than the normal wool bale. [wool-bale] *(1956)*

### wool bale
  
noun.  
> **JONES** Woolbales Sewing twine Grave stone ... Files, for crosscut and handsaw *(1919,Samuel Butler,1/82)* [NOTES: 1860 Oct Butler's list `Things for the Dray to bring up' in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler 1/82]  
> We slept on one wool bale and had two over us. *(Jan 13, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,118,1950)*  
> **KENNAWAY** We stretched the dray-cloth, or tarpaulin, full-spread over the loaded dray, pinning it to the ground all round, spreading heaps of wool-bales which it had brought, underneath the wheels to lie on. *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,72)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Wool bale.</i> A jute pack into which the wool is baled. Also called a `woolpack'. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*

### wool baron
  
noun. a sheepfarmer with a large flock and property.  
> Lyttleton Times Dec 14 (Fildes Cn 621/34) We are in the Cheviot country, the land of a famous 'wool-baron' of his day ... *(1916)*  
> J of Ag May. 40 Sheep farmers were wool barons in every sense of the word. *(1982)*  
> But the wool barons are assured, "there is still a good market in Germany." *(Oct 2, 1920,NZ Truth,2)*

### wool bin
  
noun. a container into which wool is sorted in a wool-shed  
> **PERRY** The wool bins are hinged, and can thus be swung against the wall when shearing is finished. The pieces bins are collapsible, and therefore able to be removed when not in use. *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,20)*  
> <i>Bin</i>.- Open compartments like stalls in a stable, where wool is stacked by classers until it is pressed. `We next inspected the wool tables, to which two boys were incessantly bringing armfuls of rolled up fleeces ... pronounced in a moment to which bin they belonged.'--S.L., p. 33. *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> <i>Bin</i>.- Open compartments like stalls in a stable, where wool is stacked by classers until it is pressed. `We next inspected the wool tables, to which two boys were incessantly bringing armfuls of rolled up fleeces ... pronounced in a moment to which bin they belonged.'--S.L., p. 33. *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Wool bin</i>. Open compartments in the woolshed where the classed wool is held until pressed. or store. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> .. wool bins, in which the wool, after being sorted, is classified ... *(May 28, 1879,Star, The,3)*  
> Went to sleep, and after a little while woke up and hards a footstep, about half past 11, near the wool bins. *(Dec 24, 1887,Timaru Herald,3)*  
> The fire, which appears to have started near the wool bins, broke out suddenly and cannot be accounted for, as no persons had been on the premises after 6 p.m. *(Mar 6, 1899,Southland Times,3,3)*  
> Here a large table is fitted up for this purpose, and near by are six wool bins for placing different kinds of wool in. *(Sep 30, 1903,Ashburton Guardian,2)*

### Wool Blacks
  
noun. New Zealand's international shearing team.  
> Despite vocal home crowd support, unfamiliar sheep breeds and experienced opposition, the "Wool Blacks" prevailed. *(Aug 26, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13)*

### Wool Board
  
noun.  
> The Wool Board's electoral committee, of which the Hawke's Bay farmer was chairman, had passed a vote of no confidence in Agriculture Minister Duncan McIntyre... When wool prices plummeted in 1972 the Wool Board proposed the radical move of taking over control of its buying and selling. *(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5)*

### wool book
  
noun. a book in which all details of baled wool are recorded and kept.  
> STUDHOLME Te Waimate 130 The 'sheep-oh' (penner-up) .. in addition to filling up the catching pens .. weighed the bales and recorded them .. in the wool book. *(1940)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 92 wool book: the responsibility of the pressers. In it they mark down each woolbale's serial number, its weight, and the style of wool in it. *(1981)*  
> <i>Wool book</i>.- The pressers write down in it the number, description and weight of each bale as they press it. *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **STUDHOLME** In addition to filling up the catching pens, [the sheep-o] weighed the bales and recorded them, with their descriptions in the wool book. *(1940,Te Waimate,112)*  
> Prices furnished on application for .. WOOL BOOKS ... *(Mar 14, 1898,Poverty Bay Herald,4)*  
> A reward is offered for the return of a lost wool book. *(Apr 20, 1906,Marlborough Express,3)*

### wool book
  
noun.

### wool brand
  
noun.  
See: also brander

### wool brand
  
noun. a stencilled name of a farm or station and the serial number of a woolbale.  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 92 wool brand: usually the station name, stencilled on one end and one side of each bale, together with its number. *(1981)*  
> <i>Wool brand</i>.-Usually the name of the station now, but in old days frequently the owner's name or intials, e.g. Acland is the Mount Peel brand and C.O.T. (Charles Obins Torlesse, the first owner) the Birch Hill brand. The brand and number are put on with a <i>stencil</i>. wing up to top bins. (C.) *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Wool brand</i>. The name of each station, branded on the full bale. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> The Inspector stated, that on the 14th February he had found these 5000 sheep in Mr. Moore's paddock, at Glenmark, and they were infected with scab, some had lost their wool brand from disease, and the rest were branded with defendant's registered brand. *(Mar 12, 1859,Lyttelton Times,4)*  
> True it is that after the last shearing four shorn sheep bearing the KD wool brand found their way into my flock. *(Feb 1, 1862,Lyttelton Times,4)*

### wool broker
  
noun.  
> The bales are stencilled with the owners's brand, the bale number, and a description of the wool, before being despatched to a `wool broker'. *(1974,NZ Agriculture,66)*  
> <i>wool-broker NZ & Austral.</i> company etc. that prepares a wool-grower's wool-clip for sale. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### wool cheque
  
noun.

### wool cheque
  
noun. [Rural] [fig. as in the cream cheque] a sheep farmer's income.  
> Now we want them [the sheep] to balance the books with the wool cheque, so we have to get wool weights up. *(Aug 9, 1984,NZ Farmer,18)*  
> **SCOTT** Our interest and repayment of principal came easily out of the wool cheque. *(1953,Breakfast at Six,180)*  
> He pointed out that if the wool cheques were not honoured the firm would be ruined. *(Apr 16, 1889,Southland Times,2)*  
> Writing of shearing, prices of wool ought to gladden the hearts of farmers this year, and if they keep up for the next few months it will mean a wool cheque to New Zealand such as she has never had before. *(Nov 16, 1904,Otago Witness,20)*  
> Just the ticket for high-prices lambs and big wool cheques. *(Jan 8, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,4)*  
> THE SMALLER WOOL CHEQUES. *(Aug 9, 1926,Evening Post,11)*  
> **SCANLAN** Daddy was quite pleased with his wool cheque this year. *(1938,Quest of Life,397)*

### wool classer
  
noun.  
See: shed classer  
[NOTES: aka woolclasser, wool-classer]  
> **OVERTON** The woolclasser and I were sleeping in a small cottage on the edge of the bush about fifty yards from the men's hut. *(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,34)*  
> SWANTON Notes on NZ 96 There is the wool classer with his assistant rollers. *(1892)*  
> BISLEY Practical Treatise 37 .. unless a competent wool-classer be engaged, our advice is to remove only the manifestly dirt-stained portions from the fleece. *(1897)*  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 350 Shearing was in progress; my duty as wool-classer obliged me to be in the shed before five ... *(1921)*
