> MAF Agric. Glossary 61 Wool classer: Person trained to group together wools of a similar type. *(1982)*  
> SQUIRE A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms 42 Wool classer - one who has the ability to type wools into even marketable lines. Broker classer, shed classer, or owner classer registrations are obtainable. *(1991)*  
> BARDSLEY Land Girls 62 Her interest in the station continued after the war and in 1969, at the age of seventy, she gained certification as a wool-classer. *(2000)*  
> **NEAVE** One [grave] is that of a man who drank himself to death, and the other was that of a woolclasser who was drowned in the Otematata River. *(1980,Land of Munros, The,55)*  
> **SWANTON** There is the wool-classer with his assistant rollers who number five or six. *(1892,Notes on NZ,96)*  
> **HAYES** Oct., J. Crawford (wool-classer), J. Ansell (brickwork), ... . *(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,154)* [NOTES: 1897 Normanvale, Canterbury, Wages Book in 1978 Hayes Toss Of a Coin 154]  
> **SMITH** On all large stations ... a wool-classer is employed at shearing-time to `get-up' the clip. *(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,80)*  
> <i>Wool-classer</i>.- Besides classing the wool, he is responsible (at least in my own sheds) for the work of the shed hands - pressers, rollers, and fleece-pickers. `Is there any advantage in classing each fleece?'--G.P. W. *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> Most of the large sheep station owners have their wool classed at shearing time by an experienced wool classer. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,709)*  
> **MARSH** ... a wool-classer, three boys, two gardeners, a cowman and a station cook. *(1945,Died in the Wool,28)*  
> <i>Wool classer</i>: Person trained to group together wools of a similar type. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> **RICHARDS** Otoro was George's first contract shed. ... But all his shed hands, shearers and his wool classer had been with him before. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,94)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Wool Classer.</i> Certificated person who determines the class of [check cit. incomplete???] *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> In sheds shearing fine wool (from Merino, Halfbred or Corriedale sheep) a woolclasser is employed to make lines of wool; that is, to define the differences between wool from each sheep after it has been shorn and skirted (dirty and shorter edges removed) by the shedhands, and to group similar wools together in baled lines for ease of sale and subsequent processing. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31)*  
> The first he knew of anything wrong was when the wool-classer sang out "All aboard." *(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,20)*  
> In these bigger sheds would be a dozen shearers, an expert classer who, besides classing all the wool, manages the internal running of the shed, two floor boys, and men for baling and pressing. *(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(6) : 23)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** Fred Stuthridge came as a wool-classer in 1904 and was paid aGBP1 for each 1000 fleeces with 25/- a week extra for wool-rolling. He was helped by one roller at 25/- a week. *(1982,Blue Cliffs,89)*  
> We omitted to give a list of the trades of the emigrants by the 'Matilda Wattenbach' .. 1 hat manufacturer, 5 cabinet makers, 4 sawyers, 1 wool classer, 2 miners, 8 butchers, 17 carpenters ... *(Sep 20, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> A passenger named Turnbull, a woolclasser, was removed to the hospital. *(Mar 27, 1900,Wanganui Chronicle,2)*  
> The board accordingly adjourned its decision in regard to an appeal on behalf of a woolclasser to the date mentioned. *(Jun 26, 1918,Evening Post,8)*  
> When war broke out he was employed at Tirau as a woolclasser. *(Sep 16, 1944,Evening Post,8)*

### wool classer
  
noun. a woolshed wool-sorter who typing wools into even marketable lines.  
See: shed classer  
[NOTES: this had all dog citations, now moved to dog related headword (wool classer)]  
> NGAWHIKA Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting 94 By now I had also given Bear to Mike Hutchins who had the misfortune of having his main dogs shot by a trigger-happy cockie with a severe grudge against canine wool-classers. [wool-classer] *(1996)*

### wool classer
  
noun. a dog that worries or bites sheep.  
See: meat converter  
> MCGREGOR A Dog's Life 66 Biting dogs are frowned upon by all sheep men. Those that like to taste the sheep are known as 'wool classers', or as one that likes its mutton on the hoof. *(1980)*  
> SWANTON Notes on NZ 96 There is the wool classer with his assistant rollers. *(1892)*  
> BISLEY Practical Treatise 37 .. unless a competent wool-classer be engaged, our advice is to remove only the manifestly dirt-stained portions from the fleece. *(1897)*  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 350 Shearing was in progress; my duty as wool-classer obliged me to be in the shed before five ... *(1921)*  
> MAF Agric. Glossary 61 Wool classer: Person trained to group together wools of a similar type. *(1982)*  
> SQUIRE A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms 42 Wool classer - one who has the ability to type wools into even marketable lines. Broker classer, shed classer, or owner classer registrations are obtainable. *(1991)*  
> BARDSLEY Land Girls 62 Her interest in the station continued after the war and in 1969, at the age of seventy, she gained certification as a wool-classer. *(2000)*  
> **TURNER** There is the <i>Sunday dog</i> who declines to work in a difficulty, the <i>woolclasser</i> who bites at the sheep, and the <i>sooner</i> who would sooner rest than work. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,146)*

### wool classer
  
noun.

### wool classer
  
noun.  
> <i> woolclasser n.</i> a grader of wool, whence woolclassing, the art of grading wool. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>woolclasser n.</i> a grader of wool; whence woolclassing, art of grading wool. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> <i>wool-classer NZ & Austral.</i> person who grades wool; <i>wool-classing n. </i> *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 550]]

### wool classer
  
noun.

### wool classing
  
noun. the sorting and grading of fleeces and wool after shearing

### wool classing
  
noun. the sorting of wool into types or classes, carried out at a wooltable.  
[NOTES: aka woolclassing, wool-classing]  
> MEREDITH Adventuring in Maoriland 86 I want to go in for wool-classing. *(1875)*  
> PERRY Sheep Farming 119 Wool classing is the placing of whole fleeces, after skirting, into different classes or grades according to the condition, quality, and strength and length of fibre. *(1922)*  
> STAPLES Cooks & Shepherds Come Away 118 I'm very grateful to the table-hand who taught me all I know about wool-classing. *(1964)*  
> **MEREDITH** ... I want to go in for wool-classing. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,86,1935)*  
> ... satisfactory arrangements were made for ... classes in both Machine-Shearing and Wool-Classing at Glenmark Station ... *(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,11)*  
> **MACDONALD** When the flock is small this is all the wool classing that is necessary. *(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,69)*  
> **PERRY** A boy or man to five shearers is sufficient for picking up the fleeces, but the operations at the wool table will be described in the chapter on wool-classing... <i>Ibid.</i> 119  The farmer should be careful to distinguish between `wool classing' and `wool sorting'. Wool sorting is a process employed in manufacture, ... In wool classing groups of qualities are dealt with, generally representing the fine and coarse wools of a flock, and lines are made according to condition (i.e. amount of grease), soundness, and length of staple, ... <i>Wool classing</i> is the placing of <i>whole</i> fleeces, after skirting, into different classes or grades according to the condition, quality, and strength and length of fibre *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,83)*  
> **SMITH** Wool-classing is the art of preparing the wool in the shearing-shed so that it is placed on the market in its most attractive style, to meet the requirements of buyers. *(1922,Sheep & Wool Industry,80)*  
> WANTED - Management of Shed, Station, Sheep, or Wool-classing, Working Partner, or Station on terms, by an experienced man;  good reference. *(Oct 16, 1888,Daily Telegraph,7)*  
> WANTED - Management of Shed, Wool Classing, or any responsible situation on station by experienced man;  good references. *(Nov 19, 1891,Daily Telegraph,3)*  
> DOES WOOL-CLASSING PAY? *(Feb 24, 1909,Otago Witness,22)*  
> A meeting to consider the formation of woolclassing classes at Leeston was held at the A. and P. Association Room on Tuesday evening. *(Jul 25, 1930,Ellesmere Guardian,6)*  
> Station owners can rely upon securing theservices of a party of young women intheir shearing sheds, and if they hadenough knowledge of wool-classing, wool-rolling and sheep-shearing machinery,they would be of material assistance tothem. *(Feb 23, 1918,Wairarapa Daily Times,4)*  
> **VANCE** For years he [sc. A.W. Aldridge] did the wool classing at Buccleugh Station. *(1976,Bush, Bullocks & Boulders,25)*  
> Who wants to learn wool-classing orfarming.. when we can all get cushy jobsbuilding roads. *(Jul 19, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/48 : 275)*

### wool classing
  
noun. (figurative use) the attacking of sheep by dogs  
> J of Ag Jan/Feb 13 The command to bring a heading dog smoothly and slowly onto sheep - not tearing in and doing a spot of woolclassing. *(1984)*

### wool clip
  
noun.

### wool clip
  
noun. the total wool shorn from a farm, station, or mob; or, occas., a sheep;  
See: clip  
[NOTES: woolclip, wool-clip; shortened to CLIP (CLIP in NZ from at least 1864 Revans to Chapman 230)]  
> ... growers ... very often obtain at their doors more for their wool-clip than they would realise in London ... *(1892,NZ Official Handbook,124)*  
>  *(1899,Department of Agriculture Report,11)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]  
> Moreover, in every wool clip, wherever it is classed, there are always some fleeces which cannot be placed in any of the set lines without spoiling its evenness. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710)*  
> <i>woolclip n.</i> annual amount of wool shorn. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>woolclip n.</i> annual amount of wool shorn. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> **FERNANDEZ** At the dinner table, ... Andrew brought up the subject of his wool-clip. *(1973,Tussock Fever,160)*  
> **RICHARDS** John was busy drinking to the success of his woolclip. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,68)*  
> <i>wool-clip</i> yield of wool, esp. annual. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 550]]  
> With the outbreak of WWI, wool was desperately needed by Britain so the government commandeered the wool clip in the name of patriotism. *(2008,NZ Memories,73/51)*  
> I am led to believe that the wool clip this season will be a very good one both in the Wairau and neighbouring districts. *(Nov 22, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2)*  
> All the wool clip, about 17 bales, was lost. *(Jun 23, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> In reference to your leading article of last week, it will perhaps be interesting to your readers to learn the exact produce of the wool-clip of 1853-4. *(Jun 17, 1854,Lyttelton Times,7)*  
> The wool clip is begining [sic] to come down freely, and the wool ships in harbour to fill up. *(Jan 7, 1860,Lyttelton Times,4)*  
> A "Gazette Extraordinary" issued this afternoon announces that next season's wool clip will be requisitioned by his Majesty for necessary purposes of war. *(Aug 25, 1917,Ashburton Guardian,4)*  
> WOOL CLIP DOWN. *(Dec 26, 1945,Evening Post,7)*  
> **NEWTON** The wool clip had jumped up with a bang. *(1996,Boss's Story, The,139)*  
> An application has just been made to permit the wool clip of the district to pass over the line... Today they are applying to have the clip of 30,000 sheep, or something like 1000 bales ofwool passed over the Marton-Hunterville Railway *(Nov 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19)*  
> **ANTHONY** This meant.. that the Loan Co. had a lien on my wool-clip. *(1975,Follow the Call,119)*

### wool clip
  
noun. machine-shearing (as distinct from hand-shearing, or ?idiolectal to J.D.)  
> **DEVANNY** Tutaki ... each year contracted with Messenger for the wool-clip. *(1926,Butcher Shop, The,12)*

### wool count
  
noun.  
> **DUFF** I wish I could remember the standard wool counts, or even the meaning of a count in the first place. *(Jun 21, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,35,1961)*  
> The fine wool counts have advanced ad to ad per lb. *(Jan 13, 1904,Evening Post,4)*  
> Mr J. A. Lambert waited on the committee in the matter of wool counts. *(Mar 7, 1910,Marlborough Express,2)*  
> WOOL COUNTS OF VARIOUS BREEDS. *(May 20, 1927,Ellesmere Guardian,8)*  
> For instance, among students of wool it is usual, so as to accustom the mind to wool counts to fix a given number of crimps per inch of fibre, to constitute different degrees of fineness or coarseness. *(Dec 30, 1930,Ellesmere Guardian,8)*

### wool country
  
noun. [Rural] land that is used for grazing sheep for wool production.  
> Wool is the first consideration on Leefield, as it is wool country. *(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,14)*

### wool dray
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wool-dray]  
> **BARKER** ... the wool drays are all going back empty, and we can get them to take the loads at reduced prices ... <i>Ibid.,</i>83 ... it [The Selwyn river] offers no difficulties to the wool drays. *(1866,Station Life in NZ,40,1870)*  
> **KENNAWAY** [He] was now driving wool-drays in New Zealand. *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,166)*  
> EXPECTED DAILY, the brigantine "WATERLILLY," with a cargo of MARES, (broken to saddle and harness), and GENERAL MERCHANDISE, including four of Bayle's best Sydney Wool Drays, &c., &c., which will be sold by Public Auction a day or two after arrival. *(Nov 26, 1853,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,5)*  
> Also, Ladies' BASKET PHOTONS, STATION BUGGIES, BRAKES, EXPRESSES, SPRING CARTS, WHITECHAPPEL CARTS, LIGHT TIP DRAYS, WOOL DRAYS, &c. *(Oct 16, 1886,Daily Telegraph,6)*  
> FOR ABSOLUTE SALE .. FOR ABSOLUTE SALE .. 1 Wool Dray, 1 Timber Waggon, 3 New and Secondhand Drays. *(Nov 1, 1904,Bush Advocate)*  
> On account W. Ritchie, Esq. .. 1 corn crusher, 1 hay elevator, 1 wool dray, sundries. *(Jul 25, 1919,Wanganui Chronicle,10)*

### wool dump
  
noun.  
> **MASTERS** Mr. Nairn had a boiling down works and large wool dump and storage shed at Pourere, and wool and tallow used to be shipped direct from Pourere. [1880's] *(1959,Tales of the Mails,123)*

### wool dumper
  
noun.  
[NOTES: machine that does the job of wool dumping]  
> The building was equipped with half a dozen or more seed-cleaning machines, wool dumpers, standard cutting machines, and other valuable plant. *(Jan 5, 1929,Evening Post,8)*

### wool dumper
  
noun.  
See: cf. dump(er)  
[NOTES: person who does the job of wool dumping]  
> The Wanganui Wool Dumpers Partnership hadconcluded its first financial year ofoperations... The stockpile of unsoldwools in store will contribute to thedumping trade as it is sold. *(1991,Ann. Rep. Farmers' Co-op,5)*  
> **MACKENZIE** I am told [c1853] that the wool dumpers in Wellington are taking to iron hoops instead of flax lashings. *(1893,Overland From Auckland to Wellington in 1853,37)*  
> The wool dumpers resume work at 12s for nine hours daily. *(Mar 4, 1874,Southland Times,2)*  
> Wharf warehouse hands get 8s to 8s 6d, and wool dumpers 9s to 10s a day. *(Oct 16, 1890,Daily Telegraph,2)*

### wool dumping
  
noun.  
See: dumping  
[NOTES: Includes attributive use]  
> **BOWMAN** Today wool comes in bales and is stowed into greater space than the old sailing ships had, while a considerable saving of space was effected by what became known as "wool dumping". *(1948,Port Chalmers,43)*  
> **BOWMAN** Working [i.e. handling] wool today ... is made easier ... by what became known as `wool dumping'. [ibid. 49-50] It was [at the woolstore of John Mill and Co.] that the practice of what has been called wool dumping originated in New Zealand. *(1948,Port Chalmers,43)*  
> [Hdg] The Wool-Dumping Charges... That the present charge for dumping wool be reduced at once from 2s to 1s 9d per bale. *(Nov 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> WOOL DUMPING .. We .. hereby record our opinion, that the process of Dumping Wool Bales, previous to stowage, is not in any way injurious to the Wool, and that the sale of the same is not in the slightest degree prejudiced by the said process, provided that no excessive force be applied, and that the pressing be effected with due regard to the original packing of the bales, whether vertical or horizontal. *(Aug 16, 1866,North Otago Times,2)*  
> The existence of a wool-dumping establishment in Lyttelton will doubtless prove a great boon to station masters and ship owners, if it has not done so already. *(Jun 1, 1871,Star, The,2)*  
>  *(Apr 20, 1899,Otago Witness,13)*  
> Hydraulic Elevators, Pontoons, and Wool-dumping Plant. *(Apr 20, 1899,Otago Witness,13)*  
> CAPACIOUS STORES FOR ALL CLASSES OF GOODS   FLAX AND WOOL DUMPING. *(Sep 20, 1919,Observer (Auckland),57)*  
> The Makura is seen in the Jubilee floating dock .. while other views show the Hikitia (floating crane), the tug Toia, cool storage power house, wool dumping, handling coal and fruit, the oil wharf at Miramar, and some of the other facilities provided by the Harbour Board. *(Feb 8, 1935,Evening Post,22)*

### wool dumping
  
noun.  
See: dumping

### wool gathering
  
noun.  
> **PHILLIPS** T.A.P. on hills wool gathering *(Sep 10, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,115)*

### wool grader
  
noun. someone who grades wool. Usually called 'wool classer'  
> He remembers the town fondly, but mostly recalls the closures: the Swanndri and car factories, the freezing works - a big employer when he first worked there as a wool grader. *(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34)*

### wool grease
  
noun. the natural impurities of wool (wax and suint) secreted by glands attached to the wool follicle;  yolk ??lanolin  
> <i>Wool grease</i>: Natural impurities of wool (wax and suint) secreted by glands attached to the wool follicle. Also called yolk *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> <i>Wool grease</i>: Natural impurities of wool (wax and suint) secreted by glands attached to the wool follicle. Also called yolk *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*

### wool grower
  
noun. a person who raises sheep for the production of wool; a sheep farmer  
> Mr. A.R. HOMERSHAM, WOOLSTAPLER, THE ISLAND, KAIAPOI, BEGS to inform the Wool-growers of Canterbury that he sorts wool for the English and other markets. *(Jul 11, 1857,Lyttelton Times,8)*  
> **WAITT** Wool-growers north of the Rangitata ... *(1856,Progress of Canterbury, NZ, The,7)*  
> On the other hand, the merino, of which the New Zealand flocks at first almost entirely consisted, though profitable as a wool-grower on the hilly and thinly grassed lands, yet was totally unfitted to depasture on the bush lands of the North Island, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 96 Wool-growers must bear in mind that the production of coarse Lincoln fleeces has increased enormously in South America, ... *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,95)*  
> **RUTHERFORD** I think wool growers like the noise; ... *(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,87)*  
> **MACDONALD** It is helpful to the wool grower to know what goes on at the ultimate destination of his clip. *(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,145)*  
> **WEST** ... ruin to many wool-growers. *(1937,Sheep Kings,13)*  
> **MARSH** There were the wool-growers, the runholders, the sheep-cockies, the back-countrymen.OED 1806 Glamorgan [only cit] *(1945,Died in the Wool,12)*  
> <i>Woolgrower:</i> Sheep farmer. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*

### wool grower
  
noun. a farmer whose main livelihood is involved with the production of wool, rather than mutton or lamb.  
> CAIRD Notes on Sheep Farming in NZ 42 I believe considerable sums are now being made by most colonial wool growers ... *(1874)*  
> Egmont Star June 9.8 The New Zealand woolgrower through the proposed tariff says in effect to the English woolen manufacturer ... *(1888)*  
> Settlers' Handbook 96 The wool-growers must bear in mind that the production of coarse Lincoln fleeces has increased enormously. *(1902)*  
> NZ Farmer Sept 1 1340 .. a woolgrower's hoggets proved to be larger off the shears .. By putting the Corriedale over the Romney, woolgrowers in the South closened-up [sic] the wool ... *(1925)*  
> Daily News Aug 24. 15 Wool growers are lining up behind proposals put forward in the McKinsey report while brokers and exporters are questioning the viability of the suggestions. *(2000)*

### wool grower
  
noun. a sheep kept for wool production rather than for breeding or meat.  
> Settlers' Handbook 95 .. the merino, of which the New Zealand flocks at first almost entirely consisted , though profitable as a wool-grower on the hilly and thinly grassed lands. *(1902)*

### wool king
  
noun. a sheepfarmer with a large flock on a large holding.  
See: sheep king  
[NOTES: wool-king]  
> **BARR** For hours they stood while the wool-kingsdissolved a mint of gold in great bowlsof hospitality. *(1908,With the British Rugby team,14)*  
> Concerning the late Mr. Richard Beetham.. of the big "wool-king" family of that name - Mr. M. Donnelly has a tiny anecdote to tell. *(Dec 21, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4)*  
> Hawke's Bay had lost the shield and that after getting Cookie and half the All Blacks to go and live in the capital of the province of the wool kings. *(Jun 9, 1926,NZ Observer,6)*  
> The death.. of A.L. Allan.. recalls the heyday of the New Zealand wool-kings. *(Aug 18, 1938,NZ Observer,14)*  
> HART Leaves from Early Hist Canterbury 19 Founders of the present race of wool kings in many parts of Canterbury. *(1886)*  
> A TRAMP, ESQ. Casual Ramblings 59 The wool-king reposes on rosebuds and down, while his men are downed in 'quarters' that a Chinaman would turn his nose up at. *(1891)*  
> NZ Farmer March 102 Some of the Amuri 'wool kings' also, are going in for pig-keeping on a large scale. *(1895)*  
> NZ Farmer Aug 327 But for the 'wool kings' the hall could not have been built. *(1900)*  
> JONES Autobiography of an early settler 118 This gentleman was one of those whom the Christchurch people called in those days the Amuri wool kings. *(1933)*  
> MCLEOD NZ High Country 11 Perhaps you may wonder why the places are so large and picture the runholders as wool kings with vast flocks of sheep. *(1951)*  
> NEWTON Big Country of the South Island 20 Thomas Cater was also the first man to take up the country at the head of the Clarence River and in his heyday he was widely known as the 'Wool King.' *(1973)*  
> MARTIN Forgotten Worker 144 ... in Ashley County four out of eleven stations had bad accommodation, and in Amuri County - home of the 'wool kings' - seven out of eighteen were sub-standard. *(1990)*  
> **HART** [Shagroons] ultimately became the founders of the present race of wool kings in many parts of Canterbury. *(1889,Stray Leaves,19)*  
> The wool-kings had been beaten at last. *(1889,Zealandia,1/31)* [NOTES: (Pember Reeves)]  
> **WICKHAM** The wool-king reposes on rosebuds and down, while his men are downed in `quarters' that a Chinaman would turn his nose up at. *(1891,Casual Ramblings,59)*  
> He looked so much more like a low-down lug biter than a wool-king. *(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12/5/8)*  
> ... the swindling wool-kings of the Canterbury Plains who make up cronk bales of wool and palm them off ... as the best. *(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/5/1)*  
> I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>wool king </i> *(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wool king</i> a big sheep farmer. NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> All through the South he [sc. Andrew Rutherford] is best known as the Amuri Wool King. *(Jun 11, 1904,NZ Observer,5)*  
> I could not but be struck with the exhibits of the "Wool Kings" in the Canterbury court at the Exhibition. *(Sep 4, 1890,Otago Witness,36)*  
> Mr. J. Roberts, who represents opinion which usually regards the owners of sheep as "wool kings" of fabulous wealth.. explains in support of the union's demand for higher wages.. that these men are sheep, not ship owners. *(Jun 8, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14)*  
> **ELDRED-GRIGG** [Caption]. Otekaike. This palatial residence.. enables one to understand the term 'Wool Kings'. wrote one journalist. *(1980,Southern Gentry,102)*  
> Then the elect of Patea went on to expatiate upon the benevolent virtues of the "Noo Zee wool kings." *(Oct 6, 1917,NZ Truth,6)*  
> Had this competition taken place among the "Lords of Wastes and Princes of Deserts," - as Sir Eardley Wilmot insultingly styled the Wool-kings of Van Diemen's Land, - there would have been nothing remarkable in the eagerness of the biddings ... *(Jun 13, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,2)*  
> The selfish few aid the holy Joes in keeping progress back - because it might offend the wool king. *(Jun 2, 1911,Maoriland Worker,12)*  
> In our own case, the petty sneers at "Little Peddlington" on the one hand, and at the "Wool King," on the other, should be discontinued. *(Nov 21, 1857,Hawke's Bay Herald,2)*  
> In your issue of this morning, the Rev. E. G. Penny is reported to have said, on the occasion of moving a resolution touching the proposed Church Society, that "he trusted that the wool kings would follow the example of their humbler brethren and contribute liberally to the Society." *(Dec 16, 1875,Otago Daily Times,2)*  
> They are wool kings, according to the vocabulary of your party. *(Jul 2, 1843,Evening Post,4)*

### wool king
  
noun.

### wool louse
  
noun. [Rural] a common sheep louse.  
> **STEVENS** There are several species of lice found on sheep, but those which occur in New Zealand are: 1) the red louse or body louse, or wool louse (Damalinia ovis) .. The wool or body louse is widely distributed and more difficult to deal with. *(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,83)*

### wool money
  
noun.  
[NOTES: payment for grazing]  
> **BUTLER** This person would take my sheep for either three, ... or more years, ... and would allow me yearly 2<i>s</i>. 6<i>d</i> per head in lieu of wool. This would give me 2<i>s.</i> 6<i>d</i>. as the yearly interest on 2 5<i>s</i>. Besides this he would allow me 40 per cent. per annum of increase, ...; moreover, the increase would return me 2<i>s.</i> 6<i>d.</i> per head wool money as soon a they became sheep. <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Suppose that of the wool money I have only spent [pound]62 10s. per annum, ... and that I have laid by the remainder of the wool money. I shall have from the wool money a surplus of [pound]630 ...; that is to say, my total receipts for the sheep [on terms] should be at least [pound]1530. *(Feb 10, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv 36)*  
> He had a good chance of getting some sheep at 50% increase less 3% for deaths. The terms expire on Feb. 1 1865 and 2/- per head wool money for all sheep over 12 months. *(Jul 23, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,97,1950)*  
> **MUTER** The squatter [offers] to take sheep `on terms', whereby he guarantees an increase of forty per cent, and a rent, called `wool money', of half-a-crown per head. *(1864,Travels in NZ,2/263)*  
> [My sheep] are on for one year less 40% less 3% deaths, 2/6 wool money. ... Sheep on terms are not what they used to be ... *(Mar 25, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,170,1950)*

### wool money
  
noun. the money received for a (year's) wool-clip  
[NOTES: wool-money]  
> **BUTLER** It is true I can wash well enough and can manage to stave over next shearing ..., when my wool money will be something considerable ... *(May 31, 1861,Letter)* [NOTES: 1861 May 31 Butler to his Father in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/93]  
> All the wool in the sea somewhere. Six bails have washed ashore ... We do not expect to realise one farthing on this years wool money. *(Mar 23, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,219,1950)*  
> **BARKER** I shall be three hundred pounds short in my wool-money this year. *(1872,Christmas Cake in Four Quarters,ch. iii)*  
> Another advantage [of selling rabbit skins] is that the money comes to the district, while in most cases the wool money, if there is anything over the working expenses, is kept at Home, the receivers impatiently waiting for more. *(Sep 2, 1887,Otago Witness,16)*  
> You have now a prospect of an almost perfect release from all further expenses - of a certain net and increasing amount of wool money every year - and of a large annual inrease [sic] of your flock. *(Jul 9, 1856,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> Those five millions of sheep would represent not less than twenty-five million pounds of wool, which .. in wool money alone which is annually lost to the colony, to which adding the value of the increase in our flocks, and two millions of money per annum will not cover the loss which the hosts of the little long-eared enemy of the pastoralist inflict upon us. *(Nov 27, 1889,Ashburton Guardian,2)*  
> The improvement on the previous year is considerably over 10 percent.;  showing that the bettered wool money, and the improved markets, as well as the fairly stationary prices have made a combination favorable to the colony. *(Feb 19, 1904,Marlborough Express,2)*  
> One Hawke's Bay man got aGBP4300 more wool money this season than last, and of course all the small men must have done well in proportion. *(Apr 20, 1915,Marlborough Express,6,6)*  
> So the "roseate hues of early boom" faded out and with them the prospects of lavish expenditure of "wool money" paled. *(Mar 27, 1925,Evening Post,4)*

### wool packer
  
noun. one who packs wool in bales (now presser)  
> **BUTLER** [There is] a further space [in the shed] for wool sorters and packers. *(1873,Erewhon,11)*

### wool picker
  
noun. a shedhand who picks dirty pieces and locks from a fleece at a wool table.  
[NOTES: wool-picker, woolpicker]  
> **WILSON** Meanwhile the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the wool-shed by a man appropriately named `Fleecy', and are spread out by four wool-pickers, who tear away the bad parts and fold the fleeces square, passing them to the classer, who, in turn, after a rapid examination hands them on to his attendant, and he places them in their respective bins according to their quality. *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,244)*  
> WILSON In the Land of the Tui 244 .. the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the woolshed by a man appropriately named 'Fleecy' and are spread out by four wool-pickers who tear away the bad parts ... *(1894)*  
> Wanted - One Fleeceroller and Woolpicker. *(Nov 26, 1877,Timaru Herald,2)*  
> Fyfe was a woolpicker by trade. *(May 26, 1909,Otago Witness,27)*  
> Auckland "fleecers" and "wool-pickers" will still shear the lambs. *(Dec 23, 1916,Observer (Auckland),7)*

### wool press
  
noun. the device in which wool is compacted into bales

### wool press
  
noun. a wooden [or more recently metal] press for condensing wool into bales.  
[NOTES: wool-press, woolpress]  
> **PEACHE** I saw one of Speedys patent wool presses being used and it worked well. *(Nov 4, 1876,Journal)* [NOTES: 1876 Nov 4 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 53]  
> I considered the wool-press, a marvellous invention. *(Oct 15, 1947,NZ Observer,21)*  
> PHARAZYN Journal Dec 21 George making wool press. *(1846)*  
> Weekly News Feb 19. 1 30 head of quiet cattle, wool-press. *(1870)*  
> WILSON In the Land of the Tui 242 At the main entrance of the shearing shed .. stands a wool-press and behind this are the bins ... *(1894)*  
> WATERS Mostly a Farmer 64 He was jammed in the bottom of a wool press. *(1976)*  
> Hawke's Bay Today April 28. 27 Clearing sale: old wool press, concrete mixer, docking scrim, truck crate ... [wool-press; woolpress] *(2001)*  
> **PHARAZYN73**  *(1846,Journal,73)*  
> **HARPER** ... a wool press ... bins for fleeces. *(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,62,1914)*  
> A.V.Buchanan here with lever for wool press. *(Apr 1869,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> Anderson [foundry] is making a Speedy wool press for the station. *(Mar 1876,Chudleigh Diary,249,1950)*  
> **BATHGATE** Gilbert was kept busy assisting to bale the wool, which was removed from the bins to the wool-press, where it was carefully packed and tightly pressed into the compact bale which might be adopted as emblematic of the wealth of the Australian colonies, as the woolsack was that of Britain. *(1881,Waitaruna,173)*  
> **DUNCAN** Archie Cameron ... had been working the wool press. *(1888,Wakatipians,9)*  
> **WILSON** At the main entrance of the shearing-shed, which is a long building with pens down the centre, and an outlet at each end, stands a wool-press, and behind this are the bins into which the fleeces, after having been classed, are placed preparatory to being pressed. *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242)*  
> Machinery peculiar to sheep farms is confined to shearing-shed plant, and consists of shearing machines and wool-presses. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,433)*  
> [caption] Above: Rouseabouting can be an international job. Here Margaret Way (in red) and friend Robin are pictured atop a wool pile in an English woolshed. `No woolpresses here,' comments Margaret. `Robin and I wound each one of these fleeces by hand.' *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,29)*  
> This grey goddess was in for a bit when work began round the Donald's woolpress, this very press where.. dad.. having compressed laboriously a bale of wool, knocked off the top of the press without sewing down the top of the bale. *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,81)*  
> The T wing of the shed, where the wool press and bins are located.. has a large cellar underneath, into which the daggs and such of the wool as requires handpicking is passed through the hatchway in the floor above. *(Sep 16, 1890,Otago Witness,7)*  
> AT His ROOMS, Queen-Street, on Monday, the 4th of December, 1843, at Eleven o'clock, the Cargo of the "William Stoveld," from London, consisting of the following MERCHANDIZE:- Hoes and Rakes .. Woolpress ... *(Dec 2, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,1)*  
> 1 very superior Wool Press. *(Oct 13, 1852,Wellington Independent,2)*  
> The Zealandia woolpress is used on all the big stations throughout the Wairarapa. *(Oct 19, 1905,Wairarapa Daily,5)*  
> P. & D. Duncan, Ltd., are now making a special farmers' Wool-press. *(Feb 13, 1912,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,6)*  
> New prices are:  Grass stripper, aGBP35;  woolpress, aGBP25;  double turnip ridger, aGBP45;  double turnip scuffler, aGBP25. *(Oct 21, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5)*  
> New prices are:  Grass stripper, aGBP35;  woolpress, aGBP25;  double turnip ridger, aGBP45;  double turnip scuffler, aGBP25. *(Oct 21, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5)*  
> [Advt] The "Ideal" One-Man Wool Press. Areal one-man press sold at a price thatsets a new standard in value! *(Feb 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,7)*  
> Several wool-presses were exhibited *(1986,AJHR,H-5/7 : 00)* [NOTES: Check pages]

### wool press
  
verb. to work as a wool presser  
[NOTES: wool-press]  
> **CARR** A young London clerk, who found work on a sheep station, where I was wool-pressing two seasons ago, told me that he ... passed ... without difficulty. *(1913,Country Work,7)*

### wool press
  
noun.  
> **RICHARDS** The woolpress could not be used because the roof of the shed was too low to permit the top box to be filled. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,82)*

### wool presser
  
noun. a member of a shearing gang or farm staff who presses binned and rolled fleeces into bales with a wooden or metal press.  
[NOTES: wool-presser, woolpresser]  
> Hugh McLein, the wool presser, is new to this gang and introduces himself. *(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/80)*  
> **SWANTON** .. the wool presser and his mate to bale up the wool. *(1892,Notes on NZ,96)* [NOTES: Notes on New Zealand]  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of the Pioneers 79 Mr McNab on one occasion sacked his wool presser. *(1936)*  
> **MEREDITH** I had a remarkable woolpresser - a Maori who had had both eyes blinded by an explosion of gunpowder. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,103,1935)*  
> **SWANTON** ... the wool-presser and his mate to bale up the wool. *(1892,Notes on NZ,96)*  
> **HAYES** ... A. Niall (wool-classer), R. Finn (wool-presser), ... . *(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,155)* [NOTES: 1908 Normanvale, Canterbury, Wages Book in 1978 Hayes Toss Of a Coin 155]  
> Evidence was given for the [shearers'] Union by Messrs. J. Ormiston (picker-up and woolroller), Charles Pearce (woolroller), C. Armstrong (woolpresser), W. Redyard (cook), and F. Waddell (Secretary of Canterbury Union). *(Dec 15, 1910,Maoriland Worker,4/3/1)*  
> **STUDHOLME** The wool-pressers in a big shed had a most strenuous time, especially in the days of the old screw press. *(1940,Te Waimate,130,1954)*  
> **STRONACH** The wool pressers, on the wool room among the bins, had a strenuous job, and were generally two very strong men. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31)*  
> WANTED (by 29th of this month) - WOOLPRESSER, and COOK for six shearers. *(Nov 25, 1886,Timaru Herald,1)*  
> WANTED. - 2 Shepherds with dogs, 4 Shed hands, 1 Wool-presser, 10 Boys for farm work, 15s to 35s per week;  Girls to assist. *(Sep 27, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,1)*  
> Cross-examined by Inspector Kersley, Donnolley said that his father was a wool-presser, living at Madras Street, Christchurch. *(Aug 25, 1927,NZ Truth,9)*  
> Donne is a carter in the employment of Messrs Campbell and Low, of Benmore station;  and on the 27th January, the prisoner was engaged on the station as a wool-presser. *(Jun 9, 1866,Otago Witness,4)*  
> Donne is a carter in the employment of Messrs Campbell and Low, of Benmore station;  and on the 27th January, the prisoner was engaged on the station as a wool-presser. *(Jun 9, 1866,Otago Witness,4)*  
> COX, Arthur John Collins, late of Lower Hutt, Woolpresser, died 25/9/38. *(Oct 8, 1938,Evening Post,6)*

### wool presser
  
noun. one who uses a wool press to press wool into bales; a presser

### wool presser
  
noun. one who "presses" wool, who operates the wool press

### wool pressing
  
noun. the pressing of fleece-wool into bales.  
[NOTES: wool-pressing, woolpressing]  
> **MEREDITH** .. wool-pressing is done by means of a tall square box, in which the woolpack is placed ... *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,96,1935)*  
> Is it not worth while to figure what your wool pressing costs you? *(Dec 31, 1896,Otago Witness,11)*  
> Other uses to which electricity could be put, and is being put, are:  Sawing wood, pumping water, harvesting and threshing .. wool-pressing, and running the farm workshop. *(Oct 31, 1923,Evening Post,11)*  
> Their gangs did most of the wool-pressing during the season and other work during the remainder of the year. *(Sep 10, 1932,Evening Post,23)*  
> He went on to Gore and took on farm work, contracting, and wool-pressing in the season, and then worked in various grain stores at Gore for some 25 years. *(Nov 8, 1943,Evening Post,3)*

### wool pressing
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: wool-pressing, woolpressing; relating to pressing wool into bails]  
> In the wool-pressing season they had an unlimited supply of grog, and on being refused more by the publican they would resort to all kinds of subterfuges to obtain it. *(Mar 29, 1856,Lyttelton Times,6)*  
> ON SALE BY BY THE UNDERSIGNED ONE Hydraulic Wool-pressing MACHINE .. *(Dec 22, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,5)*  
> TENDERS will be received at this office up to 2.30 p.m. on Thursday, 15th November, 18-8 [sic], for the Supply and Delivery of 40 tons of Steel Hoops and 1 ton of Studs, for Woolpressing purposes *(Nov 13, 1888,Evening Post,4)*

### wool prince
  
noun.  
> I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>wool prince </i> *(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class)*

### wool pull
  
noun. [Rural] production of wool.  
> **STEVENS** Meat remained under contract and control until 1955, but the price of export lamb followed the price of wool because schedule prices included an allowance for the slipe wool pull and the pelt. *(1970,Pyne Gould Guinness: the jubilee history,68)*  
> My father used to use Border Leicester and he swore by them for ease of lambing, increased wool pull, and no ribby pelts. *(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,4)*  
> The AFFCO killing sheet includes the skin value with the carcase price and only shows the assessed net wool pull for the line lambs over all grades on the kill sheet. *(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,4)*  
> I don't use the terminal sire on my Coopworth bloodlines because the Coopworths fall into the Y grade and they have better wool-pull. [wool-pull] *(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,31)*

### wool puller
  
noun.  
> **CARR** ... there is only one job that the most fastidious could refuse, and that is `picking pie'. Tuis [?=This] consist [sic] in pulling the wool off the trimmings that have been taken off by the `wool pullers' at the beam. These trimmings are ... allowed to rot until the wool comes away easily. [Pie pickers] live and eat by themselves. *(1913,Country Work,19)*  
> Sorters, classers, wool-pressers, wool-pullers, wool-washers, fleshers, digester men, carters, butchers, boners, drovers, gut boys, and what not. *(May 17, 1895,Mataura Ensign,2)*  
> The Waitara Freezing Works require a wool puller. *(Jan 10, 1906,Taranaki Herald,5)*  
> WANTED, WOOL PULLERS.   Apply MANAGER, Fellmongery Department, Petone. *(Dec 13, 1922,Evening Post,3)*  
> It is pointed out that while a certain number of freezing workers are paid on piecework, including slaughtermen, boners, and wool pullers, workers in other branches of the industry are paid on the hourly basis, and it is on behalf of these workers that the opposition is being voiced to the rate of 2s 1d being in force. *(Dec 24, 1936,Evening Post,10)*  
> WANTED, Experienced Wool Puller. *(Dec 20, 1943,Evening Post,1)*

### wool roll
  
verb. to roll fleecewool for pressing.  
> **NEWTON** Ernie, now well past eighty and living retired in Nelson was wool rolling in that same old shed in 1901 ... *(1973,Big Country of the South Island,20)*  
> Until four or five years ago, at shearing time, he was wool-rolling and yarding sheep. *(Jun 8, 1904,Otago Witness,80)*

### wool roller
  
noun. a member of a shearing gang who skirts and rolls fleeced on the wool-table.  
[NOTES: wool-roller, woolroller]  
> **BISLEY** The wool roller, having the fleece before him .. should first gently shake it from end to end so as to detach any locks or small pieces lying on it. *(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,37)*  
> **NEWTON** One of the woolrollers was wicket-keeper. *(1949,High Country,59)*  
> **STRONACH** The wool rollers at the table whereon the fleecies had thrown the wool took what was necessary of each fleece and then rolled it, ready for the classer's inspection. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,30)*  
> **MARTIN** In 1876 Tripp wanted twenty-four shearers, two fleece-pickers, three fleece-rollers and several shedhands. [wool-roller; woolroller] *(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,37)*  
> **CARR** The boss Maori undertakes to supply all the rouseabouts (as shed hands are termed) i.e., fleece pickers, wool rollers and `Sheep oh!' the [sic] owner or manager generally gets his own pressers and expert (where machines are run). *(1913,Country Work,14)*  
> Wanted.   TWO WOOL-ROLLERS, two FLEECE-PICKERS, and SHED HANDS. *(Jan 5, 1883,Marlborough Express,3)*  
> For instance, the act gives the privilege of voting by the electoral right to shearers, whilst all others engaged about a woolshed during shearing, such as wool-classers, wool-rollers, scourers, green hands, c., are denied the same privilege, although they have to leave the district they reside in, and are as numerous as shearers if not more so. *(Oct 31, 1896,Otago Daily Times,6)*  
> Good milkers are easily placed at from 17s 6d to 20s .. wool rollers, 25s .. and gardeners, 20s. *(Nov 26, 1902,Otago Witness,17)*  
> Of course, the eye of the proprietor is necessary to overlook the wool rollers and classer, as it is the shearers, but I have never found any difficulty in procuring an ordinary man to class my wool as above. *(Dec 30, 1876,Otago Witness,18)*

### wool roller
  
noun.

### wool rolling
  
adjective.  
> .. two men were at the wool-rolling tables ... *(Apr 10, 1886,Evening Post,1)*  
> Station owners can rely upon securing theservices of a party of young women intheir shearing sheds, and if they hadenough knowledge of wool-classing, wool-rolling and sheep-shearing machinery,they would be of material assistance tothem. *(Feb 23, 1918,Wairarapa Daily Times,4)*

### wool rolling
  
noun.  
[NOTES: woolrolling, wool-rolling]  
> **CARR** Wool Rolling. - When the fleece is thrown on the table by the fleece-picker, the wool rollers (generally there are two) skirt - i.e., take off the dirty wool - the neck (in some sheds) and legs, ... turn in the sides and roll up, from each end. The Classer, by feel, sight, and general knowledge, decides what class it belongs to. *(1913,Country Work,15)*  
> On a number of stations in the Hawke's Bay district the fleece-picking and wool-rolling is being done by Maori girls, who are said to be experts at the business. *(Nov 18, 1898,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** Fred Stuthridge came as a wool-classer in 1904 and was paid aGBP1 for each 1000 fleeces with 25/- a week extra for wool-rolling. *(1982,Blue Cliffs,89)*

### wool room
  
noun.  
> **MEREDITH** ... I returned to the shed ... I then said. `I'll give you five minutes to make up your mind,' and went into the wool-room. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,144,1935)*

### wool room
  
noun. a woolshed section where wool is binned, pressed and stored.  
[NOTES: woolroom, wool-room]  
> MCLEOD On the Hill 11 The leg of the T is the wool room, where the classer and his shed hands work, so that he is in the middle between two rows of shearers. *(1954)*  
> RIDDOLLS Farm Engineering 356 .. the shearing machines are mounted on an internal cross wall that divides the wool room from the catching pen. *(1958)*  
> NZ Farmer May 8. 2 The building is conventional in floor layout, with the woolroom and catching pens elevated to give full headroom to tally-pens and night-pens underneath. *(1969)*  
> **ADDENBROOKE** The wool room and shearing board were ideal for dancing. [wool-room; woolroom] *(1992,Home from the Hill,62)*  
> **CARR** In sheds where Maori girls are employed, it is the custom to have dances in the wool room every Saturday night ... *(1913,Country Work,17)*  
> **PERRY** The wool room serves as a granary when not occupied by wool. <i>Ibid.</i> 123 <i>Pressing</i>. - It is in the wool room that the greatest care must be exercised to ensure that nothing is done to mar otherwise careful treatment. *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,20)*  
> **NEWTON** [check] Round in the woolroom the pressers were already at work. *(1949,High Country Days,48)*  
> Clean out the wool room. *(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,310)*  
> **McLEOD** Along the front [of the woolshed] is the shearing board where the actual shearing is done, and the wool room usually forms a large projection on the front of this again. *(1951,High Country,10)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Wool shed.</i> Building in which sheep are shorn. Inside it is divided into pens where the unshorn sheep are held and which take up most of the room; the board on which the shearers work; the wool room at the top or side of the board where the wool is classed, pressed and stored. Some woolsheds have a loft built up over the pens for extra wool storage. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> Each division of wool should be covered in the wool-room with cloths and the shutters of the windows closed. *(Apr 2, 1864,Otago Witness,9)*  
> Good 4-room house, store-house, wool-shed, yards, wool-room, &c., on the Property. *(May 4, 1885,Daily Telegraph,4)*  
> Inspection of our new Wool Room is Invited. *(Oct 28, 1895,Timaru Herald,3)*  
> The work also contains chapters on the management of a wool room, station wool-scouring, and the advantageous getting-up of station sheepskins. *(Jul 18, 1903,Marlborough Express,1)*

noun.

noun.  
> Contracts have been let for the completion of the works on the Weka pass, which will be open for the next wool season. *(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times)*

Evidence has been forthcoming from every part of the Dominion indicating that the Department's efforts to cater for the needs of its customers have met with general satisfaction, and the response given by way of increased business and the promise of support for the forthcoming dairy, grain, and wool seasons has been exceedingly gratifying and a credit to all concerned." *(Aug 26, 1925,Evening Post,9)*

### wool ship
  
noun.  
> FIRST WOOL SHIP FOR LONDON DIRECT. *(Jan 19, 1861,Otago Witness,4)*

> **BAINES** ... I quickly made it fast to ... my raft, and with many a helping hand I gained the deck of the <i>Charles Martel </i>- Captain Dykes - wool ship from Sydney - Homeward bound. *(1874,Edward Crewe,103)*  
> The freight to Sydney is at present the chief draw-back;  but when (and we hope it shall be soon) flax is procured in sufficient quantity to enable us to load vessels from New Zealand direct for England, the copper will be conveyed as ballast, almost free of expence [sic], as is done from Sydney at present in the wool ships. *(Aug 12, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,2)*  
> First Wool Ship for London. *(Sep 18, 1850,Wellington Independent,2)*

> WOOL SHIPS CHARTERED. *(Nov 28, 1929,Evening Post,12)*

### wool sorter
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wool-sorter, woolsorter]  
> **BUTLER** When we got down to the wool-shed we lit a tallow candle, and having stuck it in an old bottle we sat down upon the wool bales and began to smoke. A wool-shed is a roomy place, built somewhat on the same plan as a cathedral, with aisles on the side full of pens for the sheep, a great nave, at the upper end of which the shearers work, and a further space for wool sorters and packers. *(1872,Erewhon,Ch. II)* [NOTES: 1872 Butler Erewhon Chapter II -->[here 1901 edn in Penguin Oct 1935 p.24]]  
> **HARPER** Shearing ... is in full swing, so there are a number of extra men, besides the shepherds of the station, shearers, fleece-pickers, wool sorters, and `rouse-abouts'. *(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,54,1914)*  
> **BARKER** We next inspected the wool tables, to which two boys were incessantly bringing armfuls of rolled-up fleeces; these were laid out on the tables before the wool-sorters, who opened them out, and pronounced in a moment to which <i>bin</i> they belonged ....<i>Ibid.</i> I was most impressed by the silence in the shed; not a sound was to be heard except the click of the shears, and the wool-sorter's decision as he flings the fleece behind him, given in one, or at most two words. *(Dec 1, 1865,Station Life in NZ,33,1874)*  
> [all small even caps]TABLE SHOWING THE OCCUPATIONS OF PERSONS WHO INSURED IN THE GOVERNMENT INSURANCE DEPARTMENT IN 1893. ... flaxmillers, ... gumdiggers and sorters, ... Accommodation-house keepers, ... Salvation officers, sheep-dealers, ... wool-buyer, wool-scourer, wool-sorter ... *(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,268)*  
> She was punting her behind along the oiled floor beneath the woolsorter's table crooning to herself. *(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57)*  
> A WOOL SORTER wants employment. *(Jul 13, 1859,Nelson Examiner,2)*  
> In the Supreme Court yesterday, Chapman, a woolsorter, brought an action against Sir Cracroft Wilson for L1000 damages for malicious prosecution. *(Jul 19, 1878,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> The creditors of St. [sic] George Ryder, of Havelock, wool sorter, hold their first meeting at the Court House, Napier, at 2 p.m. to-morrow. *(Apr 21, 1883,Daily Telegraph,3)*  
> A wool sorter named Chas. Stokes, a recent arrival from Sydney, on his way to Christchurch to meet his children, died suddenly in the Victoria Hotel. *(Apr 6, 1906,Thames Star,2)*  
> He was a wool sorter in the employ of the North Canterbury Freezing Company. *(Jun 26, 1924,Evening Post,12)*  
> Mr. J. Kilpatrick, secretary of the workers' union, called Mr. W. Sansom, a wool sorter, to refute the statement that there was a shortage of wool sorters. *(Jul 30, 1936,Evening Post,6)*  
> The unusual course of publicly giving his reasons for refusing a military defaulter's application for release from detention on parole was adopted by the No. 2 Revision Authority (Mr. W. H. Woodward, S.M.) yesterday, when he dismissed an appeal by Owen Robert McKirdy, wool sorter, of Timaru. *(Sep 11, 1945,Evening Post,6)*  
> The business of the Union shall be administered for the first three months by a committee of six members, and such committee shall be - Messrs. J. Muirhead (woolwasher) .. and T. Lockwood (woolsorter). *(Feb 22, 1890,Evening Post,2)*  
> A young man named Montague Clark, a wool sorter, walked in his sleep yesterday morning at the Carriers' Arms Hotel and fell through the skylight, cutting himself badly about the head. *(Mar 7, 1911,Marlborough Express,8)*

noun. a sheep-station.

### wool store
  
noun.  
[NOTES: woolstore, wool-store]  
> **HUTCHINGS** Working in a wool store was my first encounter with the real working world. I was a fleeco boy Friday ... *(1987,Tall Half-Backs,79)*

> .. 9 Strainers, 1 Wool-table, 2 bags Rye Grass Seed ... *(Mar 12, 1885,Daily Telegraph,4)*  
> Many students in those days would have worked in the wool stores stretching along Thorndon Quay. Wool stores too have gone the way of the Moa. Those who worked in the wool stores will remember the standard structure was a three-storied building with the middle floor used for sorting wool and being labout intensive. The top floor was used for displaying wool at wool sales to mainly overseas buyers. The ground floor was generally used for storage. *(Dec 5, 2001,Dominion,28)*

### wool table
  
noun. a table in a woolshed upon which fleeces are spread, trimmed and rolled.  
[NOTES: wool-table, wooltable]  
> **BARKER** We next inspected the wool tables ... *(1865,Station Life in NZ,32,1870)*  
> **MOWAT** Put up two temporary wool bins in the woolshed for the bellies and pieces, then started to plane boards to go round the bottom of the wooltable. *(1914,Diary,Oct 14)*  
> In addition to three shearers, there are two baling, two at the wool table, a fleece picker ... *(1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502,May 15)*  
> **NEWTON** As each sheep was finished the fleeces were swiftly gathered into billowing armfuls and carried to the wool tables. *(1949,High Country,51)*  
> **RIDDOLLS** The wool table may be either a flat table ..  or a sloping table for one man ... *(1958,Farm Engineering,353)*  
> snowed-in: when shearers are ahead of the wool table, so that wool is lying around the floor waiting to go on the wool table. [wooltable, wool-table] *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,58)*  
> **BARKER** see cit. WOOL-SORTER *(Dec 1, 1865,Station Life in NZ,33,1874)*  
> **CAIRD** In the perpendicular part of the T [of the woolshed] are the wool tables where the fleeces are rolled up and classed. *(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,23)*  
> ... the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. *(1901,TrNZI,33/196)*  
> Why shouldn't the `picker up' get as much as the wool-roller? Surely running up and down the board all day is as hard work as standing at a wool table. *(Mar 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,7/15/1)*  
> **MACDONALD** The wool table, about 7ft. x 5ft., is made of battens about 1-1/2in. wide ... to permit the locks or second cuts to fall through. *(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,69)*  
> **PERRY** There should be good large windows in the roof over the classing or wool tables. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 A boy or man to five shearers is sufficient for picking up the fleeces, but the operations at the wool table will be described in the chapter on wool-classing. *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,17)*  
> <i>Wool table:</i> Slatted table on which fleece wool is skirted and classed. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> **RICHARDS** A steam whistle ... blew for the commencement and finish of each run. The wool tables for rolling and skirting were in the centre of the woolboard. Close by stood the classer's table, where he sat and classed each fleece before it was carried by a shed hand to its respective bin. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,113)*  
> <i>wool-table NZ & Austral.</i> slatted table in woolshed where fleeces are skirted and the wool graded. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> .. 9 Strainers, 1 Wool-table, 2 bags Rye Grass Seed ... *(Mar 12, 1885,Daily Telegraph,4)*  
> Also .. 8 First-class Draught Horses, 2 Light harness do .. 1 Wooltable ... *(Sep 16, 1895,North Otago Times,3)*  
> The fleeces should be picked carefully by the "points" from the hind legs and thrown on the wooltable, so that they can be skirted easily without having to unravel a tangle of wool. *(Nov 23, 1934,Ellesmere Guardian,7)*  
> IMPLEMENTS AND SUNDRIES .. "Boothmac" Woolpress, Wooltable ... *(Mar 27, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,1)*  
> The whole of their valuable stock of implements, &c., comprising - Reaping machines .. Wool trucks ... *(Mar 29, 1862,Lyttelton Times,6,6)*

noun. a bench or table in a woolshed where fleeces are trimmed and classed.: the slatted table in a woolshed on which fleece wool is skirted and classed, classing table

### wool-blind
  
adjective. [of sheep, particularly Romneys and Merinos]  unable to see, due to wool growing over the eyes.  
[NOTES: aka wool blind]  
> <i>Eye-clip.</i> To cut the wool away from round a sheep's eyes. If this is not done, the wool, especially on merinos is apt to grow over the eyes and make the sheep <i>wool-blind. </i> *(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15)*  
> **STUDHOLME** ... we got a few of the [Steiger sheep] breed ..., but they were not a success, being ... `buffalo headed' and very wool blind. *(1940,Te Waimate,108,1954)*  
> **BOWEN** ... many or our sheep have become wool-blind, ie wool grows over their eyes so that they cannot see. *(1955,Wool Away!,64)*  
> **STRONACH** Our next job was to eye clip the lambs; for many of them were wool-blind. *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,54)*  
> **MOORE** The source of trouble were the wild hermit sheep. These had dodged successive musters, often had two or three years' growth of wool, and because of this were nearly wool blind. *(1969,From Forest to Farm,18)*  
> Press Oct 14 (Acland Gloss) 15 .. if this [eye-clipping] is not done the wool, especially on merinos, is apt to grow over the eyes and make the sheep wool-blind. *(1933)*  
> STRONACH Musterer on Molesworth 54 Our next job was to eye clip the lambs, for many of them were wool-blind. *(1953)*  
> J of Ag July 14 Trevor described the Romneys as typically wool-blind and dozy ... *(1983)*  
> NZ Farmer Dec 14. 26 He did not eye wig his Merinos last season and some of them came in wool blind. *(1988)*  
> STACEY Tales of a Tussock Jumper 97 A few weedy, wool-blind hoggets held up proceedings and one, just to be awkward, staggered out onto a rock jutting out into the river which was running a banker. [wool blind] *(1996)*  
> For one thing, the sheep were more difficult to work today than they were in former years - they grew eye-pieces now - and a wool-blind sheep was much more difficult to work than a clean-eyed one. *(Jun 4, 1912,Marlborough Express,5)*

### wool-blind
  
adjective. : blinded or with sight obscured by wool growing over the eyes.

noun.

noun.

### woolhandler
  
noun.

### woolhandler
  
noun. a picker-up, skirter and/or roller of fleeces in a woolshed.  
[NOTES: wool handler, wool-handler]  
> Woolpro Glossary of Wool Terms 42 woolled up: when the shearers' output exceeds the ability of the woolhandlers, classer or presser to keep up. *(2001)*  
> Wool Grower Spring 23 Rowland says shearers and wool handlers dealing with damp or wet sheep should change immediately into warm dry clothing at breaks and after shearing. *(2002)*  
> Daily News Feb 9. 24 Wanted shearers and woolhandlers - immediate start [wool handler] *(2002)*  
> A rouseabout - also variously known as a rousie, woolhandler, shedhand, fleeco, the person `picking up' or a blue tongue - is responsible for the passage of wool after being shorn from the sheep's back to the wool press where it is baled. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31)*  
> An expert wool-handler can tell very nearly how a flock has been handled between grass and grass, or between spring and spring. *(Feb 22, 1870,Otago Witness,7)*

### woolhandling
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wool handling, wool-handling]  
> I've even had the thrill of climbing onto the revered boards of the Golden Shears stage to collect a prize; second in the junior woolhandling. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,28)*  
> Judging from the greater care exercised in the washing of some of the Port Phillip flocks, the great objection of extreme dryness has been obviated, the wool handling softer, and more nature having been left in the wool. *(Jun 28, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> Wool dumping (first initiated by this firm) secured such substantial savings in freight and other charges as to stimulate the adoption of this process by other large wool-handling firms, and in this way has put money into the pockets of settlers. *(Jul 28, 1890,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> One of the applicants for wool-handling was at once given a verbal back-hander by being told that he was tight. *(Jan 11, 1908,NZ Truth,6)*  
> The Board's finance committee was given authority to purchase an up-to-date wool weighing and recording machine, so that shippers could absolutely depend on the weights supplied, but the Board also made it clear that the wool-handling business must be put on a strictly business basis. *(Nov 11, 1914,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> During the year the instructional activities of the Department have been considerably increased, espcially [sic] as regards arable farming, swine-husbandry, and woolhandling, capable and well-trained instructors in these subjects having been added to the staff. *(Jul 28, 1920,Evening Post,11)*  
> The wool-handling industry of Wellington is of the utmost importance to the commercial activity of the city, as its large warehouses at thorndon and Kaiwarra suggest, and special and extensive accommodation and facilities for the wool trade of the port is provided by the Harbour Board. *(Jul 30, 1936,Evening Post,12)*  
> WOOL HANDLING PROBLEM. *(Dec 3, 1942,Evening Post,3)*

### wool-jamming
  
noun.  
See: -  
[NOTES: wool jamming]  
> **BOWMAN** Captain Shortland personally supervised the stowing [of the wool cargo c1880s-1890s] `screwing' the bales into the hold to the singing of sea-shanties. The bales were stowed by hand until the last in each row when `screw-jacks' were used, based on `samson posts', to screw the last bale of the tier into position. ... This method of stowing wool was sometimes referred to as `wool-jamming' or `screwing'. *(1958,Port Chalmers,43)*  
> The old familiar hydraulic press is still in use, but instead of the customary gang of hands required for "wool jamming," a neat little steam engine and a couple of hands perform the necessary work in the most expeditious manner and with very little bustle or effort. *(Apr 28, 1871,Wellington Independent,2)*  
> Defendant said he had "nobbled a few drinks too many after a hard rub at wool-jamming," and admitted the drunkenness. *(Dec 15, 1883,Hawke's Bay Herald,2)*  
> As a matter of fact, a further little loan from "Bull and Co." is projected, and although the majority of these golden sovs. will doubtless be frittered through the broad sewer of party politics, we adopt the "laissez-faire" and go on cow-pumping, wool-jamming, and meat canning, and comfort ourselves by saying that we are alive. *(Jul 26, 1899,Wanganui Chronicle,2)*

### woolled up
  
adjective.

### woolled up
  
adjective. [Rural] [shearing] having a glut of unrolled and unpressed wool in a woolshed.

noun.

noun.

### woollies
  
noun. long woollen underpants [worn by high country shepherds and musterers.]

The internal padding, plus the long winter woollies, heavy boots and other paraphernalia on the outside makes mounting Mouse a formidable proposition. *(1983)*

> She gave me the woollies. *(1961,VUC First Year English Class)*

### woolly
  
adjective. [of sheep] carrying a full fleece of wool [as distinct from shorn and half-wool sheep.]  
> Drafted out all the woolly sheep and took them to the shed. *(Feb 27, 1879,Tutira Station Diary,27)*  
> Full-mouth woolly ewes $34 to $35. *(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,37)*  
> Lamb and cattle prices ease: Top woolly ewes $75.50 ... [wooly] *(Jun 6, 2001,Dominion)*  
> **Keirnan** Drafted out all the woolly sheep and took them to the shed. *(Feb 26, 1879,Diary)* [NOTES: 1879 Feb 26 T.C. Kiernan Diary in1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 127]  
> **T.C. KIERNAN** Musterers returned from back country with mob of about 2000 mixed sheep (800 woolly) about 7 P.M. *(Jan 20, 1879,Diary)* [NOTES: 1879 Feb 26 T.C. Kiernan Diary in1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 124]  
> **T.C. KIERNAN** ... would have done more but for the number of previously shorn sheep being mixed up with the woolly, making it necessary to fill the crush-yard more often, consequence being loss of time. *(Feb 26, 1879,Diary)* [NOTES: 1879 Feb 26 T.C. Kiernan Diary in1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 127]  
> In addition to the well-defined periods of risk enumerated above, a small percentage of deaths is incurred through woolly sheep getting entangled in second growth or cast in hollows, etc., *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,425)*  
> **WHEELER** Huiarua's big fourteen-stand shed ... holds 3,000 woolly sheep, and has a shearing board seventy feet long. *(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,70)*

### woolly
  
noun. an unshorn sheep  
> **CHUDLEIGH** Found rails shaken out by sheep and 1200 woolies got into shorn Ewes and lamb mob. *(Dec 6, 1888,Diary,1950)* [NOTES: Diary Dec 6 (1950)]  
> **MOWAT** Finished shearing the woollies at mid-day. *(Jun 5, 1910,Diary)*  
> At Waipukurau last week this firming tendency was maintained, though it was more apparent with shorn lines than with woollies ... *(Oct 25, 1951,NZ Farmer,34)*  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ I 53 Bringing in the woollies for shearing. *(1956)*  
> In the hoggets, heavy woollies made $50 to $55 ... *(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,37)*  
> 39 Top woolies sold to $36. *(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,39)*  
> See him coming in from the back country..his dogs handling each woolly as though its fleece was worth its weight in gold. *(1952,Arena,31:2)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** Woollies shorn in nineteen-eight will be fit to shear again./ *(1950,Farm & Station Verse,59)* [NOTES: 1909 Meek `Wet sheep' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 59]  
> **NEWTON** ...two of the musterers arrived at the [wool] shed with another `cut' of woollies. *(1947,High Country Days,56 (77 et pass))*  
> **BEATTIE** Each shepherd on the run had charge of over 2,000 woollies, ... *(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,107)*  
> **McLEOD** The same dog on a hot shingle face in mid-summer is likely to go and lie under a bush instead of climbing a thousand feet to hunt a dozen woollies through a bluff. *(1951,High Country,45)*  
> **LYON** The shearing, with fresh lots of woollies every day and drovers taking a return lot of shorn sheep, was put through in a week. *(1952,Faring South,198)*  
> Never saw such a great pack in all the King Country, as old Charlie's. See him coming in from the back country at mustering-time with a mob ... and his dogs handling each woolly as though its fleece was worth its weight in gold ... *(1966,NZ Short Stories,126,1976)* [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]  
> The new [Heinemann's] dictionary carries: ... mall, ... M.O.W. and o.n.o. ... dropper (for fencing) ... and garage sale. It does not contain diff and carby (which have now moved from slang into general use among at least half the population), leg-in, woolly (as a noun), farmlet, fat (or sharp) sand, nor, amazingly, home-brew. *(Mar 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times,21)* [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]  
> **CHAR;LES** The law says he has nothing to prove,/ As long as he keeps those woollies on the move./ -- *(1981,Black Billy Tea,46)*  
> Store stock:- 48 ewes (old woolly) 30s .. 4-tooth woolly (good) 35s ... *(Nov 7, 1917,Poverty Bay Herald,6)*  
> Prime woolly lambs, 36-under 9ad;  37-42lb 8a?d;  43-over 8ad;  second quality lambs, 8ad. *(Feb 16, 1929,Evening Post,12)*

### woolly
  
noun. a sheep that has missed at least one shearing, is heavy with wool and often hard to move.  
> **FOSTER & WRIGHT** .. there is the first sight of sheep, a mob of five or six. Two of them are woollies, which means they've escaped round-up for shearing for the past few years. *(1983,Stockman Country,18)*  
> **HALL** Some shepherds carry a shearing blade with them so they can shear any woollies they come across, rather than bring them in. Woollies are sheep that have missed shearing at least once, and they may be carrying a lot of excess weight which weakens them. *(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,81)*  
> **GIBSON** There were always a few woollies that would have to be shorn, and a few lambs to be tailed. *(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,70)*  
> **KERR** When Ken lamb-marked in ten weeks, he couldn't understand why there were so many woollies in his mob. Unfortunately they fell to figuring out why they had so many woollies that year when they'd never had so many before. [wooly] *(2000,High Times in the High Country,90)*  
> Found rails shaken out by sheep and 1200 woolies [sic] got into shorn Ewes and lamb mob. No end of a mess. And, to finish matters, a stray horse had smashed the second fence and woolies, number unknown got back on the Run. *(Dec 6, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,365,1950)*  
> (Cumberland's note to Pascoe `High Country') `Woolies' [check spg]are sheep which have escaped the shearing and as a result carry more than one year's wool on their backs. *(1945,NZ Geographer,1/39)*  
> **NEWTON** Over six thousand feet high it is, of course, unfenced and affords [check ?provides] plenty of opportunities for cunning woollies to dodge the musterers. ... These roughies duck down the Castle Hill side. *(1947,Wayleggo,94)*  
> **PASCOE** Sheep of Merino strain are easily scared into unreasonable situations where they stay to miss the next shearing. *(1952,Land Uplifted High,?164, ?165,1961)*  
> **STRONACH** Thus the sheep missed previously have the opportunity to `mob up' and join in with the shorn sheep run much better than `woollies', the advantages of this method are obvious. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 We did not get many woollies on the way to Tarndale, ... *(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31)*  
> It tries one's powers of endurance tolook out and see the patient, long-suffering "woollies" trying hard to finda nibble. *(Jul 31, 1918,Otago Witness,27)*  
> Sheep of Merino strain are easily scared into unreasonable situations where they stay to become the 'woollies' of next season if they survive the winter and miss the next shearing. *(Apr 1945,NZ Geographer,I/127)*  
> **NEWTON** These sheep were the real wild sheep - not the ordinary domestic sheep gone wild. The latter, long-tailers and woollies which have escaped muster and gone semi-wild, are known as "rough" sheep. <i>Ibid.</i> 175 That trip yielded ninety-odd woollies. *(1966,Boss's Story, The,154)*

This includes the rock fern (<i>)Cheilanthes sicheri</i>), woolly cloak fern (<i>C. distans</i>), blanket fern (<i>Pleurosorus rutifolius</i>), and <i>Pellaea callidirupium</i>. *(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/73)*

### woolly iwi
  
noun.  
> **GORDON** This is a seasonal job and gives rise to phrases like: `Chaps <i>winter</i> at a certain job (e.g. shearing) in the <i>off-season</i>, and come back regularly. The season is divided into the <i>flush</i> when lambs are coming in (two <i>flushes</i> `grass-fed' and `rape-fed'). After the first flush of lambs, a lot of sheep come in, <i>woolly iwis</i> [wou li: |i:wi:z] as they are called. they are tougher than lambs and the tally is therefore less - 80 instead of 100 say; and they are often called <i>grannies</i> or <i>grandmother lambs. </i> *(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

### woolly mullein
  
noun. nightshade, flannel leaf  
See: mullein, nightshade  
> **HILGENDORF** FLANNEL LEAF (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds ... in both islands. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967)*  
> <i>mullein, woolly</i> [s] <i>Verbascum thapsus</i> L. -<i>Ibid.</i> 88 <i>woolly mullein</i> [s] <i>Verbascum thapsus </i>L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,51)*  
> **TAYLOR** WOOLLY MULLEIN (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) ... native to the Mediterranean region, is widespread in New Zealand and most abundant on dry or stony ground where competition from other plants is weak. *(1981,Weeds of Lawns,134)*

noun.
