### woolly pully
  
noun.  
> Richard Prebble laughed at the public servant's fondness for "woolly pulleys". *(Aug 6, 1999,Dominion,8)*  
> The sight of those shiny, righteous faces, the woolly pullies and dog collars, was to National as the fingernails of God screeching down an eternal blackboard. *(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19)*

### woolly tree fern
  
noun. = wheki ponga  
> ...of our 10 native ferns only one, C. dealbata, is correctly called "ponga", though one of the Dicksonias, also known as the Woolly tree fern, has the Maori name Wheki ponga. *(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15)*  
> **FIELD** (And) <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i> *(1890,Ferns of NZ)*  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i> woolly tree-fern *(1908,AJHR)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park]  
> **TURNER** <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i> woolly tree-fern *(1909,AJHR,C-11)* [NOTES: Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District in AJHR]  
> **RICHARDS** <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i>. This is the commonest tree-fern in the Chathams. ... Woolly Tree-fern. WEKI PONGA. *(1952,Chatham Islands,7)*

### woolly woofter
  
noun. (1) a wimp (2) a passive homosexual (prison), a gay male, a drag queen, a transvestite;  
[NOTES: Newbold points out this is rhyming slang for "poofter"]  
> I'm sure their first reaction was, "Who'sthis willy-woofter in a thousand-dollarItalian suit showing us pictures ofchampagne and French perfume?" *(Jun 1993,North & South,60)*  
> [Norman Jones] would have despised hisunwanted defenders, the critics of thatcartoon - the wimps or woolly woofters ashe called them - whom he spent a life-time fighting. *(Dec 7, 1987,Evening Post,6)*  
> **JONES** [Norman Jones] will be down to haunt eachand every one of his unwanted EveningPost defenders, who will be woken in themiddle of the night by a prod from hiswalking stick, and a whispered accusationin their ear, "bloody woolly woofter". *(1989,Wimp Walloping,13)* [NOTES: This quote is not in the originalarticle but is a later addition. Our citefrom this column is wrongly attributed toWimp Walloper 1987; Wimp Walloper isactually 1989 and the original quote inthe dictionary file has also beenslightly amended.]  
> But the panel members are real people (*Footnote* <i>contrary</i> to the view of the Federated Farmers who called the STEP members a "bunch of balding, bearded woolly wofters") who read newsppapers and listen to the radio and T.V. *(1995,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,106/42)*  
> **NEWBOLD** I was inclined to think that getting it off with a woolly woofter might be a go for me. <i>Ibid</i> 132 Mata was going with one of the woolly woofs himself, so I didn't see how he could be involved. <i>Ibid.</i> 210 I had sworn never to involve myself with woofters again but they were good to have around; they had their own brand of humour and added a bit of colour to the place. <i>Ibid.</i>  255                  WOOLLY WOOFTER (n) Passive homosexual. Rhyming slang for POOFTER ( q.v.). *(1982,Big Huey, The,128)*  
> It's the war-cry of the woolly-woofters, and they're on the march again. *(Jul 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34)*  
> But who wants another woolly woofter who calls himself a poet? *(Oct 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11)*  
> It's the war-cry of the woolly woofters, and they're on the march again. *(Jun 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34)*  
> Woolly-woofters may think we've seen it all over the past eight years but to New Rightists that was just a kind of throat-clearing well short of an unbridled bronchial fit. *(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27)*  
> **JONES** [Norman Jones] would have despised his unwanted defenders.. precisely the wimps or "Woolly Woofters" as he called them, whom he spent a lifetime insulting. *(1987,Wimp Walloping,13)*  
> Wellington Government back-benchers - a pack of woolly-woofters if ever there was one - are utterly terrified of criticising the Minister in the House. *(1989,NZ Parliamentary Debates,501/12757)*

### woolly woofter
  
noun.  
> <i>woolly woofter</i>: term used to describe a person who has woolly thinking and is a bit dense; e.g., `Nice guy, but a real woolly woofter.' *(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
>  *(Sep 20, 1989,NZ Parliamentary Debates)* [NOTES: [McKinnon 4.30 p.m.: get written cit if need be]]

### woolly-back
  
noun. an unshorn sheep.  
> GORDON The Trouble with Stock Agents 39 Firstly, a set of yards .. without too many holes. A mob of woolly-backs .. preferably Perendales. *(1996)*

### woolly-bear
  
noun.  
> **POWELL** The caterpillar [of the magpie moth] is black and red, covered with numerous tufts of black hair,[sic] it is the well-known `woolly-bear' commonly found feeding on the leaves of many plants of the daisy family, ... has trifoliate clover-like leaves, and bunches of light yellow flowers. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,55)*  
> The most common enemy of cinerarias in New Zealand is the 'woolly-bear' caterpillar, the larva of the magpie moth. *(Nov 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,478/3)*  
> **McLINTOCK** This [magpie moth] caterpillar is the so-called `woolly bear' which feeds on cinerarias and groundsel in urban areas and ragwort in farming areas. Caterpillar of Magpie Moth <i> (Nyctemera annulata) </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/590)*  
> When full grown, the caterpillars are almost 1 1/4 inches long; black and ornamented with narrow brick red lines along the body and are covered with tufts of long black hairs. Because of this hairy covering the caterpillars of the Magpie Moth are known as `woolly bears.' *(Sep 16, 1972,Press, The,11/10)*  
> The Tiger-moth is so perfectly masked under the Woolly-bear that no one who was ignorant of the fact would imagine two creatures so dissimilar to have any connection with each other. *(Apr 8, 1908,Otago Witness,85)*

### woolly-faced
  
adjective. [of sheep] carrying a lot of head-wool.  
> J of Ag Oct 15. 366 .. woolly-faced ewes (which have more wool on the face and head relative to the main fleece area) gave fewer lambs, had a lower twinning rate, had a lower body weight, and were more difficult to get in lamb than open-faced ewes. At first sight it appeared that the production differences between open and woolly-faced sheep may be aimply due to wool blindness. Growth rate of woolly-faced lambs appeared to fall behind that of open-faced lambs in adverse weather. *(1962)*

### woollyhead
  
noun.  
> **RICHARDS** <i>Craspedia ... uniflora ..</i>. A perennial plant with flannelly leaves bordered with a fringe of white hairs. ... Woolly Head. PUTEA (white flower). *(1952,Chatham Islands,47)*  
> **DENNIS** Bare rock and salt air make life tough but not impossible for the yellow-flowering woollyhead <i>(Craspedia uniflora var. maritima)</i> and shore koromiko<i> (Hebe elliptica) </i>growing in crevices *(1981,Paparoas Guide,150)*  
> **WILSON** <i>Craspedia</i> WOOLYHEADS ... All have rosettes of leaves at the base, and hemispherical white or yellow flower heads. These develop into fluffy, fawn, spherical seed heads for wind dispersal. *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,202)*

adjective.

adjective.

adjective.

verb.

noun. [orig. trade name] a woolly covering for weak, newborn lambs and calves to protect against exposed conditions.

> Rural News July 22. 42 Lamb Woolover - often if you can get to them, you can save them! Calf Woolover - the natural thermal protector. [wool-over] *(2002)*

noun. an empty wool bale.

> NZ Farmer May 359 To overcome the trouble, Messrs Donald and Sons of Masterton have placed on the market a patent bale singer. The Donald woolpack and bale singer is designed for removing loose fibre etc from woolpacks. [wool-pack] *(1909)*

### woolpack
  
noun.

### woolpack
  
noun. the fabric container for a standard bale of wool. spg also <i>wool(-)pack</i> in NZ (as often in general English since 1297) the empty jute or fabric container or pack which, when filled with wool and sewn, makes a BALE of wool; a jute or polypropylene bag of regulated dimensions for packing wool in a shearing shed or wool store  
[NOTES: wool pack, wool-pack; several empty entries to follow up]  
> To Flockowners, Dundee Woolpacks. *(Feb 16, 1861,Otago Witness,3)*  
> **HUNT** A branch factory in Levin.. produced woolpacks... By 1969 Japanese complaints about the quality of flax woolpacks.. became known. *(1987,Foxton 1888-1988,136)*  
> **SCRYMGEOUR** Had I known you were such a carbine, would have had a placard on your back, 'Dalgety's for Woolpacks.' *(1960,Memories of Maoriland,180)*  
> **PHARAZYN**  *(1846,Journal,71)*  
>  *(1850,McLean Papers,8/246)*  
> Table of Duties of Customs ... Woolpacks, each - 6d. *(Aug 9, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3)*  
> **HURSTHOUSE** [List of dutiable goods] ... linen manufactures (except corn and gunny bags and woolpacks.), ... run-holder, sheep-farmer, flock-master, or Squatter. *(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/563)*  
> Harry went over to Eweburn with woolpacks. *(Nov 7, 1960,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> **BUTLER**  *(1872,Erewhon,Chapter II)*  
> **MEREDITH** For example, wool-pressing is done by means of a tall square box, in which the woolpack is placed and fastened to the top by means of a few spikes fixed around the outside top of the box. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,96,1935)*  
> **MEREDITH** ... , he snatched up an empty wool-pack, threw it like a mantle over his shoulders, jumped upon the uppermost bale, and sat upon it. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,25,[1901 rev edn Penguin Oct 1935 p.25])*  
> WOOL-PRESSING. - The rolled fleeces should be placed in even tiers in the wool pack ... *(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,320)*  
>  *(1933,Press, The)* [NOTES: Acland woolbale]  
> **BEATTIE** It appeared to be sacking and may have been a woolpack lightly stuffed with straw. *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,86)*  
> Frames hinged to a wall can be very useful to support a wool pack for bellies, necks, etc. *(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,311)*  
> <i>Woolpack:</i> Jute or polypropylene bag of regulated dimensions for packing wool in a shearing shed or wool store. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> The wool pack was stronger than the jute pack for standing a long journey. *(Dec 30, 1924,Otago Witness,19)*  
> Farmboy Streekstra was impressed that the drum cymbals were wrapped in woolpacks. *(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65)*  
> In the DAWSON, from SYDNEY. - 1,050 sheep, 7 horses .. 1 bale woolpacks, 1 hive bees, 2 cases green fruit ... *(May 18, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,42)*  
> EX BERKSHIRE, and other late arrivals .. 10 bales Sacks and Wool Packs. *(Mar 23, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,13)*  
> Woolpacks, Cornsacks, Twine. *(Nov 4, 1887,Auckland  Star,4)*  
> On the figures given by the Minister of Industries and Commerce (Mr. Sullivan) it cost farmers over aGBP100,000 a year for the privilege of manufacturing one-third of the woolpack requirements. *(Jun 6, 1941,Evening Post,4)*  
> A thirteen-year-old boy who was foundsleeping on a wool-pack on the wharf..told the police that he had run away fromhis home in the Wairarapa. *(Jan 8, 1918,Evening Post,6)*

noun. the scouring and drying of wool before packing for export.

### woolshed
  
noun. .  
>  *(1933,Press, The)* [NOTES: Acland]

> <i>woolshed n.</i> building or buildings used for shearing, packing or storing wool. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*

### woolshed
  
noun. a shed where wool is pressed and stored. [orig. separate from a shearing shed]  
[NOTES: cf wool room, woolroom, wool-room]  
> PHARAZYN Journal Dec 11 .. Teddy and self to wool shed at Watarangi ... *(1846)*

### woolshed
  
noun. a shearing shed [usually containing a woolroom where fleeces are pressed and stored before sale.]  
> She also keeps an eye on the whole of the woolshed, making sure all members of the gang do their work carefully. *(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/84)*  
> **MONEY** There was the .. woolshed, where the sheep were shorn and the wool packed in bales for transmission home. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,6)*  
> **WILSON** .. the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the woolshed by a man appropriately named 'Fleecy' ... *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,244)*  
> **BOWEN** These two plans illustrate the two main types of woolshed - the board shed and the chute shed. *(1955,Wool Away!,110)*  
> **KERR** Otairi had a second woolshed in its backblock ... [wool shed; wool-shed] *(2000,High Times in the High Country,172)*  
> **WHEELER** [caption] The woolshed. From left, dump shed (formerly sited on the shore ...) woolroom, toolshed, carpenter's shop, saw bench. *(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,14)*  
> **RICHARDS** The woolshed was ... enormous with its four-stand shearing plant, large wool room and [73] storage space for about 200 bales, and a huge night pen to cover over 1,500 sheep. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,72)*  
> **PHARAZYN** ... wool shearing shed [71] ... wool shed *(1846,Journal,68)*  
> Saw [Rhodes's] sheep-washing place and wool-shed. ...  E.J.W. and I walked to Rhodes' by 2 p.m. Saw their sheep-washing place and wool-shed, also garden ... *(Nov 24, 1850,Torlesse Papers,177,1958)*  
> **WELD** I do not mean that he should necessarily commence by putting up an expensive dwelling-house or wool-shed ... good pens; and an apparatus for dipping in case of disease, should at once be erected. The wool and shearing sheds ... are of less immediate importance. *(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,9)*  
> **PHILLIPS** Richards & John cutting firewood & stakes for Hurdles at Woolshed - *(Dec 31, 1855,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point)*  
> A sheep-owner need not reside on his run, but live in a more civilised district, and visit his flock at times. ... It would take [pounds]150 to set the sheep-station going well, [184] with wool-shed, &c.; *(Mar 1855,Letter,183)* [NOTES: 1855 Mar Letter in 1856 Fitton NZ 183-184]  
> **FITTON** Wool sheds must also be erected for shearing and storing away the wool; and occasionally the whole flock requires dipping to cure it from scab and other diseases. *(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),221)*  
> **PHILLIPS** Leach called at Woolshed early on his way to Xch. Ball - Shearing. *(Dec 16, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,59)*  
> **PAUL** One of these huts must serve you for your first year's wool-shed. ... with a few boards to shear on. *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90)*  
> Took washpool down, and put up pen round wool-shed. *(Dec 3, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> **HARPER** A New Zealand woolshed, long and roughly built of wood ... *(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Early NZ,62,1914)*  
> **BUTLER** How many hands shall you want? We will say a couple of good bush hands, who will put up your hut and yards and wool-shed. *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x 147)*  
> **BARKER** One of the best wool-sheds in the country. <i>Ibid.</i> 321 After this repast we ... set out for the wool-shed, which has ... all the newest improvements. *(1865,Station Life in NZ,31,1874)*  
> A wool-shed - improperly so called as it ought to be termed the `shearing-house', for it is here that the sheep are shorn and the wool packed. *(1868,Journal Saved from Shipwreck,33)*  
> **MONEY** There was the usual manager's house, with a loft for the accommodation of the hands who preferred it to the woolshed, where the sheep were shorn and the wool [7] packed in bales for transmission home. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,6)*  
> When we got down to the wool-shed we lit a tallow candle, and having stuck it in an old bottle we sat down upon the wool bales and began to smoke. A wool-shed is a roomy place, built somewhat on the same plan as a cathedral, with aisles on either side full of pens for the sheep, a great nave, at the upper end of which the shearers work, and a further space for wool sorters and packers. *(1872,Erewhon,Ch. II,[here 1901 edn in Penguin Oct 1935 p.24])*  
> **KENNAWAY** ... a rider on a very starved horse rode slowly up, one midday, to our wool-shed. *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,220)*  
> **MEREDITH** There are two woolsheds on the station: one at the homestead and the other at the outstation, where shearing commenced this season with the wether flock. *(1975,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,42,1935)*  
> **MASTERS** ..., `we proceeded up the steep hill leading towards Messrs. Birch's woolshed, and having passed through several gates erected for the purpose of subdividing the paddocks, we were astonished to see so much cultivation for winter feed.' *(1959,Tales of the Mails,45)* [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 45]  
> **BATHGATE** He had seen sheep shorn at home, but he had never beheld such a scene as the shed at Waitaruna. The shed, a large building of corrugated iron, was surrounded outside by innumerable pens, most of them crowded with sheep packed as close as they could stand. Inside, the woolshed was also filled with penned sheep, except at the end where the wool-press stood, and along each side, where space was left for the shearers. *(1881,Waitaruna,171)*  
> **WILSON** We inspected the [station] wool-shed ... *(Sep 6, 1891,In the Land of the Tui,194)*  
> **SWANTON** The term woolshed may not be understood by everyone; it is an immense shed in which the shearing takes place, the wool is baled up and stowed and the sheep are left overnight for the shearers to start on early in the morning. *(1892,Notes on NZ,95)*  
> **MACKENZIE** I walked up to the wool-shed which is a mile from the house. *(1893,Overland From Auckland to Wellington in 1853,43)*  
> **WILSON** At the main entrance of the shearing-shed, which is a long building with pens down the centre, ...The sheep to be shorn are ... placed in smaller and more convenient enclosures adjoining the wool-sheds, from which are drafted ... [243] a number sufficient to keep the pens, inside the building, full. *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wool-shed, n. </i>the principal building of a station, at which the shearing and wool-packing is doney. Often called the <i>shed.</i> [1850] *(1898,Austral English,517)*  
> **BELL** A large clearing opened out on the right, and a little way back from the road-line stood a slab hut - or whare [e-acute], as it is generally called in New Zealand - of a rather better appearance than ordinary, from the iron chimney of which issued the smoke which the travellers had seen. Farther back from the road were yards and a larger building evidently used as a wool-shed. *(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,8)*  
> **ADAMS** .. wool-shed *(1903,Kowhai Blossom,73)*  
> **PERRY** <i>Shearing Shed.</i> - The erection of a shearing shed is quite an expensive matter ... [17] <i>Plan of Large Wool Shed </i>... Doors leading from the shearing board to catching pens are ... swing doors. *(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16)*  
> <i>Woolshed</i>.- Often <i>shed</i> for short. Building in which sheep are shorn. All <i>w.ss.</i> have three parts, the <i>pens</i> (formerly skillions) where the sheep are held, the <i>board </i>where they are shorn, and the <i>wool room</i> where the wool is classed, pressed, and stored. Some<i> ss</i>. have a loft above the<i> ps</i>. to give extra storage-room. In old-fashioned<i> ss</i>. the board was usually in the middle, the <i>skillions</i> at each side, and the <i>w.r.</i> at one end. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **McLEOD** The most important outbuilding on the place is the wool-shed. This is usually a corrugated iron building with holding space for anything up to 1500 sheep. *(1951,High Country,10)*  
> <i>Woolshed</i>: (See shearing shed). *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> **PHARAZYN** ... wool shearing shed [71] ... wool shed *(1846,Journal,68)*  
> Saw [Rhodes's] sheep-washing place and wool-shed. ... E.J.W. and I walked to Rhodes' by 2 p.m. Saw their sheep-washing place and wool-shed, also garden ... *(Nov 24, 1850,Torlesse Papers,177,1958)*  
> **WELD** I do not mean that he should necessarily commence by putting up an expensive dwelling-house or wool-shed ... good pens; and an apparatus for dipping in case of disease, should at once be erected. The wool and shearing sheds ... are of less immediate importance *(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,9)*  
> Richards & John cutting firewood & stakes for Hurdles at Woolshed - *(Dec 31, 1855,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point)*  
> A sheep-owner need not reside on his run, but live in a more civilised district, and visit his flock at times. ... It would take [pounds]150 to set the sheep-station going well, [184] with wool-shed, &c.; *(Mar 1855,Letter,183)* [NOTES: 1855 Mar Letter in 1856 Fitton NZ 183-184]  
> **FITTON** Wool sheds must also be erected for shearing and storing away the wool; and occasionally the whole flock requires dipping to cure it from scab and other diseases *(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),221)*  
> **PHILLIPS** Leach called at Woolshed early on his way to Xch. Ball - Shearing. *(Dec 16, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,59)*  
> **PAUL** One of these huts must serve you for your first year's wool-shed. ... with a few boards to shear on. *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90)*  
> Took washpool down, and put up pen round wool-shed *(Dec 3, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> **HARPER** A New Zealand woolshed, long and roughly built of wood ... *(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Early NZ,62,1914)*  
> **BUTLER** How many hands shall you want? We will say a couple of good bush hands, who will put up your hut and yards and wool-shed. *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,X /147)*  
> **BARKER** One of the best wool-sheds in the country. Ibid. 321 After this repast we ... set out for the wool-shed, which has ... all the newest improvements. *(1865,Station Life in NZ,31)*  
> A wool-shed - improperly so called as it ought to be termed the `shearing-house', for it is here that the sheep are shorn and the wool packed. *(1868,Journal Saved from Shipwreck,33)*  
> **MONEY** There was the usual manager's house, with a loft for the accommodation of the hands who preferred it to the woolshed, where the sheep were shorn and the wool [7] packed in bales for transmission home. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,6)*  
> **BUTLER** When we got down to the wool-shed we lit a tallow candle, and having stuck it in an old bottle we sat down upon the wool bales and began to smoke. A wool-shed is a roomy place, built somewhat on the same plan as a cathedral, with aisles on either side full of pens for the sheep, a great nave, at the upper end of which the shearers work, and a further space for wool sorters and packers. *(1872,Erewhon)* [NOTES: Ch. II Edition [here 1901 edn in Penguin Oct 1935 p.24]]  
> **KENNAWAY** ... a rider on a very starved horse rode slowly up, one midday, to our wool-shed. *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,220)*  
> **MEREDITH** There are two woolsheds on the station: one at the homestead and the other at the outstation, where shearing commenced this season with the wether flock. *(1975,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,42,1935)*  
> **WILSON** We inspected the [station] wool-shed ... Notes *(Sep 6, 1891,In the Land of the Tui,194)*  
> **SWANTON** The term woolshed may not be understood by everyone; it is an immense shed in which the shearing takes place, the wool is baled up and stowed and the sheep are left overnight for the shearers to start on early in the morning. *(1892,Notes on NZ,95)*  
> **MACKENZIE** I walked up to the wool-shed which is a mile from the house. *(1893,Overland From Auckland to Wellington in 1853,43)*  
> **WILSON** At the main entrance of the shearing-shed, which is a long building with pens down the centre, ...The sheep to be shorn are ... placed in smaller and more convenient enclosures adjoining the wool-sheds, from which are drafted ... [243] a number sufficient to keep the pens, inside the building, full *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242)*  
> **MASTERS** ..., `we proceeded up the steep hill leading towards Messrs. Birch's woolshed, and having passed through several gates erected for the purpose of subdividing the paddocks, we were astonished to see so much cultivation for winter feed.' *(1959,Tales of the Mails,45)* [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 45]  
> Shearing at Langdale was concluded on Wednesday last, three hundred bales having been clipped in a fortnight... A luncheon was provided in the woolshed. *(Nov 23, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> [Hdg] Opera in the woolshed... Opera buffs will be greeted with a glass of bubbly in a 100-year-old woolshed decorated with ostrich feathers, says Jo Beetham, who runs Ostrich Blue ostrich farm on the property. "This woolshed is the place where bachelor balls used to be held... It's a living, breathing eight-stand woolshed (eight shearers can work at once) with a lot of history. *(Oct 4, 2000,Capital Times,10)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** 250 acres - a block of land 50 chains square to include homestead, woolshed, fenced land, etc. *(1982,Blue Cliffs,5)* [NOTES: Quotation from POINGDESTRE 1857]  
> **ELDRED-GRIGG** At Glenmark Sir David Monro was astonished to observe a 'gigantic' new woolshed, 'capable of accommodating 100 shearers'. *(1980,Southern Gentry,114)*  
> He pursued this theme further south, explaining that while low-pitched Auckland suburban houses were "woolsheds", in Christchurch houses had to pop up over garden walls and be "stables". *(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101)*  
> [Hdg] National Party Folk Dance in Woolshed. *(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,28)*  
> "THE AFFAIR AT HANDLEY'S WOOLSHEDS, NUKUMARU." *(Nov 10, 1898,Otago Witness,5)*  
> Wharf sheds and stores were in the same position as were the farmers' wool sheds:  the wool sheds stood empty for a part of the year did not suggest, surely, that woolsheds were unnecessary. *(Aug 28, 1924,Evening Post,9)*  
> Homestead comprises well-build residents of 8 rooms and all conveniences;  2 bathrooms;  cottage of three rooms with bathroom, large room for single man, whare;  wool shed, dip and yards, stable, loose boxes, harness room, chaff shed, hay sheds, implement shed, tractor and manure shed. *(Sep 28, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,1)*  
> BUILDINGS. - Comprise good six-roomed house, large wool shed, dip, yards, etc. *(Jan 27, 1900,Wanganui Chronicle,8)* [NOTES: Year is incorrect.]

### woolshed
  
adjective.  
> Each show is like a woolshed singalong, and to this day Neil [Fnn] swears that's his idea of a great night out. *(Aug 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20)*  
> Crowded House, presumably, are not yet ready to make their woolshed recordings. *(Nov 6, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52)*

### woolshed
  
noun. a large building with facilities for the shearing, classing, baling and storing of wool; also called shearing shed  
> <i>Wool.shed.</i> (w&obreve.&obreve.l.sh&ebreve.d.), <i>n</i>. A building or range of buildings, often very extensive, on a sheep station, in which sheep are sheared and wool is prepared for market; - called also <i>shed. </i> *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034)*

### woolshed dance
  
noun. a party with live music and dance, held in a woolshed [popular in the 1920s and in the 1950s and 60s, and often hosted by parents of boarding-school students during holidays.]  
> **PATOKA REUNION COMMITTEE** The first fern-decorated woolshed dances, inexpensively run with a local pianist ... *(1985,Podocarp to Pasture,35)*  
> **RICHARDS** The Red Cross socials, woolshed dances, stock drives and many other functions I remembered as a boy, appeared to be only half-heartedly supported. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,143)*  
> In some years the normal island population of farm manager, stock manager and their families has swelled to 600 sharing the hangi and woolshed dance. *(1989,Cuisine,12,April / May)*  
> **ADDENBROOKE** Home From the Hill  At Mangamahu we used to have quite big woolshed dances. The wool room and shearing board were ideal for dancing .. We used to have three or four big dances a year, the Settlers Ball, the Bachelors Ball, the Spinsters Ball ... *(1992,Home from the Hill,62)*  
> **ROGERS** `After the woolshed dance,' said Jill ... *(1960,Long White Cloud, The,13)*  
> **FERNANDEZ** `Mum, I've nothing to wear to the shearers' dance ...' ... `I haven't time to sew for you now , dear. You don't need a new dress for a woolshed dance.' *(1973,Tussock Fever,29)*  
> **RICHARDS** The Red Cross socials, woolshed dances, stock drives and many other functions that I remembered as a boy, appeared to be only half-heartedly supported. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,143)*  
> At woolshed dances he would recite select poems of his own with the greatest emotion. *(Jun 21, 1911,Evening Post,12)*  
> Saturday's competitions will be followed by the first woolshed dance of the season. *(Apr 21, 1939,Evening Post,18)*  
> WOOLSHED DANCE.   After the ceremony, which was conducted by the Rev. J. R. Higgs, a reception was held at the home of the bride's parents, followed by a dance in the woolshed, which had been attractively decorated with Allied flags and greenery by friends of the bride. *(Jul 1, 1943,Evening Post,8)*

### woolshed dance
  
noun.  
[NOTES: ?also shearers' dance]

### woolshed paddock
  
noun. a fenced area on a farm or station where a woolshed is located.  
> Puketoi Station Diary June 7 Irrigating woolshed paddock. *(1869)*  
> MOWAT Diary Sept 8 Sowed grass seed in woolshed paddock. *(1913)*  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of the Pioneers 68 They built a sod fence from the woolshed paddock to Waikaka ... *(1936)*  
> ALLEN A Farm on the Wanganui River 57 I tried to imagine the woolshed paddock become a mushroom town of cabins.. *(1980)*

### woolshed party
  
noun. a celebration held in a woolshed to mark the end of shearing.  
> Listener May 12. 64 At a woolshed party a few days after John Lennon died, they took to the boards and played air microphones with the handpieces. *(2001)*

### wool-sorter
  
noun. a wool classer.  
> Barker Station Life in NZ 31 .. these [fleeces] were laid on the tables before the wool-sorters, who opened them out, and pronounced in a moment to which bin they belonged. *(1867)*

### Woolspill
  
noun. a floating boom filled with wool soft knops, used in soaking up oil spills.  
See: knop  
> SQUIRE A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms 44 Woolspill - a WRONZ developed product by which floating booms filled with wool soft knops, trapping up to forty times their own weight, can mop up oil spills. *(1991)*

### wool-walker
  
noun. a backing dog that walks along the backs of sheep to clear congestion.  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 2 A dog backs when it leaps up and runs across on top of the sheep (wool-walking). Hence a backing dog (wool-walker.) *(1981)*

### wool-walking
  
noun. the leaping of a sheepdog on to the backs of sheep in yards or in a mob to break up congestion.  
> MOMATIUK & EASTCOTT High Country 74 When a frightened mob clusters into one solid mass, a good collie jumps on their backs to break the congestion and get them moving again - a manoeuvre known as wool-walking. *(1980)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 2 Back: a dog backs when it leaps up and runs across on top of sheep (wool-walking). Hence a backing dog or wool-walker. *(1981)*

### woolwash
  
noun. a series of pools in a manageable stream where wool could be scoured or sheep washed.  
See: wash dip, washpool, sheep-wash  
[NOTES: wool wash, wool-wash]  
> **TAIT** A woolwash existed at Owhaoka Station at the turn of the century. A river (Woolwash Creek) was dammed up between 1900 and 1905. *(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,73)*  
> **TAIT** A wool-wash was operated on the Mangatutu Stream on the station. *(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,106)*  
> **NEWTON** In those days the homestead was in a clearing in the bush at Kariori and it was here that the woolwash was situated. *(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,70)*  
> **BREMNER** At Kaikoura about 1880, James and Charles Fulton had a woolwash on Rorrisons Road. *(1985,Woolscours of NZ,83)*  
> **BREMNER** Future community woolwashes were to be established on the Makimiko Stream at 'Oruamatua', on the Rangitikei River at 'Erewhon' and subsequently at Springvale but they were not commercial ventures ... *(1985,Woolscours of NZ,85)*  
> **MARTIN** .. he also worked at a woolwash and at digging drains. *(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,46)*  
> **MASTERS** `Crossing the Rangitikei river we were soon inside the cookhouse at Messrs. Birch's woolwash, and were regaled with mugs of tea and plenty of good bread.' *(1959,Tales of the Mails,45)* [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 45]  
> **BOYD** He grew up in Howick and served in the Anglo-Maori wars of the sixties before taking up farming for John Heslop at Puketapu and marrying Jane Tucker, whose uncle had a wool wash at Clive. *(1984,City of the Plains,64)*  
> It appears that Connolly, who is employed at the Rangitikei wool wash, on Mr. Birch's property at Erewhon, with two companions went to look for wild honey. *(Jan 8, 1894,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> R I is generally warbling "On the banks by the wool-wash, my loved one has strayed." *(Feb 28, 1903,Observer (Auckland),21)*  
> TO LET BY TENDER.   TINWALD TERRACE, ABOVE WOOLWASH. *(Dec 3, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,1)*

### woolwash
  
noun.  
[NOTES: process for washing wool; wool-wash, wool wash]  
> NEW WOOLWASH. - The Argus reports that "a number of gentleman [sic] connected with the wool trade and pastoral pursuits met at the office of Mr. Lande, Collins-street west, where a number of samples of greasy wool were submitted to a new washing process ..." *(Oct 7, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)* [NOTES: Collins St West is Melbourne, not clear where or by whom this was written.]  
> Patent for an Invention for Washing Wool and Other Materials, to be called "Preston's Self-Acting Wool-wash." *(Apr 16, 1881,Marlborough Express,3)*

### wool-wash
  
noun.  
[NOTES: product for washing wool; wool wash, woolwash]  
> For Oamaru, free and duty paid - 1 keg butter .. 2 casks wool-wash ... *(Jan 24, 1867,Otago Daily Times,4)*

### woolworks
  
noun. a wool-scouring plant.  
> BREMNER Woolscours of NZ 75 Robert and Harry Smith's woolworks was on their father's 'Smithfield Farm' west of Winchester. *(1985)*

### woolyhead
  
noun.  
See: woollyhead  
> White woolyheads were shedding seed, and as the sun dried the downy foliage of these enduring plants, their seeds took flight in the gathering wind. *(May 8, 2000,Evening Post,15)*

### woop woop
  
noun.  
[NOTES: occas. variant of woop-woops q.v.]  
> <i> woop woop n.(phrase). </i>name jocularly applied to small remote or out-back districts. [cap W: HWO] *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>woop woop ... n.(Phr.)</i> name jocularly applied to small remote or outback districts. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*

### woopcacker
  
noun.  
[NOTES: 1981 Rangiora High School (Goldie Brown Collection)<i> woopkacker</i>: big]  
> **SLATTER** He's a hard shot. Yeah, he's a woopkacker all right. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91)*  
> **TURNER** Yeah, he's a woopcacker all right. A hard shot only needs to be a little unusual to be a woopcacker, though <i>woopcacker </i>seems to apply to things as well as people. The word illustrates the lack of standardization in these seldom written words: I would have spelt it <i>wopcacker</i>. Baker records the word as <i>whopcacker</i> but gives a slightly variant meaning, as `very good', `bonzer', `snitcher', `rumptydooler', and the many synonyms of these words. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: (quoting 1959 Gordon Slatter <i>A Gun in My Hand</i>:]  
> **VIRTUE** It had been a woopkacker day. *(1981,Always the Islands of Memory)*

### woopknacker
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** <i>woopknacker</i> a hard case, variant spelling of `whopcacker' and once more common, from c.1920; eg Gordon Slatter: `He's a [check???] all right.' NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*

### woop-wooper
  
noun. a backblocker (see woop-woops)  
> [Hdg] Mr Morton and the Woop-Woopers. *(Jun 7, 1915,Te Aroha Times,5)* [NOTES: No further mention in text which is about small towns in NZ]

### woop-woops
  
noun. the backblocks; remote areas  
See: more commonly wop-wops q.v.  
> **TURNER** <i>Up the boo-ay </i>means `altogether wrong'. This seems to me a commoner meaning for <i>boo-ay</i> than `backblocks', `remote rural districts', mentioned by Keith Sinclair, in the <i>Pelican History of New Zealand</i>, with suggested derivation from the settlement at Puhoi. Since the backblocks are already called the <i>woop woops</i> (the form given by Partridge who says it is mainly restricted to New South Wales: I know the word but think of it as <i>wook wooks), the sticks, the tea tree</i> and <i>the cactus</i> some semantic differentiation is not impossible. It must be pointed out that my meaning for <i>up the boo-ay</i> is already supplied by <i>up the chute</i>, however. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,122)*  
> **HILLIARD** `Me for the woop-woops smartly,' Trevor said. `That'd be the stone cold end of it for me.' *(1976,Send Somebody Nice,119)*  
> <i>woop-woops n. NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> jocular name for remote outback district (<i>in the woop-woops </i>thus isolated). [orig. unkn.] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887)* [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1359; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 550, WOP-WOPS]]  
> The Australian/New Zealand term "woop-woops" has much the same meaning as "the sticks". *(Oct 20, 2004,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> In the smallest towns, the remotest woop-woops.. you may rely on good food, scrupulous cleanliness, reasonable comfort and courtesy. *(May 18, 1915,Morrinsville Star,15)*  
> The worst of it is that you simply can't educate the Taranaki rustic or the denizen of the Taranaki woop-woops. *(May 8, 1915,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*  
> THERE WAS TROUBLE DOWN IN WOOP-WOOP AND JOE WAS FEELIN' BLUE. *(Jul 14, 1938,Evening Post,30)*

### woorser
  
noun.  
> **BAINES** At different times over twenty men at Great Barrier Island heard, as it were, a huge creature speeding through the forest; even the dogs were frightened, bristling up their hair and howling. The wonderful `woorser', as the bushmen had named it, was heard in many different parts of the island, some, indeed, were ready to swear that they had seen the monster. These, however, were mariners ... *(1874,Edward Crewe,238)*

### woos
  
noun. variant spelling of wuss  
See: -  
> Fair play is not about being a woos, it's about giving it heaps and not turning ugly. *(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,15)*  
> You have to hand it to her [Lisa Chappell], though; for such a big woos, she did a really good job of pretending to be able to run an outback ranch. *(Nov 23, 2005,Capital Times,10,23-29 November 2005)*

### wop
  
verb. achieve, finish  
> "It poured with rain the whole time we were up there. So to wop that sort of time off was a great thing." *(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post,D6)*

### wopcacker
  
noun. [informal] something great or good  
[NOTES: ca1938 Havelock schoolboys in forms <i>w(h)op-c(r)acker</i>1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 WOPCACKER [something good] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 4 WOPCACKER [WHOP KEKER]]  
> GILHOOLY, <i>holding up a [fish] tail</i>: Will you just look at this dirty big wop-kacker! [note 98] <i>wop-kacker</i>: New Zealand slang for `thingummy', [does Mason mean penis?], implying size, proportion, remarkable qualities. Etymology dubious. Now slightly archaic; Miss Gilhooly's [99] stock of <i>argot</i> is behind the times, nor does she always know the full meaning of her blunt and scabrous expressions. *(1969,Awatea,37,1978)*  
> For some weeks, though. I've ben trying to find space for my notes on a `Glossy' subtitled `This Will Really Kill You'. It's a wopcacker if ever there was, so here goes. *(Jun 17, 1972,Dominion,26)*  
> **McGILL** Now here is a wopkacker of a generation crosser. *(1985,G'day Country,183)*

### wops
  
noun. variant of wop-wops q.v.  
> **McALPINE** My father.. Used to take me out with him sometimes in his truck when he was fixing telephone wires as a faultman in the wops. *(1998,Passionate Pen, The,55)*  
> `[Hawke's Bay's] got the best wop-wops in the world. I do love the wop-wops.' ... He should know - he's seen European, African and `lots of Aussie wop-wops'. ... Since then ... he has just received an ICI writer's bursary for a novel set in (of course) the wops. *(Oct 4, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> **LELAND** <i>wops or wop-wops</i>: ... The wop-wops are the BOONDOCKS or BACKBLOCKS. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,114)*  
> <i>out in the wops:</i> far from town *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> I was about 3000 feet up and right out in the wops, so I thought I can either wait out for a helicopter or I can ride it out. *(Sep 16, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> ...Gary was raised in Rangiwahia, which he describes as "smaller and further out in the wops than Mangaweka", and grew up living off the sheep's back. *(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43)*  
> You'd lost points already for living out in the wops. *(Nov 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C4)*  
> **David Geary** That summer we grew close. Not Brokeback Mountain close, but the close men in the wops get when they revert to being boys without the pressures of women and society. *(2008,Six Pack 3, The,114)*  
> One of 12 and a farm girl to boot, she grew up in the wops on the outskirts of Gisborne. *(Jan 20, 2004,Capital Times,5)*  
> It is a convenience or a safety product. If you are in the wops you can lie in the sunshine and get your power from it. *(Nov 5, 2003,Capital Times,19)*

### wop-wop
  
adjective.  
> [headline to a Warren Mayne review of a book of paintings of NZ scenery] Wop-wop scenery for coffee tables *(Sep 19, 1986,National Business Review,22)*

### wop-wops
  
noun. the remote or primitive rural districts, the backblocks; the remote hinterland.  
See: woop-woops, wops.  
[NOTES: Orsman DNZE:  [Origin uncertain: poss. a variation of (Austral.) woop-woops. ] *ca1945 Victoria University students]  
> [Hdg] Never mind Queenstown, how about checking out Wellington's wop-wops... Six Ewes Tours has been running since January taking up to seven people at a time on the "Wellington Wop-Wops tour" around the region, stopping everywhere from emu farms to a spot of fishing on the beach. *(Apr 15, 2004,Wellington Contact,8)*  
> **SLATTER** You kidding? Where you been? Out in the wop-wops? Sonny's dead for ages. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,205)*  
> **WATSON** What on earth would a shy bushman like Fergus do with a woman away out in the wop-wops. *(1965,Stand in the Rain,102)*  
> **ARMFELT** .. for to them Waitapa was an Endsville in the wop-wops. *(1971,Catching Up,243)*  
> **ASHTON-WARNER** She herself had to return to the country, to `the wop-wops,'... *(1979,I Passed This Way,172,1980)*  
> **LELAND** <i>wops or wop-wops: </i>The wop-wops are the BOONDOCKS or BACKBLOCKS *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,114)*  
> <i>wop wops</i>: the sticks *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> <i>wop wops</i>: country esp. remote areas *(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> **MANTELL** But Angela! Lived all her life in the wopwops. ... Then she comes down to the great big city *(1980,Murder or Three, A,9)*  
> **HOOPER** Max was telling me about ... when they'd got dozens of deer ..., four of them way up in the wop-wops somewhere, pretty rough going by the sound of it, ... *(1981,Goat Paddock,23)*  
> **ROSIER-JONES** Maude turns to Tui. `How are you?' `Kapai. Ke te pai.' Tui presses noses with the old lady, then Maude. ... `Whenever I'm out here in the wop-wops with youse jokers, the hori comes out in me. Kapai and hongi..' *(1985,Cast Two Shadows,88)*  
> `[Hawke's Bay's] got the best wop-wops in the world. I do love the wop-wops.' ... He should know - he's seen European, African and `lots of Aussie wop-wops'. ... Since then ... he has just received an ICI writer's bursary for a novel set in (of course) the wops *(Oct 4, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> **HULME** `Where you been this time? Commune? Nga Tama Toa? The wopwops?' *(1986,Te Kaihau,122)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wopwops</i> remote district, variant of Australian `woopwoops', which is apparently a satirical play on Aboriginal habit of duplication of place names; eg `I've lived too long in the wopwops to ever get used to driving over the harbour bridge.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> **MACRAE** We live in Mt Eden, not out in the wop-wops. *(1988,Awful Childhoods,193)*  
> We lived out in the wop-wops then - on a rehab place, miles from town up a metal road. Apart from a mate on the next farm I didn't have much to do with anyone outside the family. There was the Plunket Nurse, of course, when the baby was little. And the Bon Brush man, and a few other salesmen. *(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6)*  
> ...we are a long way from the world's major markets, which means we don't have as much interchange of people, ideas and processes, so we are a bit out in the wop-wops. *(Sep 25, 2004,Dominion Post,E2)*  
> Marlow and Hastings emerge less as young gallants and more as a couple of insufferable London fops down in the wop-wops hunting for love and dowries. *(May 15, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55)*  
> Paritai Drive, Herne Bay and Parnell are not the wop-wops. *(Jan 31, 2007,NZ Herald,A2)*

### wop-wops
  
noun.  
> [The play] concerns Isaac, who runs a tearooms in the "wop wops". *(Sep 28, 2000,Dominion,18)*  
> There are no shop-shops in the wop-wops. *(Nov 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C4)*  
> Who do I complain to before I slap  over page 1 the story of a journo who nearly died of exposure in the wop-wops? *(Jul 3, 1992,National Business Review,10)*  
> I grew up in Rangiwahia which is in thewopwops of Manawatu. *(Apr 29, 1995,Dominion,20)*  
> The only organisation he was in favour of backing was surf lifesaving. "Where does it end?" he asked. Where indeed? Down the gurgler because of the insularity of one small council out in the wop-wops, it seems. *(May 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A2)*  
> [Hawkes' Bay's] got the best wop-wops in the world. *(Aug 4, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*

### wop-wops, wops
  
noun. [informal; Rural] an isolated farming area.

> .. Geary was raised in Rangiwahia, which he describes as "smaller and further out in the wops than Managaweka" and grew up living off the sheep's back. *(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43)*  
> .. throw a drag queen into a shearing gang way out in the wops and see what happens. *(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44)*

noun.

[ed. fn] A portable font, usually metal, carried by itinerant clegy in the Colonies and in the U.S.A. *(Dec 12, 1850,Auckland Journals,45,1971)*

verb. to give a beating to, work over

### work
  
verb. [stock] to fertilise, aerate or pug a paddock.  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 142 What happens in every paddock 'worked' by sheep for any length of time had not then occurred ... *(1921)*  
> **NESFIELD** In a few months' time I had learnt all the most important items of a bush education: how to cook, make bread or damper, ... work a dog, work in the yards .. *(1881,Chequered Career,37)*

### work
  
verb. [sheepdog handlers]  to instruct dogs to work.  
> **ELWELL** .. delighted to show the new chum .. how to work a cattle dog. *(1878,Boy Colonists,48)*  
> **GORDON** One of the most important factors in working a dog on the zigzag is being aware of the boundaries of the set the sheep are in at the time. *(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,153)*  
> **CHERRILL** [The sheep-dog] was never allowed to be worked by anyone but her own master. *(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,160)*  
> **JOHNSON** Mr Parkins and Stan were to leave a 3 a.m. for the range, the former working his two dogs and the latter his faithful Pat. `Stan, you know your beat,' announced Mr Parkins ... *(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,63)*  
> **ELWELL** F. was delighted to show the `new chum' ... how to work a cattle dog *(1878,Boy Colonists,48)*  
> **REES** She had not yet learnt to `work' a dog. *(1924,Heather of the South,108)*  
> **ELWELL** The two mobs now took opposite directions and it was a long time before Ernest and `Maidie' by careful working could bring them together *(1878,Boy Colonists,183)*  
> **CHAMIER** work them in circles, my man [?cit???] *(1891,Philosopher Dick,335)*  
> He can sing well and play both the piano and the fiddle almost to perfection, and, though trained as a clerk at Home, he can as soon as he lands in the Colony ride a buck-jumper and work dogs in mustering sheep. *(Jul 19, 1889,Clutha Leader,6)*  
> WANTED, Employment, Energetic Lad, 15, on Sheep Station, accustomed to sheep and horses.   Work dogs. *(Jul 17, 1908,Nelson Evening Mail,3)*  
> One of them could work dogs as well as a man, using a tin whistle. *(Feb 2, 1917,Feilding Star,2)*

verb.

verb. to extract work from, to manage (animals, as dogs, sheep)

### work
  
verb.

verb.

### work
  
verb. to extract resources, etc. from (by mining, milling, hunting, etc  
[NOTES: this hdwd all mining]  
> That the troughs and lead be made by the diggers: each party supplying an additional length, ... [184] The Californian diggers on the stream [at Kapanga] agree in the belief that the bed rock could be worked in such a manner to great advantage. *(Dec 10, 1852,New Zealand's French Pass & D'Urville Island,V/30/183-184)* [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]  
> **J.G. WALKER** Tuesday 9th bottomed the hole, found colour, but not enough quantities to pay for working. *(Jan 9, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker))* [NOTES: 1863 Jan 9 J.G. Walker Journal and Letters (ATB:TS)(Dunstan Diggings)]  
> **HAAST** ... the whole banks, principally where the coast formed shallow bays, had been worked by gold-miners, but most of the claims were now abandoned. *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,158)*  
> **SWAINSON** Persons found working alluvial Gold on any Land within the district aforesaid, without having previously paid the License Fee ... will be proceeded against according to law. <i>Ibid.</i> 161 Every person desirous of establishing a claim to a particular portion of unoccupied ground, by working in the ordinary method, for alluvial Gold, may have his claim marked out on the following scale, viz., (1) 15 feet frontage to either side of a River. ... *(1853,Auckland,159)* [NOTES: 1852 Coromandel Gold Field. Provisional Regulations in 1853 Swainson Auckland 159]

### work
  
verb.

### work
  
verb.  
[NOTES: re bush, milling]  
> **HARPER** On the Arawata and Blue Rivers ... there is a very fine patch of birch (really a beech) bush, which could be worked with advantage from one centre. *(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,31)*  
> **RICHARDS** A bush gang must always work as a team and work a block accordingly, ... A bushman has two meals during the day, one at 10 a.m. and the next at 2 p.m. They are always referred to as `smoko'. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,59)*  
> **BARRY** ... to join forces for the purpose of erecting an up to date saw-mill to work this and other adjacent areas of native timber. *(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,146)* [NOTES: 1926 Prospectus of the Waitane Saw-milling and Afforestation Ltd in 1966 Barry In the Lee of the Hokonuis 146]

verb.

verb. to handle  
[NOTES: working wool; 1 cit]  
> **BOWMAN** ... working wool today *(1948,Port Chalmers,43)*

### work (gumland) on a face
  
phrase. to trench and cut the land to extract gum.  
> **THOMAS** Instead of relying on a spear to locate gum in pockets or patches, they began working gum-bearing land `on a face'. *(1970,Way Up North,35)*

### work (off) a dead horse
  
phrase. (occas. bury a dead horse) to work off a debt, esp. an advance on wages.  
> By the time the Government have got all their money, the Board will be a creditor of Hynes' to a considerable extent, and the same policy of working off a dead horse will have to be carried on. *(Nov 16, 1886,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> The fact is that the producers have been working off a "dead horse" so far. The money received for their stock and produce has not gone into their pockets for use and circulation in the ordinary manner. *(Sep 11, 1891,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> He had earned nothing, was looking after a farm and was not paid for it, because he was "working off a dead horse." *(Mar 30, 1899,Timaru Herald,4)*  
> **T.J. THOMPSON** It was customary for crews to receive two months' wages before going on board, and this they spent promptly, so that for the first part of the voyage they were `working off a dead horse.' *(Feb 16, 1842,Journal)* [NOTES: 1842 Feb 16 Journal in `Nelson is Our Port' Nelson Evening Mail (?1978)]  
> **HENRY BRETT** Then the diary goes on to duly chronicle the daily round of life aboard, one uncommon thing noted being the old sailoring custom, now a thing of the past, of `working off a dead horse.' The name is derived from the fact that in those days a sailor had a month's wages advanced to him before leaving Port. As he invariably spent it, he called the first four weeks at sea `working off a dead horse', and the end of the term was signalised by the heaving overboard, with muchceremony, of an effigy of the defunct equine. *(Jun 1862,Diary)* [NOTES: 1862 June Diary Henry Brett in 1927 Brett and Hook The Albertlanders 45]  
> **BUTLER** They [station hands] will come back [from a spree] possibly with a  dead horse to work off - that is, a debt at the accommodation house - and will work hard for another year to have another drinking bout at the end of it. *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x 146)*  
> Work A Dead Horse: Working off an old debt, usually for overdrawn wages. [[1951 p.365] This is a nautical expression and the sailors on sailing ships used to burn orthrow overboard a symbolical horse of canvas and tow when they had worked off the dead horse.] (Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement],p.129) *(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15)* [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]  
> Dead horse.-Advance of wages. This is a nautical expression and the sailors on sailing ships used to burn or throw overboard a symbolical horse of canvas and tow when they had worked off the  d.h. *(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15)* [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]  
> **BAKER** Work a dead horse, a New Zealand variation of the Australian  ride the dead horse and work off a dead horse, meaning to perform work that has already been paid for or to redeem a debt. *(1941,NZ Slang,54)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** Work a dead horse (New Zealand); ride the dead horse or  work off the dead horse (Australian). Variants of  work for a dead horse, q.v. at dead horse. *(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1225)*  
> **MAY** The miners were given a respite sufficient to allow them to open their claims and work off the `dead horse' - that is, the preparation of the ground before regular mining started *(1975,Miners and Militants,64)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]  
> **BUCKLEY** The passing of the first month [at sea in the  Westminster in 1842] brought the sailors' ceremony of `burying the dead horse'. When engaged, the men received one month's pay in advance to enable them to outfit themselves. Few would have much left, so for the first month they felt themselves to be working for nothing, or `working a dead horse' which must now be buried.  An equine effigy was placed on a wooden frame with four wheels, and amid loud singing was drawn around the deck three times by sailors dressed as policemen bearing staves of sailcloth filled with straw, hats of the same material and whiskers and long wigs of white yarn. The `horse' was put up for auction (the money being for liquor afterwards) and was then hoisted to the main yard-arm mounted by a sailor, during another chorus. Duly hoisted with this ceremony the `horse' was set alight by a long squib, the rope was cut, and the `poor oldman' (as the song went) fell into the sea. Thus was the dead horse buried. [Possibly from William Harris's MS Journal of a voyage; or John Newland diary from 1841.] *(1983,Of Toffs and Toilers,58)*  
> **RICHARDS** With the promise of a weekly wage I would no longer be compelled to shear for three months each year which would mostly go in paying off a `dead horse' (store account). *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,107)*  
> We are only working a dead horse here with the mortgage and one thing and another. *(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,2/591)*  
> .. `dead horse' should have been in because the time that had to be worked off for an advance pay not only meant a lot to the sailors coming out here, but was transferred to our mining life to signify the overburden which had to be stripped before paydirt could be reached. *(1978,Otago Daily Times)* [NOTES: ?1978 Griffiths Otago Daily Times Review of HNZD p? .]  
> Working off a dead horse is never a pleasant operation, either to a corporation or an individual. *(Jun 10, 1889,Evening Post,3)*  
> We are not going to deny it, but we should like to point out that we have been working off our dead horse during the past five years of unostentatious advance. According to Mr Stead, we have reduced the debt per head during the last four years very considerably, until it now stands at L54 15s 11d per head. *(Sep 10, 1886,Otago Witness,21)*

### work a slinter
  
phrase.  
> **McGILL** <i>slinter</i>, sometimes<i> slanter</i>, occasionally <i>shlanter</i>, a trick; adapted from `sclenter' or `slenter', probably Afrikaans or Dutch for an untrustworthy person on South African diamond fields; eg Gordon Slatter's <i>A Gun in My Hand:</i> `Wolkinson ... worked a slinter at the end.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,104)*  
> Origin only to be guessed at. A false call or call where the driver can't locate the person who ordered the cab. Can be used as a noun - `It's a slinter'; adjectivally - `The slintered car will get the next job'; or as a verb - `I've been slintered'. *(Jul 1983,Metro,no.25 81)*  
> Other unusual words are explained in the text, except perhaps one or two which are in almost universal use in New Zealand ... Finally there is `slinter' which alone presented the editor with any difficulty. To `work a slinter' apparently is - to utilize alien idiom - to `pull a fast one' *(1945,NZ Geographer,39)* [NOTES: (Cumberland's Note to Pascoe's article)]  
> **SLATTER** That bloody Wilkinson. Cunning sod. He worked a slinter at the end. Ref shoulda penalised him for deliberate infringement of the rules. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,166)*  
> **SYDER & HODGETTS** To make a gain through the deception or trickery at the expense of another, but not in a serious way entailing heavy loss. .in 4;`He's worked a slinter there. I see he's used up his old supply of screws instead of the brass ones I said I wanted.' *(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],1102)*  
> It was Brixton's habit - talking to himself, a long monologue which reproduced the slang of forty years ago. Sometimes in the company of somebody younger he might for a change talk it out loud, tell about some bloke he had a snitcher on, some joker who would work a slinter if you didn't watch out - or he might start poking borax. *(1974,Islands,7/91)* [NOTES: Sargeson (Brixton)]  
> **SARGESON** It was Brixton's habit, this talking to himself, a long monologue which reproduced the slang of forty years ago. Sometimes ... he might ... tell about some bloke he had a snitcher on, or some joker [30] with the bad habit of poking borax, or he might work a slinter if you gave him half the chance. *(1976,Sunset Village,29)*  
> Your Donkey Has Eaten My Passport. [woman telling story of another woman being asked to wear a furcoat through customs to get someone out of paying duty] `The original person who was trying to work the slinter couldn't do anything about it.' *(Feb 14, 1982,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: 1982 February 14 10.20.a.m. 2YA]  
> **BEARDSLEY** Somebody tried to work a bloody slinter on the union, but it didn't come off.<i> Ibid.</i>245  slinter (work a s-) deceive *(1984,Blackball 08,215)*

phrase. to sluice up a slope away from the tail-race

This is <i>working away from home. </i> *(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,5(NS)/47)* [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]

### workers' compensation
  
noun. [historical]

### Workers' Compensation Act
  
noun. [historical]  
[NOTES: incl workers' compensation amendment bill]  
> The Labour Bills Committee recommended to the House of Representatives last evening that the Workers' Compensation Amendment Bill should not be allowed to proceed this session. *(Nov 4, 1924,Evening Post,7)*  
> [The questions] are as follows - "(1) If the Premier has seen a copy of a Bill recently passed in the Imperial Parliament intituled "The Workingmen's Compensation for Accidents Act?" *(Oct 25, 1897,Evening Post,00)* [NOTES: check page]  
> The Short Title of this Act is "The Workers' Compensation for Accidents Act, 1900." *(1900,NZ Statutes,64 Vict. 43/227)*  
> We may have a claim under the existing Workers' Compensation Act... The Workers' Compensation Act was largely a tentative measure of which, I believe, Mr. Joseph Chamberlain was either the father, or.. he had a good deal to do with advocating its main provisions. *(1908,NZ Parliamentary Debates,145/1072)*  
> Workers' Compensation for Accidents... An Act to consolidate certain Enactments of the General Assembly related to Compensation to Workers for Accidental Injuries Suffered in the Course of their Employment. *(1908,NZ Consolidated Statutes,207/13)*  
> Workers' Compensation for Accidents... An Act to consolidate certain Enactments of the General Assembly related to Compensation to Workers for Accidental Injuries Suffered in the Course of their Employment. *(1908,NZ Consolidated Statutes,207/13)*  
> This Act may be cited as the Workers' Compensation Act, 1922. *(1922,NZ Statutes,XIII Geo.V. 39/171)*  
> An Act to amend the Workers' Compensation Act, 1922. *(1936,NZ Statutes,I Edw.VIII. 45/442)*  
> It was the intention of the Government to make a complete overhaul of the Workers' Compensation Act this session.<i>Ibid.</i> 1009 The Court held that the man had died at his employment because of heart disease, and that his case did not therefore come within the provisions of the Workers' Compensation Act. *(1936,NZ Parliamentary Debates,247/1001)*  
> This Act may be cited as the Workers' Compensation Act 1956. *(1956,NZ Statutes,I 62/562)*  
> The first Workers' Compensation Act was passed in 1900, the Bill having been largely fashioned on the English legislation. *(1956,NZ Parliamentary Debates,310/2089)*  
> An Act to amend the Workers' Compensation Act 1956. *(1964,NZ Statutes,I 33/172)*  
> It is gazetted that reciprocal application of the Workers' Compensation Act, 1908, has been established with the United Kingdom, Queensland and Western Australia. *(Nov 7, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,6)*

noun. [historical]

### working
  
noun. the extraction of timber from the bush  
[NOTES: cf. workings]  
> **MOORE** The first stage in bringing bush country into use was usually the extraction or `working' of the millable timber. *(1969,From Forest to Farm,37)*  
> 256   RIMU END, TOTARA BUSH WORKINGS. *(May 1, 1907,Progress,256)*

### working bee
  
noun. an unpaid working party, often for social or charitable purposes; [AND 1908] a group of volunteers formed to carry out a specific task  
[NOTES: aka bee]  
> "Working Bees" are still being held everyThursday and Saturday. *(May 31, 1933,NZ Tablet,37)*  
> On Saturday afternoon next there will beanother working bee to finish hoeing theweeds between the potatoes... Every younglady who attended last Saturday's "bee"will be given two photos of the groups. *(Nov 1, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4)*  
> After trying these contemporary apple cakes, I decided to revisit a simpler recipe from the 60s, the sort of cake to serve at a working bee or after a winter walk. *(Jun 15, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43)*  
> **HAY** [Nearly all the men in the district had been asked to come and assist at the mustering ... of the herd. It was a gathering of the kind known in America as a `bee'.] *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/214)*  
> **EVANS** [In 1913] T. Middlemiss gained a tender of 10s. per acre for eight acres to `digger plough' the domain. At a working bee in August [1913] tussocks and other plants were removed. *(1962,Waikaka Saga,172)*  
> **SEDDON** One of our friends comes to us with the news that whitebait are running in the Teremakau River and that the rills or runways we made last week with large stones on the bank of the river may be `jumped' by early comers causes the working bee to break up. *(1968,Seddons, The,20)*  
> **McLAGLAN** In due time we formed a working bee with volunteers from the committee, friends and myself, and all proceeded to `Ilfracombe' where we camped for nearly a week, sweeping, cleaning, and touching up the paintwork. *(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,154)*  
> **KING** [caption] The ohu, or working bee, which cleared and sowed the Panguru block. By 1932, groups like this were bringing Maori land into production all over the North Island. *(1988,Apirana Ngata)*  
> Funds were found for a Temuka scout den by working bees engaged in picking potatoes. *(May 29, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> The Trelissick Park Working Group has been running for 13 years, and working bee groups gather every two weeks to restore the park to a semi-wilderness state by clearing blackberry, removing noxious weeds and planting native trees. *(Jun 22, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),5)*  
> For some time past a number of ladies in Greytown have been holding "bees" for the purpose of making articles for a bazaar. *(Mar 19, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> **HENDERSON** Working bees dug and levelled the corner, put down the lawns and the path. *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,143)*  
> It is intended to call a meeting of the settlers of Pahiatua at an early date for the purpose of forming a "bee" to clean the school ground. *(Jan 30, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> From the president (who was at the head of a working bee nearly all last week) to the ordinary member there was a general effort towards maintaining smartness and regularity. *(Nov 1, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*

### working?/are you working?
  
phrase. said by the user of a party or extension telephone line to discover if the line is already in use.

> Mum rang Aunty Moe on the party line. ... Mum said, 'Working?' first. You have to say that just in case somebody on the party line might be talking. *(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,249)*

### works
  
noun. [ellipsis] a freezing works or slaughterhouse.  
> Only a small percentage of the lambs have gone to the works from their mothers. *(Mar 3, 1916,NZ Farmer,10)*  
> There are large numbers [of sheep] being sent to the works as potters, and others are being killed for their skins. *(Mar 4, 1916,NZ Farmer,19)*  
> Milk lambs received at the works in the early weeks of the season were in fine condition ... *(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,7)*  
> The bobbies are left on the mother for the first four days or until they are ready for the works. *(Nov 11, 1963,Dairy Exporter (NZ),5)*  
> Prime lamb farmers now should have most of their lambs away to the works. *(Jan 28, 1965,NZ Farmer,36)*  
> The tops of the wether lambs go to the works ... *(1969,Big Country of the North Island,209)*  
> Often, though, tailenders left when the fastest gainers have gone to the works are the ones used for breeding. *(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,11)*  
> **LOUGHNAN** works: abbreviation of the term freezing works. It means that only, no other kind of works. *(1981,Glossary,93)*  
> **JARVIS** Freight was lower with stock that was normally sent to the works being sold on the farm as stores. *(1989,Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in NZ,19)* [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in NZ App.]  
> Overfats taken from works lines, and thought to be about 15kg sold up to expectations. *(Oct 22, 1998,NZ Farmer,35)*  
> **D.AVIGDOR** These gentlemen [Messrs Nelson Brothers] have under their own control the many manufactures subsidiary to the slaughter and freezing-houses, and their works [at Napier] have increased enormously since they were first started a few years ago. *(1888,Antipodean Notes,84)*  
> The number of works and hands employed ... were as follow:- *(1892,NZ Official Handbook,140)*  
> In addition, there would be stock which would be fit to slaughter at a local works ... *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/342)*  
> **REES** Fat lambs from the Works. *(1928,Wild, Wild Heart,17)*  
> **STUDHOLME** Later [than the mid-1880s] cattle were killed at the works. *(1940,Te Waimate,123)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** Then you sweat at the ramps of a two-deck truck,/ And the lambs go mad with fright, / ... /And you slake your thirst as they drive away,/ To the Works with their fancy smells,/ And wait with patience the killing day / And the tale that the weight note tells./ *(1985,Farm & Station Verse,185)* [NOTES: 1949 Stronach `Drafting Fat Lambs' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 185]  
> <i>Caseous lymphadenitis</i> is referred to as `C.L.A.' or `lympho' in the works killing sheets. ... There is substantial economic loss ... as *(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,345)*  
> **COLIN GORDON** The freezing works generally called<i> the works</i>. The building is called <i>the shed</i> (W.H. Mabbett comments: `especially among older hands who pride themselves in keeping up the traditional terms'). *(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]  
> **DUFF** I felt like following him to the `works' and driving a bargain with the butcher; ... *(Dec 17, 1952,Shepherd's Calendar,106,1961)*  
> **MASTERS** Strange to say that domestic beast was the only one out of the whole mob to be comdemned by the works inspector, and hence sent down the chute. *(1960,Back-Country Tales,221)* [NOTES: attrib]  
> **SHARPE** Four months hence, when unsatisfactory breeding stock were being culled for fattening and [14] `the works', that slit ear would be ... evidence of her [sc. a ewe's] failure. *(1962,Country Occasions,13)*  
> **TAYLOR** A little stock work, the drafting and picking of lambs for despatch to the works or sale yards, some help if foot-rotting or dipping was in progress. *(1971,Plekhov Place, The,11)*  
> **MORRIESON** I don't like to see the bobby calves ... hauled off to the Works any more then you do. *(1976,Pallet on the Floor,11)*  
> **LELAND** (the) <i>works</i>: This is the meatworks or SLAUGHTERHOUSE for export lamb and beef *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,114)*  
> **GEE** Sent the pair of yer off to the works like a couple of bobby calves. *(1982,Halfmen of O,65,1984)*  
> **WHEELER**  *(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,25)*  
> (Smither) The Works closed at four, which meant at least two hours before Danny could expect them [the parents] - and the `kai' - greasies probably, and his stomach sent out signals. *(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/491)*  
> Messrs. Nelson Brothers, of the meat-freezing works at Tomoana, kindly offered to receive and paddock any black lambs forwarded ... to their works until such time as a drive of black lambs should be collected together. *(1901,TrNZI,33/197)*

### wouldn't it
  
exclamation. expressing exasperation or disgust : since WW2 used alone or in various exclamations of disgust (often with rising tone or stress), pained surprise (often with falling tone), as a shortening or alteration of (e.g.) <i>wouldn't it make you sick</i> (in the longer collocations, IT often replaced by THAT: <i>wouldn't that rock you,</i> etc.); `smart' catchphrase giving an offhand (or slightly contemptuous) reply to an obvious question  
[NOTES: >ca1941-45 D Singe Services]  
> Well, wouldn't it!" said the bloke. *(Mar 20, 1944,NZEF Times,III/143 : 2)*  
> "Wouldn't it rock you," says Snow... "Here's a bloke in a magazine nattering about what the well-dressed man will wear this spring. Wouldn't it!" *(May 22, 1944,NZEF Times,III/152 : 2)*  
> Well, <i>WOULDN'T</i> it. *(1941,NZEF Times,19/6)*  
> ... and your radio gives out. Wouldn't it! *(1950,2ZB)* [NOTES: (Advt for Columbus Radio)]  
> <i>(Army slang phrase).</i> shortened form of `wouldn't it make you sick?'. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>wouldn't it? (Army sl. phr.) </i>shortened form of <i>Wouldn't it make you sick? </i> *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wouldn't it! </i>exclamation of disgust or exasperation; eg `Wouldn't it make you spit tacks!' c. 1925, NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,125)*  
> **McGill** wouldn't it rip your ration book/rock you/root you/rotate you/rot your socks/make you spit?/make you spit chips?' extensions of above exclamation, [[wouldn't it!]] sometimes including being startled or unsettled, especially in `rock' and `rotate'; the latter recorded by Jim Henderson in his Glorious Inglorious, 1945; eg `Well, did you hear about Sid winning the quinella? First time he's punted. Wouldn't it rip your rationbook/rock you/rotate you etc ...' NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,125)*

> **MacKENZIE** <i>wow </i>mental hospital *(1980,While We Have Prisons,99)*
