### wowse
  
verb. to preach temperance or teetotalism  
> He has our full forgiveness for being angry, but he shouldn't wowse about it and talk of `Christian restraints' and tell dirty lies. *(Feb 28, 1909,NZ Truth,192/4/7)* [NOTES: (nb: date originally input intop the database as 29/2/1909)]  
> **THORNTON** No doubt as you gets a good fat screw for wowsin'. *(1916,Wowser, The,170)*  
> So much of the self-denial so many people "wowse" about is pure meanness. *(Jul 12, 1913,NZ Observer,2)*  
> **Whim Wham (Allen Curnow)** The Verb To Wowse (title)... When a Bunch of Wowsers get together They don't carouse. Where Revellers revel and Gamblers gamble, Wowsers just wowse. A Wowser is One who wowses, And wowsing, Sir, Is a delightful pastime, even if it's not the one YOU prefer ... *(1967,Whim Wham Land,157)* [NOTES: poem originally published in The Press, Saturday 13 November 1965, p.14 (editorial page)]  
> The goodies wowse, and parsons preach . . . *(Jan 7, 1926,NZ Truth,7)*  
> He has our full forgiveness for being angry, but he shouldn't wowse about it and talk of "Christian restraints," and tell dirty lies. *(Feb 20, 1909,NZ Truth,4)*

### wowser
  
noun. a prohibitionist; an advocate of temperance or teetotalism in respect of liquor; hence, a kill-joy, spoil-sport; a puritanically-minded person  
[NOTES: cf non-wowser; John Norton, 1862-1916, journalist and politician.  Born Brighton, England, emigrated NSW 1884, 1890 invited to join editorial staff of Truth when it began publication in Sydney. Editor and part- owner 1891, dismissed for drunkenness, after "tortuous litigation". By 1896 had again become editor and sole proprietor. Noted for such things as section on Divorces headed "Prickly Pairs in the Garden." Claimed he invented the term wowser but this is disputed. C.  J. Dennis : A wowser is an ineffably pious person who mistakes this world for a penitentiary and himself for a warder.The first recorded use of the word is in Truth 8 Oct. 1899 but it is said to have been in spoken use for some years before. (also in attrib. or adj. use below)]  
> Tho' many Wowsers stay at home, / And hope to make a name, / The town will ne'er be painted red / Till I come home again. *(1918,Digger Junior, The,7)*  
> **CADEY** Is a wowser a teetotaller then? *(1933,Broken Pattern,115)*  
> **SARGESON** <i>Wowser</i>: non-drinker: Mrs Grundy of either sex. *(1949,I Saw in My Dream,279)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>Wowser</i>. Adopted, ca. 1925, by New Zealand. (R. G. C. McNab in<i> The Press,</i> Christchurch, New Zealand, April 2, 1938.) *(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1226)*  
> **SARGESON** You like a spot Johnny don't you? Don't tell me you're a wowser. And again he thought it over. I like a drop, he said. *(1949,I Saw in My Dream,98,1974)*  
> I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>wowser </i> *(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class)*  
> Regarding the origin of the term `Wowser' discussed in this column, `F.M.' writes:- `During the latter part of the last century a crusade was launched by the Women's Christian Temperance Union against the social evils in Australia. One woman who spoke always had a ready answer to the jeers and jibes of her audience. On one occasion when she and her party were accused of being `kill-joys' she retorted `We only want social evils removed'. The initial letters of those words were taken up by her opponents, and `wowser' became a term of opprobrium which has continued to the present time'. *(Mar 19, 1952,Dominion)*  
> **SINCLAIR** Wowser Opponent of gambling, drinking alcohol, etc; a Puritan *(1959,History of New Zealand,303)* [NOTES: [Colonial-N.Z. glossary]]  
> <i>wowser n.</i> narrow-minded, prudish, puritanical person, a kill-joy, akin to Mrs Grundy: whence <i>wowserism, wowserish, wowseristic </i> [Orig. said to have been invented by John Norton of [<i>Truth</i>, a Sydney newspaper]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> **SLATTER** [They] were out in the alley, leaning on each other ... and vowing eternal friendship in the face of all drongos, no-hopers, wowsers and bush carpenters and all those poufs who had promised to ???to the big game and then backed out ... *(1968,Pagan Game, The,171)*  
> **MITCHELL** <i>wowser:</i> Anti-drink fanatic. Not to be confused with bowser, a man of spirit. *(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,184)*  
> **GEE** That cretin, I thought, that half-man that self-castrated, mother-worshipping, obscusantist, priestly, wowser prick. *(1972,In My Father's Den,146)* [NOTES: moved to adjective]  
> **LELAND** ~ A wowser started off to be a teetotaller, but has expanded in meaning to cover any sort of PURITAN attitudes. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,114)*  
> <i>wowser</i>: person who is teetotal and openly decries the use of alcohol; e.g., `I don't drink myself, but I'm no wowser.' *(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> <i>wowser n. Austral. & N.Z. sl. 1. </i>a fanatically puritanical person. 2. a teetotaller. [C20: < E dialect wow to complain. *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1363)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 890; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 552]]  
> The recent furore over the sign outside Palmerston North's Fitz Bar, urging students to get drunk rather than study, confirms that the most helpful allies of the wowsers and neo-puritans who want the drinking age raised back to 20, are, paradoxically, in the liquor business. *(Nov 1, 2005,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> How the money to run clubs comes in reveals good and bad news for blue-nosed wowsers. *(Sep 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2)*  
> Sir Geoffrey Palmer does not appear to remember his party was trounced at the last election and most New Zealanders rejected its nanny-state policies. Chardonnay socialists and wowsers of his ilk are always ready to tell others what to do. *(May 4, 2009,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> Your "Wine Press" column of 16 January suggests that the Alcohol Advisory Council (ALAC) is a wowser for suggesting advisory labels on alcohol containers warning of the risks of fetal alcohol spectrum disorder. *(Feb 5, 2008,Capital Times,4)*  
> Mr. George Hutcheson, the ex-politician, defined a wowser as "a man who had corns on his chest from leaning up against pub counters begging for beer." *(Feb 15, 1913,NZ Observer,24)*  
> When I was growing up in Dunedin, my father had the dismissive word "wowser" for anyone even vaguely puritanical, and the introduction here suggests that the reader is in for a vintage wowser yarn. *(Apr 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63)*  
> Contrast this with the constant current under-use of Eden Park, which, in between World Cup finals, is about as full as a wowser on Temperance day. *(Jul 5, 2006,Capital Times,6)*  
> A wowser is a crepuscular-minded person of religious proclivities, having one eye on Paradise and the other on the main chance. He generally - but not always-belongs to one of the Non-conformist churches. His pursuits are the holding of conventions and bun-rushes, at the latter of which he consumes marvellous quantities of indigestible tea and buns. When he drinks whisky he does it in secret [From a letter written by an 84-year-old woman to a Wanganui newspaper and naturally quoted with glee by `Truth'] *(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,4)*  
> If history has one lesson, it is to beware the wowsers, of whom Hitler was the most extreme, if not the most recent. *(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44)*  
> He's no wowser. "If you can afford it [gambling] and it's fun, fair enough. But there are some who obviously have a problem. I once saw a chap who was chasing the jackpot with $1400 in his machine - to get a $1000 jackpot!" *(Jan 2008,North & South,73)*  
> **Barrington** At soap-box meetings I developed a formula to get quickly through the irrelevant. I'd say 'No, I'm a wowser, I don't smoke, drink, swear, gamble or wear a hat; all my vices are big ones.' *(1970,Trials of a Pacifist,16)*  
> When we are having a harmless spree - / The wowser who tries to make us shut up! *(May 22, 1944,NZEF Times,III/152 : 7)*  
> "Wowser" is a word unique to New Zealand and Australia. It is a word rapidly passing into extinction, because the type of person it describes - someone who is implacably opposed to alcohol and, by implication, to hedonistic indulgence of any kind - is also dying out. As the once-powerful temperance movement fades into history and a new generation of New Zealanders grows up accustomed to the civilised pleasures of a deregulated liquor industry, "wowser" is a word that seems to belong in the 50s. *(Aug 4, 1998,Evening Post,4)*  
> **IRELAND** Supplies [of liquor] were acquired and passed along quietly and discreetly, so as not to attract attention from the neighbouring wowsers who pried on their districts vigilantly and venomously from behind the camouflage of their lace curtains. *(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,82)*  
> Well, call me a wowser but I don't want my bright, curious nieces sucked into premature sex and alcohol experimentation - or stupid fad diets for that matter. *(Nov 2005,North & South,8)*  
> ... `lucky bags' ... in addition to the sweets they contained they had a gambling element. Some of them ... contained a three-penny bit ... soon clamped down on by the `wowser' of the day. *(Sep 17, 1977,Dominion,7)* [NOTES: 1903 Jan 15 in 1977 Sep 17 Dominion 7]  
> Weary Wesleyan Wowsers. *(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,1)*  
> To call a man a wowser is to indicate him as being a hypocritical, shuffling, double-lived person, who preys when not praying; who is eternally canting and ranting, while remaining inwardly a whited sepulchre. *(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,249/4/4)*  
> [Long discussion of meaning, and claim to invention by John Norton, <i>Aust Truth:</i> the article republished from Sydney `Truth' of 1910 May 15 5/6: See folder <i>Long quotations</i> (Wilkes 1899 Truth)] What the word wowser precisely means and the type of person it particularly designates is well known, best known to the Wowser h I [John Norton] invented the word myself. I was the first man to publicly use the word. I first gave it utterance in the City Council [Sydney?], when I applied it to Alderman Waterhouse, whom I referred to - I hope and believe with perfect good humor, and without gall or guile-as `the white, woolly, weary, wayery, word-wasting wowser from Waverley.' I am proud of my invention. The fabrication of such a word - absolutely absent from, but as absolutely required in our local vernacular until I invented and introduced it - was a stroke of genius, done on the spur of the moment, impromptu, the result of a divine or diabolical inspiration. Therefore, `Palmam qui meruit ferat' - the motto of Lord Nelson. Let it be the motto of John Norton in the circumstances of this case and for the purposes of establishing his claim to immortal glory as the inventor of the word Wowser. [Photocopy of whole article in files] *(May 21, 1910,NZ Truth,256/1/4-5)*  
> Of the order PHARISEAE. Vegetative body, spheroidal or ovoid, isolated or connected in filamentous or other growth series. <i>Family</i> WOWSEREAE. <i>Sub-family</i> - TEETOTALACEAE. *(Feb 10, 1912,Triad, The,46)*  
> ... new Wowser parson ... *(Jul 10, 1912,Triad, The,20(4)/22)*  
>  *(Jun 14, 1913,NZ Bulletin,6)* [NOTES: [get cit]]  
> **THORNTON** Wowser is a term somewhat indefinite in its application and meaning. The following definitions given to the author by bushmen themselves may help the reader to understand what a Colonial means when he uses the word. It may mean:- 1) `A man who tries to rob a bloke of his beer and pleasure' - therefore especially applicable to the temperance reformer.2) `A bloke who wears out the knees of his pants on Sundays praying, and another part of his pants all the week backsliding' - i.e. a hypocrite. 3) `Any sky-pilot, priest, or local preacher who makes a bloke uncomfortable, and pretends to be religious.' *(1916,Wowser, The,8)*  
> **GRANT** Two characters captioned `The Wowser' and `Jimmy Woodser' hold jointly a song-book with cover respectively showing `Six O'C Closing' and `No-shouting'. *(1980,Unauthorised Version,105)* [NOTES: 1916 Glover cartoon in Truth `The Cranks' Chorus' in 1980 Grant Unauthorised Version 105]  
> The Wowsers *(1917,Flare, The)*  
> **THORNTON** I am fed up with wowsers (slang for ministers or Christians) who say one thing ... *(1917,With the Anzacs in Cairo,148)*  
> **FOSTON** <i>Wowser</i>: The word, it is understood, was derived from a Chinese term, meaning a man of probity and character; but he had seen it stated that the word was a corruption of `rouser'. However, neither definition worried him, because it was the sneer now implied that he proposed to deal with. According to opponents, the `wowser' was the most objectionable person in the world. *(1921,At The Front,123)*  
> **MENCKEN** We have a Federal padlock on the wowsers in Boston, and shall proceed against then for damages in due order. At their behest, the Postoffice banned the April issue ex post facto. *(Apr 20, 1926,Letter)* [NOTES: 1926 H.L.Mencken to JM Patterson Letter 20 April  (1977 (Bode The Mencken Letters 197)]  
> <i>wowser n.</i> a kill-joy, a gloomy, puritanical person. [origin uncertain] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **SCANLAN** `I'm no wowser ... I like me glass of beer and five bob on a horse.' *(1933,Tides of Youth,124)*  
> **HYDE** `What's it like over in New Zealand? I heard New Zealand was a good little country, spoilt by too many wowsers.' *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,216)*  
> **GORMACK** Then the first `wowser' came to the microphone, a Dr O'Brien, ... A medical man, supposedly a scientist, and out he comes with the most astonishing collection of puritanical, Victorian utterances that I have ever heard; ... *(1975,Diary of A Hundred Days,2/52)* [NOTES: 1942 Sep 2 in 1975 Gormack Diary of A Hundred Days 2/52]  
> **BENNETT** Whether <i>wowser</i> (a prohibitionist, an excessively religious person, a kill-joy) originated in Australia or New Zealand is uncertain, and so is its etymology. [fn 13] It gained some popularity in New Zealand during World War I. The adjective <i>wowseristic</i> has been formed from it. [fn] See Mencken, <i>The American Language</i> (4th ed., 1936), p. 265. The word was in use in New Zealand as early as 1895. [??is evidence in Mencken] *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89)*  
> <i>Wowser </i>- spoil-sport, temperance advocate, puritanical person. *(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39)*  
> **SOLJAK** Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>wowser</i>: spoil-sport, bluenose *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115)*

adjective.

noun.

> [advt] KAISERISM WAS DEFEATED IN EUROPE. Help to Defeat Wowserism in New Zealand. ... I Vote for National Continuance. *(Apr 1919,Quick March,7)*

> **HICKEY** While retaining a wholesome contempt for what is commonly known as `Wowserism', nevertheless these men were most temperate. *(1925,Red Fed. Memoirs,31)*  
> It isn't only wowserism that keeps women out of bars: ... *(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/215)* [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

> In a nation long bedevilled by wowserism, he was reluctant to change the law concerning the drinking age. *(1995,All Honourable Men,142)*  
> But, astonishingly, there's a deep and ugly streak of wowserism here in young New Zealand. *(Dec 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30)*

[fn 13] It gained some popularity in New Zealand during World War I. The adjective <i>wowseristic</i> has been formed from it. [fn] See Mencken, <i>The American Language</i> (4th ed., 1936), p. 265. ### wrapping

### wrasse
  
noun. in New Zealand often called parrot fish any of various elongated, compressed, usually brilliantly coloured, spiny-finned fishes, including (in warm seas) some important food fishes, as well as some believed poisonous.  
> **HECTOR** A small wrasse, ... called Spotty or Poddly *(1872,Fishes NZ,108)*  
> **HECTOR** 68.LABRICHTHYS BOTHRYOCOSMUS. Rich. Wrasse. *(1872,Fishes,43)*  
> **SENIOR** Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... aua, rock cod, wrasse, and patiki; ... *(1880,Travel & Trout,288)*  
> **THOMSON** 27. <i>Bligh Sound</i> - John-dory, rock-cod, wrasse, butter-fish. <i>Ibid.</i> 211Wrasse, or Spotty (<i>Labrichtys bothryocosmus</i>). NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 36. LOCALITIES - Mokohinou, the Brothers, Bligh Sound, Milford Sound. *(1892,TrNZI,24/204)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wrasse, n.</i> This English name for many fishes is given, in New Zealand, to <i>Labrichthys bothryocosmus</i>, Richards. Called also <i>Poddly, Spotty</i>, and <i>Kelp-fish. </i> *(1898,Austral English,519)*  
> [Native or Settlers' Name] Wrasse, or Perch .. [Scientific Name] <i>Labrichthys celidota </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 11/384 The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. *(1879,TrNZI,11/382)* [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]  
> **AYLING** Most members of this family [Labridae: wrasses] have been known in New Zealand as parrotfish although in all other countries they are collectively called wrasses and the name parrotfish is applied to a completely different family. A true parrotfish (family Scaridae) has recently been found in New Zealand ... *(1982,Collins Guide,251,1984)*  
> Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. *(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167)*  
> **SORENSON** COD, ROCK <i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. 4. <i>Pictilabrus</i> [?separate gen, from Pseud.??] *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18)*  
> A Wanganui man advances the theory that deep-sea fishermen would get much better results if they used as bait the apparently favourite food, the wrasse. *(Feb 7, 1939,Evening Post,15)*

### wrasse
  
noun. (in New Zealand often called parrot fish) any of various elongated, compressed, usually brilliantly coloured, spiny-finned fishes, including (in warm seas) some important food fishes, as well as some believed poisonous.  
See: spotty, soldier, kelp-fish, pigfish, poddly

### wraxing
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** <i>wraxing</i> swinging backwards and forwards, as on the legs of a chair, which Stevan Eldred-Grigg exemplifies thus: `Stop that bloody wraxing on that bloody chair.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,126)*

### wreck-fish
  
noun.  
> **DIEFFENBACH** 2.<i>Polyprion cernuum</i>. - Wreck-fish, Cherney, or Jew-fish. *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/206)*  
> <i> Polyprion americanus</i> Bloch and Schneider. This deep-water representative of the genus is now identified by Waite ... with the wreck-fish of the Atlantic. It is occasionally taken outside Otago Heads, and is known by the local fishermen as `bass groper' or `black bass'. *(1913,TrNZI,45/228)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]  
> <i> Polyprion americanus</i> Bloch and Schneider. This deep-water representative of the genus is now identified by Waite ... with the wreck-fish of the Atlantic. It is occasionally taken outside Otago Heads, and is known by the local fishermen as `bass groper' or `black bass'. *(1913,TrNZI,45/228)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]  
> wreckfish 1288-90 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### wren
  
noun. <i>Xenicus</i> sp. (occas. popularly and mistakenly <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i> rifleman; and <i>Gerygone igata</i> grey warbler, see 1923 cit.); a tiny bird with an erect tail, also called matuhituhi, riroriro  
See: also bush wren  
[NOTES: (Cf riroriro 1859)]  
> **TAYLOR** As we came through the woods one of my boys whistled to a little bird the miromiro or wren the least bird of this country and it actually flew into his clasped hands ... it is less then the English wren but much resembles it ... *(Dec 6, 1846,Journal,IV/148)*  
> **ANGAS** Among the smaller varieties [of birds], I observed the white-headed manakin, a black and yellow fly-catcher, and an extremely diminutive wren. *(1847,Savage Life,2/106)*  
> **BUTLER** The wren and the tomtit and the thrush all are represented quite as nearly or even more so than the robin save that the tomtit is black headed with a yellow breast. *(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44)*  
> **BARRINGTON** Killed a robin and three wrens; roasted them: the smallest joints I ever saw; .. *(Apr 13, 1864,Diary)* [NOTES: 1864 Apr 13 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 406]  
> ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS, Sparrm. Pi wau wau. Wren. *(1870,TrNZI,2/57)* [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]  
> 17. <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, Sparrm. Wren, Titipounamu, Pi-wau-wau. - Often builds in a small hole in a tree. I know one old konini which is resorted to yearly ... *(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,282)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wren</i> ... In New Zealand, the name is applied to the Bush-Wren, <i>Xenicus longipes,</i> Gmel., and the Rock (or Mountain) Wren,<i> X. gilviventris</i>, von Pelz. *(1898,Austral English,519)*  
> <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i> Sparr. Rifleman. Owing to the uncertainty regarding the species denoted by the term `wren' as used by the average observer, many records which may apply to this species have been left strictly alone. *(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/87)* [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]  
> There is still some difference of opinion in regard to the scientific positions of New Zealand's wrens. *(Jan 30, 1909,Evening Post,19)*  
> Several active small birds, not found elsewhere than in New Zealand, are popularly called "wrens," although not related to the English bird of that name. *(Mar 20, 1926,Evening Post,13)*  
> "All day long the wrens had been darting here and there, flying low over the ground, collecting horsehair, straw, grass, and moss ..." *(Mar 15, 1941,Evening Post,17)*

### wriggle gate
  
noun.  
> A sign on the corner of the shelter points to the left, announcing the bushwalk... But if you'd prefer the bushwalk and a look at the Manawatu River, go straight ahead and through the "wriggle gate" (the sort designed to keep out cyclists) and turn right down the hill... The perimeter track zigzags through another pair of wriggle gates before going straight for a further 150 yards... *(Jan 7, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*

### wring someone's leg
  
phrase. ? = pull someone's (here a country's collective) leg  
> Jim Wall, who wrung the Dominion's leg with enthusiasm about the soil and climate of Darling Downs, is one of the few young New Zealanders who imbibed Tory principles with his imported infants' food, and the result is to be found in his base ingratitude to the country of God which has turned him out fat in person and in pocket. *(Jul 11, 1908,NZ Truth,160/1/7)*

### wrinkle
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (shearers') ?omit as normal extension of OED]  
> **BOWEN** Fine-wool sheep, especially Merinos, are much slower than crossbred sheep. Wrinkles, dense wool, trimmings, all have a bearing on shearing. *(1955,Wool Away!,3)*

### wrinkle
  
noun. arse; ring  
[NOTES: ca1938 Havelock I'll kick your wrinkle for you]

### wrinkle stretcher
  
noun. an imaginary piece of equipment called for when a sheep is heavily wrinkled.  
> <i>wrinkle stretcher:</i> An imaginary piece of equipment called for when a sheep is heavily wrinkled. *(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/29)* [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### wrinkly
  
noun. a Merino or other fine-woolled sheep.  
> NZ Shearer 3 2 Spring/Summer 12 Now that he's proved himself on the wrinklies, it's likely he'll look harder at the possibilities of emulating Sam Te Whata's feats on the crossbreds. *(1987)*

### wrong white crowd
  
phrase.  
> Niu Tireni (Long White Shroud) /  (a bare-arsed land, manipulated by the wrong white crowd of minority speculators - with big foreign investors - busily weaving a long white shroud to fit us all). *(1997,Shape-Shifter,88)*

### WRONZ
  
abbreviation. Wool Research Organisation of New Zealand.  
> Last month, the Wool Research Organisation of New Zealand (WRONZ) spun off all its research into a new science company, Canesis Network Ltd, which has taken on its operations, intellectual property, and equipment. I has also taken on the rump of the old Wool Board, Wool Interiors Ltd, which owns the Fernmark brand. Wronz - an incorporated society - continues to own its buildings and property, and a 66 per cent share of Canesis... *(Aug 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C7)*  
> J of Ag Feb 61 The Wool Research Organisation of New Zealand came in for some praise recently..Mr Malcolm Townsend spoke very highly of the WRONZ comprehensive scouring system. *(1978)*  
> NZ Farmer Sept 12 30 WRONZ isn't likely to investigate fibre fineness measurement yet. *(1985)*  
> SQUIRE A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms 44 Woolspill - a WRONZ developed product by which floating booms filled with wool soft knops, trapping up to forty times their own weight, can mop up oils spills. ibid. 44 W.R.O.N.Z - Wool Research Organisation of New Zealand (inc) .. Funded by NZWB., NZ. Govt and Industry. *(1991)*  
> NZ Farmer June 8. 8 WRONZ has already started, with Producer Fibre Technologies, such as dust free wool, wool knops and Treoted superfine spinning ... *(1994)*  
> NZ Farmer Aug 1. 9 WRONZ is looking at ways to drop the cost of getting wool to processors. *(1996)*  
> Daily News Oct 28. 16 ..that the farmer owners of WRONZ and Wools of New Zealand be allocated shares in the company and those shares are to be listed on the New Zealand secondary market. *(1999)*

### wrought
  
noun.  
See: rort

### wrought
  
verb. (as participle) worked (of claims)  
[NOTES: Pyke uses this several times]  
> **PYKE** As summer advanced, floods in the Molyneux became more frequent, ... so that it was with much difficulty that the beach claims could be wrought. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/i/74,1889,1974)*

### wrybill
  
noun. Elliptical for wrybill(ed) plover; also called <i>ngutu parore</i> a bird which lives on some shingle riverbeds and mudflats and is the only species with a bill bent to one side  
> <i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i> Quoy & Gaim. Wrybill. Mr. G. Shepherd has seen eight specimens in two adjacent North Island localities during the years 1910-17, all singly. No records are forthcoming from the South Island, probably on account of lack of coastal observers. *(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/76)* [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH** We also renewed a very brief North Island acquaintance with the Wrybill (<i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i>). *(1936,NZ Naturalist,105)*  
> **SOLJAK** Other waders are the wrybill, the only bird in the world with a twisted beak, ... *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),10)*  
> <i>wrybill or wry-billed plover n </i>: a peculiar shorebird (<i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i>) of New Zealand that is related to the plovers and unique in having its bill sharply deflected to the right paintwork. *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2642)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Wrybill.. .. ngutu-parore .. .. <i>Anarhynchus frontalis </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WRYBILL <i>Anarhynchus frontalis Other Names:</i> Wry-billed Plover, Crook-bill Plover, Ngutu-parore fattened by the maestro of the poultry business. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,130)*  
> <i>wry bill, wry-billed plover n.</i> rare New Zealand bird with bill turned to the right. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> <i>wry bill, wry-billed plover n<.i>. rare N.Z. bird with bill turned to the right. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> <i>156. Anarhynchus frontalis</i> Quoy and Gaimard, 1830 <i>Wrybill (Ngutu parore) </i> *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,46,1980)*  
> <i>wrybill n</i>. a New Zealand plover, having its bill deflected to one side enabling it to search for food beneath stones. *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1366)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 892; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 553]]  
> Wrybill <i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i> QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830 ... No bird in the world has a beak like the wrybill. The last third of its long bill turns to the bird's right at an angle of 15 to 22 degrees. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Ngutu-parore, wrybilled plover. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,188)*  
> Throughout the whole class of birds, nature has produced a remarkable variety of bills - long, short, thick, slender, straight or curved upward or downward, - but only one, that of the wrybill, is bent to one side. *(Aug 18, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F4)*  
> She sat on the beach and watched wrybills and oystercatchers dipping and bobbing out on the flat. *(Jan 9, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> Wrybill is the tamest of shore birds. *(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(3) : 15)*

### wrybill(ed)  plover
  
noun.  
See: also wrybill  
> **BULLER** ANARHYNCUS FRONTALIS. (WRY-BILLED PLOVER.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Ngutupare. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,216,1888 2nd edn 2/9)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wry-billed Plover, n</i>. a very rare bird of New Zealand, <i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i>, Quoy and Gaim. *(1898,Austral English,519)*  
> **PARKER** `The curious wry-billed plover ... the only bird known in which the bill is turned not up or down, but to one side - the right.' *(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,116)*  
> **WINKS** Another bird of interest is the Wry-bill Plover, the only bird in the world which has its beak to one side; a sharp curvature to the right gives the plover a chance to forage under flat stones. *(1954,These New Zealanders,163)*  
> <i>wrybill</i> or <i>wry-billed plover n</i> : a peculiar shorebird (<i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i>) of New Zealand that is related to the plovers and unique in having its bill sharply deflected to the right *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2642)*  
> <i>wry bill, wry-billed plover n</i>. rare New Zealand bird with bill turned to the right. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),629)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WRYBILL <i>Anarhynchus frontalis Other Names</i>: Wry-billed Plover, Crook-bill Plover, Ngutu-parore *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,130)*  
> **NATUSCH** The wrybilled plover (<i>Anarhynchus frontalis</i>) winters in the North Island and breeds along the great shingle flats of the Canterbury rivers. It has a curiously skewed beak, and is in fact the only bird in the world known to have this odd feature. *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,262)*  
> <i>wry bill, wry-billed plover n.</i> rare N.Z. bird with bill turned to the right. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> **LOCKLEY** The fourth [endemic wader] is a charming little wader about the size of a starling, grey and white, with a very unusual bill - twenty-five millimetres long and always<i> twisted to the right</i> - hence its name, wry-billed plover. *(1980,House Above the Sea,181)*  
> <i>Wrybill Anarhynchus frontalis </i>QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830 ... No bird in the world has a beak like the wrybill. The last third of its long bill turns to the bird's right at an angle of 15 to 22 degrees. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Ngutu-parore, wrybilled plover. *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,188)*  
> The least conspicuous of our river-bed nesters is the wry-billed plover. *(Nov 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60)*

### WSCC
  
abbreviation. Wellington Safer Community Council  
> Ray Good.. ends up by decrying Northern ward councillors over the Wellington Safer Community Council (WSCC), Churton Park, and Uncle Tom Cobley and all...Lets [<i>sic</i>] look at the WSCC debacle. *(Jun 12, 2001,Independent Herald (Wellington),4)*

verb.

noun.

### Wussleeyan
  
noun.

### wutter
  
noun.  
See: whata

### WWOOF
  
abbreviation. World Wide Opportunities on Organic Farms, also known as Willing Workers on Organic Farms  
> WWOOF, Willing Workers on Organic Farms, is an international organisation that sets out to match-make between organic farms and gardens and people looking for temporary work in return for accomodation and food... Created in Britain in 1971, WWOOF was established in New Zealand three years later. *(Jul 6, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,9)*

### WWOOF
  
verb. work with WWOOF  
See: WWOOF (abbreviation)

### WX
  
noun. a marketing grade of prime lamb.  
> J of Ag Sept 16 Nearly three-quarters of the Early's total ram kill (74.4 per cent) fell into the heavy WX grade this year. I really set out to push the system, to see how many WXs we could get in this very good season. *(1986)*  
> ibid. 20 .. Probably more than 50 per cent of the farmers growing WXs have practised this reconditioning at some stage. *(1986)*  
> J of Ag Feb/March 24 To ensure he didn't have his priorities wrong, Bradfield last year tried the WX option with the lambs born in the spring of 1986. *(1988)*  
> Jarvis Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in Otago App. 27 Most lambs averaged $20.85, with special WX grades bringing $28.00. *(1989)*  
> NZ Farmer Jan 11. 12 With the Poll Dorsets, the out of season potential for WXs was good as well. *(1989)*  
> NZ Farmer May 4. 4 Back around 1987-88 we were carrying lambs through to WX. *(1993)*

### wyata
  
noun.  
See: waiata  
> Nor did she contribute to the roles of the second row, such as supporting the korero, lead waiata, lead or support karanga. *(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6)*

### wych elm
  
noun.  
> <i>wych elm [s] Ulmus glabra</i> Huds. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,88)*

### Wydrass
  
noun. Wairarapa (see Wydrop)  
> **WAKEFIELD** Wainerap *(1839,Diary,61)*  
> We have a fine district at Wydrass. <i>Ibid.</i>. 20  (Alex. Perry) It is called Wydrass, or Warerapa. ' *(1843,Letters from Settlers,6)*

### Wydrop
  
noun. the commonest forms of the many variants in pronunciation and spelling of early Anglicisations of Wairarapa, mainly with Maori /r/ perceived as English [d], an unstressed /a/ as [ ], and the -a final not heard  
[NOTES: The NZ Journal 1841-1845 record: Wydirup (once), Wyderop (8 times) Wairarapa (twice)]  
> [marginal note] Wyderop is a corruptin - the real name being Wye re Roppa. *(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,271,1966)*  
> **McLEAN** (Domett) ... a Wairau or Wydrop runholder. *(1858,Papers,15/83)*  
> **HEYWOOD** The tract up the Valley leads into the Wairarapa (called Waidrup) Plains. *(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,158)*  
> Witness asked me where Mr. Thompson was, she replied, `up in the Waidrup home'. *(Jul 11, 1871,Evening Post,2)*  
> **PAYTON** Wairarapa (known among the old Colonials as the `Waidrup') ... *(1885,Round about NZ,239)*  
> In the early days of the colony, the Wairarapa Valley was called `Wy-drop' by the Wellington settlers. Anyone calling it by the proper name would have been laughed at as a prig. *(1893,TrNZI,25/403)* [NOTES: (Smith `Maori Nomenclature')]  
> Mr Wardell was next transferred to.. the Wairarapa, or the "Wydrop" as the first settlers used to pronounce the name... As a result of his efforts.. no "gun-play" took place in the "Wydrop" valley. *(May 11, 1912,NZ Truth,4)*  
> **Bradey** he has taken a lease of Wideropu. *(Feb 14, 1844,Papers)* [NOTES: 1844 Feb 14 Bradey papers (MS:ATB)]

### wyena
  
noun.

abbreviation. [odds and sods] the shorn wool
